Maersk · MAERSK.B-DK · the new Memo conveyor's card of 11 November 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote behind each of the eleven ratings: Maersk

The Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions and its 13 group judgements, 45,262 words, set out under the eleven ratings: each rating, then each of its lettered sub-items, then the answers beneath.

Word for word. Every answer is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written; a script checked that every word is on this page, in order. What is changed is layout only: answers are grouped by rating rather than by layer; each answer is titled with the question as it was set (the Investment Analyst's own heading appears beneath only where it adds words); an answer that belongs under more than one sub-item is shown in full once, at its first place, and as a one-line link everywhere else.

Which answer sits where comes from the same table the ratings map uses. Each answer carries its layer and group, because "positive" means something different in each layer. Each layer's own material (what it was asked, what its scores mean, the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices) is at the end, so nothing is left out.

The ratings alone: the ratings map.

How to read the scores and letters

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning (listed just below).

Where the Rater has scored this stock (every memo from 3 November 2026), the number in each pill is the Rater's, set after the memo was written from the Investment Analyst's judgements against a key per group of questions; the Investment Analyst's own score is kept in the pill's tooltip. The letter beside each score is read from the one canonical source for ratings, the same file the eleven ratings page and the memo scorecards read; nothing is graded on this page. The letter follows the item's rule: for most items this stock's rank among the stocks scored on the same scale on that same question or group judgement, re-cut each night (Richard's ruling of 4-Nov-26: every letter by rank; in the file's words: A above 90%; B+ above 82.5% to 90%; B above 75% to 82.5%; C+ above 66.67% to 75%; C above 58.33% to 66.67%; C- above 50% to 58.33%; D above 25% to 50%; F up to 25%); on the External change forces and Internal change forces items the number carries a sign (50 = neither positive nor negative) and the letter is the rank, so read the two together. “37th of 56” is the stock's place among them; a dashed outline marks a provisional grade (fewer than 30 stocks scored).

What the scores mean in each layer

Layer 1

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 2

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 3 and Layer 4

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

Inputs

1

External headwinds/tailwinds

F1253rd of 53

Memo topic 1, Tailwinds in the operating environment: the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

External change forces

Layer 1 · External change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

The external environment is a strong, fading, costly tailwind giving way to a strong, visible, structural headwind; the balance is already negative on any horizon beyond the current quarter. The 2026 earnings peak was made by capacity absorption, not demand, and absorption reverses mechanically as Suez reopens (about 7% of effective capacity) while a contracted delivery wave (fleet +7.4% in 2027 and +13.2% in 2028 on Bernstein's count) arrives with scrapping near zero and an industry rich enough to fight. Demand, macro and regulation all lean mildly negative and none can offset the supply arithmetic. The Consumer should build the 2027-2028 Ocean rate path from the supply-demand balance and the marginal carrier's cash cost, treat demand as a second-order input, and carry a separately weighted disruption-extended scenario (about 20%) rather than blending it into the base case, because it shifts timing by two to four quarters but does not cancel the oversupply.

1a

External headwinds/threats

6 answers
C-0210C-0528C-0730C-0938C-3555C-3928
C-02

To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

F1031st of 31

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: competitive intensity is rising sharply and contractually; over the last six months it was hidden by capacity absorption, and over the next six months it becomes visible as that absorption unwinds.

  • Supply arriving is contracted. The orderbook is 39-44% of the active fleet (record 13-14.9m TEU; Morgan Stanley and DNB Carnegie 44%, Deutsche Bank 42.5%). Fleet growth: Bernstein 4.6% (2026), 7.4% (2027), 13.2% (2028); Deutsche Bank supply 9.5% in 2027 against demand 4.1%; DNB Carnegie about 10% and 16%. Scrapping is near nil (5k TEU in 2025, about 41k TEU estimated for 2026); idle capacity 2.2-2.4%.
  • The money to fight exists. Industry net cash of USD 26-30bn lets carriers sustain losses ("game of chicken"); the orderbook added about 1.6m TEU in Q3 2026 alone, and orders grew 12% in Q1 2026 despite tariff threats.
  • The price-setters are volume-first. MSC (21.5% share, 7.3m TEU, 2.9m TEU on order, standalone direct-call network), CMA CGM (12.7%, fleet +34% gross through 2028, expected to overtake Maersk in 2027), COSCO (state-owned, +4.5% Q3 volume, 19.96% nine-month EBIT margin).
  • Maersk joined the arms race. Its order of 26 dual-fuel 18,600 TEU ships (483,600 TEU, 2029-2030) breaches its own 4.1-4.3m TEU fleet cap and is framed as defending a 14% share. Even the most return-focused large carrier is now ordering to protect volume.
  • First signs of the shift are already visible: transpacific discounting in late October; Maersk itself used aggressive spot pricing in early 2026 to fill Gemini; Q3 volume share lost to carriers with spare capacity.

For the model: competitive intensity is the mechanism that converts the supply wave into rates. Assume the 2027-2028 rate path is set by the marginal carrier's cash cost, not by Maersk's cost or service.

C-05

How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

F2831st of 31

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: a headwind, and an asymmetric one: costs fixed at peak-cycle prices (charters, leases, newbuilds, fuel offtakes) stay while revenue falls, and fuel recovery weakens as the market loosens.

  • Bunker. Average price USD 777 a tonne in Q2 2026 (+44%) and USD 688 in Q3 (+25%); Q3 bunker cost USD 2.1bn (+37%) despite consumption -5.3%. Brent USD 96-111. Maersk has no bunker hedges (Hapag-Lloyd can hedge up to 80% but currently has none either). Formulaic bunker surcharges cover about 44% of contracted volume; recovery about 80-90% in a tight market and about 70% in a loose one (memo). If peace lowers oil, the fuel tailwind arrives with the capacity release that cuts rates, so it is not an offset of the same size.
  • Charters and leases. 416 of 754 vessels are chartered (Q3 2026); charter availability is very low and durations have lengthened to five to seven years; Maersk extended three 9,500 TEU charters at USD 41,000 a day to 2031; about USD 9.3bn of leases are committed but not yet started (memo). The 2016 and 2023 playbook of handing back tonnage is therefore smaller than before.
  • Ships. Yards are full to 2029; China won 65-72% of orders; newbuild prices are about 46% above pre-COVID. Maersk's 26-ship order was placed at these prices; capital commitments rose to USD 19.5bn at Q3 2026 from 13.4bn at end-2025 (+46%).
  • Green fuels. Two to three times conventional fuel cost; USD 5.9bn of euro-indexed offtake commitments over five to ten years.
  • Congestion and terminal handling. Terminal handling is 39% of Ocean cost; congestion raised unit cost ex-fuel 5% in Q3.
  • Offsets. Gemini's network savings (USD 950m), slow steaming (more than USD 300m saved in past downcycles), Seaspan retrofits (slot cost -10-13% on four ships), and congestion relief when queues clear.
C-07

To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

F3030th of 31

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: macro forces are moderately negative and bite hardest at the industry level, because a small demand shortfall on an industry already 5-10 points oversupplied deepens the rate fall through fixed-cost leverage; at the company level macro is second-order (currency, interest); at the market level the risk-off regime raises the vulnerability of crowded cyclical winners.

  • Growth. IMF 3.1% global growth for 2026, 2.0% in a severe escalation case. US headline inflation 3.7%, a restrictive Fed into 2027; Europe's energy shock and real-wage squeeze; uneven China.
  • Industry level. Container demand at about 1.1x GDP; each point of lost demand in 2027 adds to an oversupply of supply growth 7-13% against demand 2-4%. Because Ocean cost is fixed per sailing, rates rather than volumes absorb the gap.
  • Sector level (logistics). Goods inventories are full, so forwarders' volumes are already falling (DSV -3%); asset-light forwarders protect margins through self-help; asset owners cannot.
  • Company level. USD reporting shields translation; a 10% USD move is about 0.3bn of EBIT; hedging covers about 50% of 12-month net flows. Net cash of about USD 15bn means higher rates raise interest income. Oil is a cost (recovered with a lag) and also a supply lever (slow steaming persists above about USD 100 Brent, re-absorbing capacity).
  • Market level. The Fitness for Fighting digest reads risk-off beneath an index at its high, with defensives leading on all five windows and Momentum tilted Underweight; the shipping cohort is +82% over twelve months at the dearest points of its ranges. A crowded, high-momentum cyclical at a peak multiple is exposed to de-rating regardless of company news.
C-09

What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

–38

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: on balance mildly negative; the material dynamics are forwarder consolidation (more buyer power against carriers), the deepening of the Gemini partnership (positive for Terminals), and non-return-driven ownership of the largest competitors.

  • Negative: DSV's acquisition of DB Schenker (about 7% global forwarding share) concentrates freight buying; the top three forwarders compete in every tender and forwarders are up to 50% of ocean volume (C-33). MSC (private), CMA CGM (family) and COSCO (state) are not run for listed-equity returns. The shipping cohort sits at the dearest points of its ranges, so sector de-rating is a risk in itself. Climate constraints (Panama slots cut from 36 to 32 a day, auction fees above USD 1.1m) absorb capacity temporarily and will fade.
  • Positive: Hapag-Lloyd bought 25% of APM Terminals Maasvlakte II (completed November 2026), which validates the value of Maersk's terminals, ties the Gemini partner to Maersk hubs and doubles the terminal to 5.4m TEU. Maersk's entry to the OMX Nordic 40 on 1 December 2026 brings passive buying (small). US port fees on Chinese vessels could marginally help non-Chinese carriers.
C-35

To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

C-5512th of 31

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the threat from new entrants is low in Ocean and moderate in Logistics; the meaningful "entrant" is financial capital supplying ships through leasing houses, which adds to supply rather than competing for customers.

  • Adjacent entrants: Amazon's Supply Chain Services for non-Amazon merchants (mainly domestic and air; low threat to Ocean; some threat to Maersk's e-commerce and warehousing in Solutions); forwarders do not own ships.
  • Standard entrants: small regional carriers on intra-Asia lanes (Haian holds 30% of Vietnam's domestic market); low threat to mainlanes.
  • Financial entrants: Chinese bank-backed leasing houses (about 9% of the merchant fleet; CDB Leasing 236 vessels) and yard-linked owners finance capacity that ends up on charter to carriers; this is a supply threat, not a customer threat.
  • Orthogonal entrants: digital forwarders and AI-driven freight matching (Flexport, truck-optimisation platforms); they compress Logistics margins at the edges.
C-39

Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

F2830th of 31

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: a source of risk; suppliers hold the power and have used it to lock Maersk into peak-cycle costs; the remaining release valve (handing back charters) is smaller than in past downturns.

  • Shipyards: books full to 2029 (more than four years of cover), China 65-72% of orders, prices about 46% above pre-COVID; Maersk ordered at these prices; delivery delays are possible.
  • Charter owners: charter-in availability very low; durations stretched to five to seven years; Maersk extended 9,500 TEU charters at USD 41,000 a day to 2031; but Seaspan retrofits cut slot cost 10-13%, and 416 chartered ships still allow redelivery as contracts expire.
  • Fuel: green methanol scarce and two to three times the price of conventional fuel; Maersk turned to LNG dual-fuel for optionality (the 26-ship order); USD 5.9bn of offtakes.
  • Others: container makers under a DOJ antitrust class action (price fixing); about 40,000 suppliers with a new risk platform; industrial suppliers tightening payment terms to 60-90 days.
1b

External tailwinds

7 answers
C-0130C-0318C-0432C-0818C-3222C-3733C-4340
C-01

To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

F3030th of 31

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: end-market demand is turning cyclically softer from a front-loaded first half of 2026, is structurally anchored at low single-digit growth, and will fall in the measure that matters for freight rates (TEU-miles) in 2027; it is not the swing variable in this case, and its visibility beyond six months is low.

  • Cyclical turn under way. Global container volumes grew about 5% in H1 2026, helped by tariff front-loading and strong Far East exports (July +4.5%). By late October the peak season had ended early: a Morgan Stanley survey found 79% of shippers already held all the peak-season inventory they need, transpacific spot rates softened, and carriers discounted US East Coast routes. Forwarders confirm the softening (DSV Q3 air and ocean volumes each -3%). The macro backdrop is weak for goods: US real hourly earnings falling, consumer confidence low, European real wages squeezed, China nominal retail sales +1.1% against GDP +4.5%.
  • Structural anchor. The container-trade-to-GDP multiplier has fallen from about 2x before 2008 to about 1.0-1.2x (Bernstein, DNB Carnegie to 2029). With global GDP growth of about 3%, structural volume growth is about 2-4% a year, which is what every broker uses (Deutsche Bank 4.1% for 2027).
  • The insight: in 2024-2026 a large part of "demand" was distance. Cape of Good Hope routing raised TEU-miles without raising TEUs. As Suez transits normalise, Deutsche Bank projects demand in TEU-miles falls 0.7% in 2027 even with TEU volumes up about 4%. The demand that supported rates was a routing artefact and it reverses mechanically.
  • Maersk's own volume is capacity-capped, not demand-led. Loaded volume fell about 1% in Q3 2026 (East-West -4%, North-South -0.5%, Intra-regional +7%) at 96% utilisation, after +6.5% in H1. Lower volume in Q3 was mostly lost share, not lost demand.
  • Visibility. Direction for the next six months: moderately visible (soft). Level beyond six months: low (tariffs, Hormuz, consumer). What has two-plus years of visibility is the supply side (C-02), and against supply growth of 7-13% a year in 2027-2028, the difference between 2% and 4% demand growth does not change the conclusion.

For the model: use Ocean volume growth of about 1-3% a year for Maersk to 2028 (below market because its own new ships arrive only in 2029-2030 and it is losing share), and treat demand as a second-order input; the rate assumption carries the model.

C-03

To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

F1830th of 31

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the dominant disruption force of 2024-2026, geopolitical capacity absorption (Red Sea and Hormuz), is weakening and will act strongly against Maersk as it unwinds; management's structural "landside bottleneck" thesis is being disproved by the data; decarbonisation is a near-term cost and a deferred option.

  • Route disruption unwinding. Suez containership transits doubled from Q1 2026 to 50-59% of 2023 levels by October (Bernstein, Morgan Stanley). Gemini moved four services back to Suez on 14 September (six trans-Suez services, a seventh in late October); CMA CGM had led; the Suez Canal Authority expects all Maersk services back by March 2027. A full return releases about 7% of effective global capacity (Morgan Stanley; 2-2.3m TEU) and cuts transit by about two weeks. Holdouts (ONE, HMM, Yang Ming, Evergreen) still avoid Suez, so the release is staggered over several quarters, which spreads rather than reduces the hit.
  • The landside thesis. Management argues trade grew 28% since 2019 against 21% for top-hub terminal capacity, so congestion will keep rates high. Disaggregated data contradict it: Greater China terminal utilisation 92-94%, but North-West Europe 58%, North America 63%, rest of world 60-70%; global utilisation falling as a record 58m TEU of terminal capacity opens in 2026 and 36m TEU in 2027 (Drewry via memo). APM Terminals' own utilisation fell to 85% from 90% in Q3. Congestion is Chinese, seasonal and weather-driven (typhoons), so episodic.
  • Congestion is two-edged for Maersk. It absorbed 8.5-12.6% of the fleet (4.3m TEU queued, Jefferies, October) and supported rates, but in Q3 it raised Ocean unit cost at constant fuel by 5%, capped Maersk's volume at 96% utilisation and cut Gemini reliability (68% in August-September against about 90%). Congestion-supported earnings are lower quality than rate-supported earnings.
  • Decarbonisation. EU ETS and FuelEU raise costs for all carriers; customers will not pay green premiums (an expert: USD 50 a container decides); the IMO delayed its global pricing framework. Maersk's dual-fuel lead is an advantage only when carbon pricing binds, which is beyond the model horizon.
  • The tail. A renewed Red Sea or Hormuz closure (memo about 20% in the next year) would re-absorb capacity within weeks; this is the only force that can reverse the direction, and it is unforecastable.
C-04

To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

F3231st of 31

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: political change is the largest single determinant of the next 18 months (Red Sea security), and its likely direction (normalisation) is negative; regulatory change is a steady, mostly recoverable cost headwind of about USD 0.3bn of incremental cost by 2027; trade policy is a mild demand headwind.

  • Geopolitics. The Red Sea and Hormuz are policy and security questions, not market ones. Houthi blockades of Saudi-linked vessels, the Perim capture and US-Iran tension keep a tail alive, but the trend in transits is toward return (C-03).
  • Carbon regulation. EU ETS at 100% of verified emissions from 2026: Maersk's cost USD 196m (2025) rising to about USD 491m by 2027, up to USD 935m in a full-coverage bullish carbon case (Morgan Stanley); FuelEU requires a 2% intensity cut (2025) rising to 6% (2030), penalties EUR 2,400 a tonne; UK ETS from July 2026. Surcharges recover these in a tight market; recovery weakens in a loose one. The IMO delay removes the timing of Maersk's green advantage from the model window.
  • Trade policy. US Section 301 tariffs on about 60 countries (24 July 2026), average US tariff about 22.8%; proposed USTR port fees on China-linked vessels (could marginally favour non-Chinese carriers); Mexico customs reform from 1 January 2027. Net: shorter hauls, more intra-regional trade (where Maersk grew 7%), a softer transpacific.
  • Competition law. Maersk's network depends on cooperation agreements; antitrust action could delay or constrain them (low probability, high impact). Israel's opposition to Hapag-Lloyd's ZIM deal shows political actors can block consolidation.
  • Tax. Tonnage tax keeps the tax rate on Ocean low (2-15% reported, FY2019-FY2025), but see C-54: it creates a tax floor at the trough.
C-08

To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

F1831st of 31

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: none is accelerating Maersk's demand; the industry driver that lifted its business in 2024-2026 (capacity absorption acting like demand) is decelerating and reversing.

  • Capacity absorption (Cape routing about 7% of fleet, Panama restrictions pushing 42% of US-Asia voyages via the Cape, congestion 8.5-12.6%) raised effective demand per ship; all three are fading or episodic.
  • Smaller positive drivers exist but are local: intra-regional and nearshoring trade (Maersk Intra-regional volume +7% in Q3), African imports (+13% in Q2) and Latin American imports (+7%), Terminals volume growth (+2.6% in Q3; Africa +14.1%, Europe +6.7%), airfreight rates in Forwarding (+19% revenue). None is large enough to move group earnings against the Ocean rate.
  • Congestion storage income at Terminals is a transitory boost that fades as queues clear.
C-32

How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

F2231st of 31

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: customer behaviour supports a fast transmission of the coming rate fall into Maersk's realised rates (spot exposure, shortening contracts, price-first buying); for a long thesis it is unsupportive, and the only supportive element (shippers paying for space in a tight market) is fragile.

  • Inventories full: 79% of shippers stocked for peak season; a late-2026 and early-2027 air pocket after front-loading (Morgan Stanley).
  • Contract structure: about 44% of Ocean volume on long-term rate products, 56% spot-exposed; contract terms shortening from annual to quarterly or half-yearly; contract rates follow spot with a lag of up to twelve months on Europe and transpacific lanes (Risks digest) but faster now; large e-commerce marketplaces contract up to 90% of cargo, commodity shippers stay spot.
  • Price sensitivity: "95% buy the cheapest rate"; green premiums refused; shippers demanded rate concessions of up to 35% in early 2026 (expert, in the Hapag-Lloyd file); in 2025 customers kept to agreed rates rather than reopening contracts, which helped the downside then and will cap the upside now.
  • Counterweight: carriers are seeking 2027 Asia-Europe contract increases and COSCO expects 2027 contracts up; if contracts settle near 2026 levels, H1 2027 holds up and the reset slips by about two quarters (memo).
  • Concentration: more than 100,000 customers, top 200 about 46% of group revenue at peak; more than half of the top 200 have science-based targets, a slow positive for green services.
C-37

What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

F3331st of 31

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: on balance they hurt over the model horizon; the most important political actor is the Suez Canal Authority, which is actively encouraging the return that releases capacity.

  • Suez Canal Authority expects all Maersk services back by March 2027; Gemini already runs six or seven trans-Suez services.
  • EU (ETS, FuelEU) raises costs (about USD 0.5bn a year by 2027 for Maersk), recoverable in tight markets; ship-owners can pass ETS costs to charterers during on-hire periods, which helps chartered tonnage.
  • IMO delayed its global pricing framework, removing near-term regulatory pull for Maersk's green fleet.
  • US tariffs and proposed China-vessel port fees; Mexico customs reform from January 2027.
  • Competition authorities watch alliances; Israel is opposing the Hapag-Lloyd-ZIM deal.
  • Denmark: exit taxes impede senior international hiring; tonnage tax regimes keep Ocean tax low.
  • Fragility: moderate; a single ruling on alliances or a ceasefire changes the picture.
C-43

How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

F4027th of 31

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: the addressable markets are large relative to Maersk and are not the constraint; they allow only low single-digit volume growth in Ocean, mid-single-digit growth in Terminals and mid-to-high single-digit growth in Logistics, so mid-term group revenue growth through the cycle is about 0-4% a year, with the Ocean rate swamping the trend in any given year.

  • Ocean: world container trade grows about 2-4% a year (1.0-1.2x GDP); Maersk has about 14% of capacity; its volume grows below market until its own ships arrive (2029-2030).
  • Terminals: gateway volume grows at GDP-plus (Bernstein) with tariff indexation of about 5-7% a year; capacity +30% by about 2028-2030; consensus Terminals EBITDA 2.06 / 2.12 / 2.21bn (FY2026-FY2028).
  • Logistics & Services: a very large market in which Maersk is small; target 10% organic growth has been missed (7.2% in 2024); consensus revenue 16.9 / 17.5 / 18.1bn (+3-4% a year).
  • Insight: the problem is not room to grow but the price of growth; any volume added in Ocean must be bought with ships into an oversupplied market.
1c

Overall external picture

2 answers
C-0614C-1012
C-06

Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

F1431st of 31

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: at its peak and already turning in rates and in the share price; reported revenue and EBITDA will still grow year on year until Q1 2027 because of a depressed base, with the first year-on-year revenue decline in Q2 2027.

  • Peak evidence. Q3 2026 revenue USD 18,095m (+27%), EBITDA 4,710m (+75%), loaded rate USD 3,287 per FFE (+46%), utilisation 96%. The SCFI peaked about 3,590 in early September; the B share peaked near DKK 24,900-25,380 shortly after.
  • Turning evidence. Q3 missed consensus EBITDA by about 14% and EPS by about 20%; volume turned negative; transpacific rates fell from late October; guidance implies Q4 EBITDA flat to down on Q3; the shares are 22% below their high despite a guidance raise.
  • The base effect the narrative will miss. Q1 2026 was depressed (revenue 12,970m, EBITDA 1,830m, Ocean EBIT -192m). Q1 2027 will therefore still grow year on year (the memo has about +13% revenue) even as rates fall sequentially; Q2 2027 is the first year-on-year decline (memo about -14%). The first two prints of 2027 will look better than the cycle, the next two worse.
  • Consensus shape: FY2027 revenue -8%, EBITDA -37%, EPS -63%; FY2028 EBITDA a further -17%.
C-10

Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

F1231st of 31

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the external picture is strongly negative over 6-18 months. The tailwinds are temporary and fading; the headwinds are contractual, quantified and peak in 2028. The only force able to reverse this is an unforecastable geopolitical event.

Thematic driver Direction and strength, next 6-18 months
Freight rates Strong headwind building from Q4 2026; consensus FY2027 EBITDA -37%
Fleet supply and orderbook Strong structural headwind 2027-2028 (fleet +7-13% a year against demand +2-4%)
Route disruption (Red Sea, Hormuz, Panama) Tailwind fading and reversing; about 20% tail of renewal
Port congestion Episodic tailwind to rates, headwind to Maersk's costs and volume
End-market demand Mild headwind; TEU-miles -0.7% in 2027
Input costs and carbon regulation Moderate headwind, recoverable only in tight markets
Terminal infrastructure demand Modest tailwind (GDP-plus volumes, indexed tariffs)
Forwarder consolidation Mild headwind (buyer power, channel conflict)
Decarbonisation Near-term cost; long-term option beyond the horizon
  • Value chain: power sits upstream (shipyards with books to 2029, charter owners locking five-to-seven-year charters) and downstream (consolidating forwarders, spot-oriented shippers); terminals hold steady; carriers are squeezed between them as their own capacity floods the market.
  • Industry: the most visible supply wave in the industry's history meets the unwinding of its largest temporary capacity buffer.
  • Sector (logistics): splitting into asset-light businesses whose recovery is management's to deliver (DSV, Kuehne + Nagel) and asset owners priced on a cycle they do not control (Maersk, Wallenius Wilhelmsen, NORDEN).

Internal change forces/risks

F4452nd of 53

Memo topic 2, Internal change forces: the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Internal change forces

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

Internal change is real, mostly sensible and well funded, but small against the cycle, and its most consequential new element is a regression in capital discipline. The leadership refresh (Kuehne + Nagel-lineage CFO and Solutions head), Gemini's cost gains, the Terminals expansion and the overhead cut are genuine positives worth perhaps USD 0.8-1.3bn of EBITDA by FY2028; the fleet order, rising fixed commitments, share loss to a twenty-year low and the vessel-life accounting change offset much of that in quality terms. Guidance has been raised three times, but by the freight rate, not by management. For the model: hold capex at USD 5-6bn a year to 2028 regardless of earnings; add self-help of about 0.5-0.8bn EBITDA spread over FY2027-FY2028 in the base case (more only in the deeper case); assume no buyback expansion and a dividend at about 40% of underlying profit; and keep a Terminals separation and a capital-markets reset outside the base case as options.

2a

Internal risks

6 answers
Judgement18C-1448C-8618C-5935C-8125D-0325
Group judgement

Risks

F1831st of 31

Layer 1 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The risk profile is dominated by one fundamental variable with a wide, two-sided cone and a one-sided price: the downside is more probable, the upside larger and faster, and the price already sits near where the upside would put it. The growth engine has turned beneath still-rising reported revenue; the sudden fundamental risks cluster in a deeper price war (about 30%) and in book-value events (impairment); the sentiment risks are high because the stock is crowded, at the top of its multiple history and in a risk-off market; and a fair trough multiple of 0.4-0.5x book is 30-40% below today's price. For the model: run three scenarios rather than one (base, deeper about 30%, disruption-extended about 20%), carry an Ocean impairment scenario through book value and depreciation, and date the risk calendar (February 2027 guidance and annual report; Q2 2027 first year-on-year decline).

C-14

To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

F4828th of 31

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: execution standards improved structurally in 2025 and H1 2026 through Gemini and overhead cuts, but Q3 2026 showed those gains are not robust when the network is congested; governance and earnings-quality standards slipped with the vessel-life change.

  • Improving: Gemini delivered USD 950m of benefits against an 820m-1.1bn target, about 90% schedule reliability in year one (industry 50-60%), Ocean unit cost at fixed bunker -7% in Q1 2026 and -0.8% in Q2 despite fuel +44%, and a 26% rise in Ocean volume through Maersk-owned terminals (Q1). About 1,000 corporate roles cut (15% of corporate functions; savings about USD 130-180m against restructuring cost of about 200m). Logistics & Services EBIT margin up for nine consecutive quarters to 5.8% (Q3). Terminals revenue per move +6.4% against cost per move +5.7%.
  • Slipping: Q3 Ocean unit cost at constant fuel +5%; reliability down to about 68% in August-September; loaded volume -1% against a market +2-4% (share loss at 96% utilisation); Solutions margin 1.7% (Q2) and 3.2% (Q3) against 3.8-4.9% a year earlier; acquired logistics brands still on separate IT.
  • Governance and quality: the 20-to-25-year vessel-life change adds about USD 700m a year to EBIT without changing cash and was not explained in depth; remuneration has no EPS or TSR element.
C-86

What negative corporate change developments are present/plausible?

F1831st of 31

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: a dense, mutually reinforcing set, and several are dated within the next 15 months.

  • Capital discipline regression: the 483,600 TEU order at peak yard prices into a supply wave; commitments +46% in nine months.
  • Market share loss: about 13.7% capacity share (DAOL, mid-2026), the lowest in 20 years (19.3% in 2018); CMA CGM expected to pass Maersk in 2027; Q3 volume -1% against market +2-4%.
  • Earnings quality: the vessel-life change (+USD 700m a year EBIT) is about 90% of consensus FY2028 EBIT.
  • Impairment risk (dated, February 2027 and the FY2027 test): Ocean headroom about USD 1.1bn after the life extension lifted carrying values; a 0.5-point change in the discount rate is worth about 2.7bn (memo). Logistics goodwill of about USD 4.6bn now tested across three units, exposing Solutions.
  • Distribution decline: consensus DPS falls from USD 203 to 76 to 21; buyback expansion unlikely.
  • Fixed commitments: about USD 9.3bn of leases not yet started, 5.9bn of fuel offtakes, charters to 2031.
  • Organisation: turnover 14%; a matrix described by former executives as "too big, too complex, and too slow"; forwarder channel conflict; Gemini hub concentration (Tanjung Pelepas) as a single point of failure.
C-59

How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system (as at Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair)? Such that the goals, strategy, culture, execution, decisions are congruent, holistic and symbiotic?

F3528th of 31

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system?

Judgement: low congruence: strategy, channel, incentives and capital decisions pull in different directions.

  • Strategy versus channel: integrating logistics conflicts with selling to forwarders.
  • Incentives versus capital decisions: the 2025-2027 long-term incentive puts 80% weight on segment return on invested capital (Ocean, Logistics, Terminals) and none on EPS or TSR; a large Ocean fleet order raises Ocean invested capital and lowers Ocean ROIC, which sits oddly with that incentive; Morgan Stanley warns the ROIC weight could also encourage shrinking the asset base.
  • Culture: former employees say the "our word is our bond" ethos is gone and the matrix is "too big, too complex, and too slow"; engagement scored 83 on Glint (top 10%) while turnover rose to 14%, a disconnect between survey and behaviour.
  • Positive congruence: Gemini, Terminals and the decarbonisation agenda are aligned with each other (hubs, dual-fuel, gateway investment).

D-03

In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?

F256th of 6

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: as concentrated as a forecast can be. One variable, the average loaded Ocean rate, generates about 160% of the fall in EBITDA from FY2026 to FY2028; its mirror image, the fall in cost per FFE caused by the same events, gives back about 50%; everything else in the group sums to about 10%. The same variable explains the last six years (EBITDA of 5.8bn, 36.6bn, 9.3bn, 14.2bn). Beneath it sit only two root causes, one contractual (the delivery schedule) and one political (the Red Sea). Concentration makes the case simple to state and to track, and it removes the averaging of errors that makes forecasts predictable: the error in the forecast is the error in one number multiplied by about 13.9m FFE.

FY2026 to FY2028 EBITDA bridge USDbn Share of the change Who controls it
Ocean rate (about 2,840 to 1,920 per FFE) -12.8 161% The market
Ocean cash cost per FFE (about 2,060 to 1,770) +4.0 -50% Mostly the same events (Suez, congestion, oil); about 1.0bn is self-help
Ocean volume +0.5 -6% Bought with price
Terminals, Logistics & Services, unallocated +0.35 -4% The company
Total -7.9 100%
  • Rate and cost are one driver seen twice. The Suez return, the clearing of congestion and lower oil cut the rate and the cost together. Netted, the Ocean unit margin explains 111% of the change. This is why the forecast is less fragile than "one variable" suggests in the base case (a faster normalisation cuts both) and more fragile in the deeper case (a price war cuts the rate without cutting cost).
  • Below EBITDA the drivers look diffuse but are not independent. Tax that does not fall with profit, interest income that decays, a share count that stops shrinking and depreciation that drifts up together take about USD 70-85 a share a year from FY2028-FY2029. Three of the four are fixed charges, and the fourth, interest income, is a function of the same rate through the cash balance. They do not diversify the forecast; they convert a break-even EBIT into a loss.
  • Everything the company says it will deliver, added together, equals a move of about USD 115 per FFE in the rate. Self-help of 1.0bn (about USD 70 a share), Terminals growth of about 0.4bn of EBITDA to FY2030 (about 20 after tax) and Logistics & Services growth of about 0.35bn (about 17) come to about USD 110 a share over four years. USD 100 per FFE is USD 95-97. The realised rate moved by more than 500 per FFE in each of the last two quarters (2,081, 2,746, 3,287).
  • The root causes are two, with opposite predictability. The delivery schedule is contracted (3.3-3.5m TEU in 2027, 5.2-5.5m in 2028, 3.6m in 2029) and its direction is close to certain; slippage of 21-35% occurred in 2017 but only after losses. The Red Sea is a security question; its direction is now visible (73% of 2023 transits) but it can reverse in weeks. The contractual cause fixes the direction of the forecast; the political cause fixes its timing and its tail.
  • A second, smaller driver exists and the company's sensitivities hide it: network utilisation. Q3 2026 missed consensus EBITDA by 14% in a quarter when the rate rose 46%, because volume fell 1% and unit cost at fixed fuel rose 5%. The published sensitivities (rate, volume, bunker, currency) would not have predicted that miss. Morgan Stanley uses 1.0bn, not 1.3bn, per USD 100 per FFE and ten times the company's volume sensitivity; DNB Carnegie five to seven times. For 2027 this second driver works in Maersk's favour (congestion clears), which is the cost relief of D-02.
  • Against peers. Hapag-Lloyd has the same single driver with a smaller cushion (consensus has its EBIT margin at -1% three years out). DSV and Kuehne + Nagel have several partly independent drivers (synergies, conversion ratio, yields, volumes), which is why their forecasts are more predictable. Maersk's Terminals and Logistics floor (about 4.1-4.8bn of EBITDA; 2.3-2.9bn of EBIT) does not diversify the change; it sets the level around which the single driver swings, and it is why the sign of group EBIT at the trough is uncertain where Hapag-Lloyd's is expected to be negative.
  • How often has a one-driver forecast been met for this company? Measured by the first annual EBITDA guidance against the outcome: 2019, about 5.0bn against 5.7bn; 2023, 8-11bn against about 9.6-9.8bn; 2025, 6-9bn against 9.5bn; but 2024, 1-6bn against 12.1bn, and 2026, 4.5-7.0bn against 14.0-14.5bn. In years without a new disruption the outcome landed inside or just above a wide range; the two very large errors were both upward and both caused by a new closure (the Red Sea from December 2023; Hormuz in 2026). The one-driver forecast is reasonably reliable conditional on no new disruption and useless otherwise. That shape of error, two regimes instead of one wide bell, governs D-04.

Where I differ from the earlier sets. I agree with the first set (C-79) that breadth of perimeter does not buy predictability. I add that the concentration is in the root causes too, and that the second driver (utilisation and unit cost), which both sets treat as part of the cost line, is the one place where the company's own published transmission failed in the latest quarter.

C-11

To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders (e.g. finance director, operations director, board, division heads, etc.) or other key actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) changed, and what does the change signal?

F4827th of 31

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the chief executive and the controlling owner are unchanged; the finance and logistics leadership has been rebuilt with forwarder-lineage outsiders. The signal is continuity of the integrator strategy with a push to fix Logistics, not a change of direction or of capital discipline.

  • Unchanged: CEO Vincent Clerc (internal, 27-plus years at Maersk, CEO since January 2023); Chair Robert Maersk Uggla (since 2022, also CEO of A.P. Moller Holding); A.P. Moller Holding with 51.5% of votes on 41.5% of capital.
  • Changed: CFO Patrick Jany (ex-Clariant, credited with financial discipline) was replaced in February 2026 by Robert Erni (30-plus years of logistics finance: 20 at Kuehne + Nagel, CFO of Panalpina and Dachser). Bruno Plantaz (ex-Kuehne + Nagel and CEVA) heads Solutions from 1 November 2026 after Narin Phol left; Roberta Duarte returns as Chief People Officer; Ditlev Blicher runs North America. The board added Xavier Urbain (CEVA, 2025).
  • What it signals. Every external hire comes from the asset-light forwarding world, so the priority is Logistics margins and integration. It does not yet signal an asset-light turn in capital allocation: the 500k TEU newbuild order was placed in Erni's first months.
  • Fragility. The executive board has only two members; there is no obvious internal successor to Clerc; senior bench depletion (Henriette Hallberg Thygesen to Terma in 2024, Aymeric Chandavoine in 2025); employee turnover 14% in 2025 (11% in 2024); a competitor's former director cites a "humongous number of executive changes".

For the model: no change to forecasts from leadership; a possible capital-markets reset under the new CFO is a low-probability positive option, not a base case.

C-12

To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

F3531st of 31

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the stated strategy (integrated end-to-end logistics) is unchanged, but the priorities have shifted materially back toward Ocean fleet growth and Terminals; the shift is well resourced financially and poorly articulated, and its fleet element is pro-cyclical.

  • The real change: 26 dual-fuel 18,600 TEU ships (483,600 TEU, delivery 2029-2030), justified by Clerc as needed to keep about 14% share and handle landside congestion. It contradicts the 4.1-4.3m TEU fleet cap and Clerc's own February 2025 statement that there is "no correlation" between fleet size and margin. Bernstein reads it as share defence over capital discipline.
  • Other changes, sensibly framed: Logistics & Services split into Forwarding, Solutions and Landside (from 1 April 2026) as separate cash-generating units, for comparability with forwarders; Gemini hub-and-spoke network (February 2025); a USD 3bn Terminals programme to add about 30% capacity; disposal of Maersk Training and H2S Safety.
  • Resourcing: total capital commitments USD 19.5bn at Q3 2026 against 13.4bn at end-2025; joint 2026-2027 capex USD 10-11bn; consensus capex 5.3-6.0bn a year to 2028 against 3.6-4.8bn in FY2023-FY2025.
  • Articulation gap: the fleet cap was abandoned without a stated new capital framework, return hurdle or capacity target; buyback decisions were deferred to February 2027.

For the model: capex does not fall with earnings; keep 5-6bn a year through 2028 and treat the newbuild order as fixed.

C-13

To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions been set or raised? (choices)

  • FY2026 underlying EBITDA: February 4.5-7.0bn; 29 June (ad hoc) 8.0-10.0bn; 13 August 10.5-12.5bn; 5 November 14.0-14.5bn. EBIT: February -1.5bn to -1.0bn; June 2-4bn; August 4.5-6.5bn; November 8.0-8.5bn. Free cash flow: about -1.5bn, then above 0, then above 3.0bn.
  • Mid-term targets unchanged and unmet: return on invested capital above 7.5% through the cycle (5.7% in 2025); Logistics & Services organic growth of 10% (7.2% in 2024) and EBIT margin of 6% (4.8% in 2025, 5.8% in Q3 2026); Terminals return above 9% (met, 13.8-16%). No capital-markets day is scheduled in the files.
  • Informational value of the raises is falling: the November raise was the first the shares did not reward (Morgan Stanley: "not enough to support shares"); Bernstein says the new range only matched consensus.

C-15

To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units? (actions)

F4526th of 31

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: there is little perimeter reduction in progress (only small disposals), but the owner's record of separating businesses (Oil 2017, Drilling 2019, Svitzer 2024) and the new Logistics structure keep a Terminals separation or Logistics pruning alive as an option, with no evidence it is imminent.

  • Under way: sale of Maersk Training and Maersk H2S Safety Services to OpenGate Capital (August 2026), a small non-core exit.
  • Enabling: three Logistics cash-generating units make loss-making pieces (warehousing in Solutions; Fulfilled by Maersk at -5.5% margin in late 2024) easier to isolate, and also expose about USD 4.6bn of logistics goodwill to unit-level impairment tests.

C-16

To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly? (actions)

C-6012th of 31

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: expansion is concentrated in Terminals in growing gateways, which is sensible and high-return; Logistics expansion into warehousing has been less sensible so far.

  • Terminals (sensible, close to the core): Laldia, Bangladesh (greenfield, 900k TEU); Suape, Brazil (USD 350m, +55% capacity, operating September 2026); Da Nang, Vietnam (50-year concession); 37.5% of Jeddah South Container Terminal; Maasvlakte II doubling to 5.4m TEU (25% sold to Hapag-Lloyd); Bremerhaven (EUR 1bn, +33%); Rijeka, Callao. Terminals return on invested capital 13.8-16% against a 9% hurdle; 63% of revenue from third-party carriers.
  • Logistics (less sensible): new warehouses (for example a USD 100m, 57,300 square metre centre in Hopedale, Massachusetts) carry "whitespace" costs that cut Solutions margins to 1.7-3.2%; landbridge solutions in the Gulf were a genuine success (Landside 6.3% margin, Q2).
  • Industrial adjacencies: India-made containers (DCM Shriram, 1,000-unit follow-on order), talks with Indian yards about 3,000 TEU ships; small.
C-17

To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

F2030th of 31

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent?

Judgement: Ocean price rises are nominal and exogenous (market rates plus cost-recovery surcharges) and are about to reverse; real price rises exist only in Terminals and are thin; a reliability premium is latent and increasingly cited by management, but unproven.

  • Ocean: loaded rate +22% (Q2) and +46% (Q3), but realised rates track the spot indices with a beta of about 0.4-0.5 and a one-quarter lag (Q1 2026: -14% against -32% for the Drewry index; Q2: +32% quarter on quarter against SCFI +55%). Surcharges (emergency bunker USD 300-400, war risk, a USD 200 per TEU fuel surcharge, a 16% intermodal fuel fee, heavy-load USD 500) recovered about USD 500m a month of conflict costs: pass-through, not pricing power. Q3 rate growth of 46% missed Deutsche Bank's expected 55%.
  • Reliability premium: experts claim 5-10% premiums from some shippers, but another expert says "approximately 95% of the time, standard customers simply demand the cheapest freight rate"; Morgan Stanley doubts customers pay for punctuality in a soft market; customers refused green premiums.
  • Terminals: revenue per move +6.4-7.1% against cost per move +5.7% and labour inflation about 7%, on indexed concessions averaging about 22 years: real but narrow.
C-18

To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

F4529th of 31

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: M&A has stepped down, not up; the restraint since January 2023 is sensible, but the legacy of more than USD 8bn of pro-cyclical logistics acquisitions (2019-2023) still earns below the cost of capital and remains poorly integrated.

  • Legacy deals: LF Logistics USD 3.6bn, Pilot Freight Services 1.6bn, Senator International 644m, Performance Team 545m (more than 7.7-8bn since 2019), bought near peak multiples; Logistics returns below the group's 7.5% hurdle since 2021; acquired brands such as Pilot and LF still run on standalone systems; retention targets for acquired leaders were often missed.
  • Current activity: small terminal stakes and concessions (Jeddah 37.5%), plus disposals. No large deal pending.
  • Against peers: CMA CGM bought FedEx Supply Chain (USD 1.4bn, 2026); Hapag-Lloyd agreed to buy ZIM (USD 4.2bn, USD 35 a share, a 58% premium, facing Israeli opposition); DSV is integrating Schenker. Maersk is the one large carrier not consolidating, which is defensible at this point in the cycle.
C-19

To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

D4521st of 31

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the balance sheet is being turned toward capex and fixed commitments rather than incremental returns; the FY2026 dividend will be large because the policy is mechanical; an expanded buyback is mooted but deferred to February 2027 and is less likely than not.

  • Capacity: net cash excluding leases about USD 15.5bn (end-2025), cash 18.4-21.4bn, BBB+/Baa1; net cash including leases USD 3.2bn at Q3 2026.
  • Returns policy: dividend 30-50% of underlying net profit (40% used in practice; FY2024 DKK 1,120, FY2025 DKK 480); consensus FY2026 DPS USD 203 (about DKK 1,370, about 6.9% yield at DKK 19,895), falling to USD 76 (FY2027) and 21 (FY2028). The buyback was suspended in February 2024 (USD 3bn programme), restarted at USD 2bn in 2025 and cut to USD 1bn for 2026; management has modelled lifting it to USD 2bn and deferred the decision to February 2027. Morgan Stanley expects 2bn; the memo puts some programme at about 45% and a 2bn programme at about 15%.
  • History: share count fell from 20.1m (FY2019) to 14.7m (FY2025), a 27% reduction; USD 10.9bn of dividends (DKK 4,300 a share) paid in early 2023 at the peak.
  • Competing calls on cash: capex 5-6bn a year, lease repayments 3.5-3.9bn a year, commitments 19.5bn, green fuel offtakes 5.9bn.

For the model: base case: FY2026 dividend paid March 2027 at about 40% of FY2026 underlying net profit; no new buyback beyond the 1bn programme; a 1bn buyback is about 2.3% of shares at today's price.

C-20

What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

–55

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: several genuine positives, all either small relative to the cycle or 18-plus months away.

  • Terminals compounding and value discovery: Hapag-Lloyd's purchase of 25% of Maasvlakte II gives an external price for APM Terminals assets; the 3bn expansion adds about 30% capacity; Gemini funnels volume into seven Maersk hubs.
  • The downturn playbook is proven: 2016 (idling, charter returns, about 4,000 roles, unit cost below USD 2,000 per FFE) and 2023 (about 10,000 roles, network cost about -14%); a 43% leased-vessel ratio (by capacity) still gives some flexibility.
  • New CFO from cost-disciplined, family-owned forwarding, with scope to reset targets.
  • An Ocean impairment (headroom about USD 1.1bn at end-2025) would cut book value but lower depreciation by about USD 0.2bn a year.
  • Decarbonisation lead (net zero 2040, more than 50% of 2027 dual-fuel demand covered by biomethanol offtakes) becomes a cost advantage only if carbon pricing binds, probably after 2028.
C-87

To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

F4229th of 31

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the controlling family has indicated no change; it backs Clerc's integrator strategy and the fleet renewal, and insulates management from outside pressure; its historical willingness to restructure is the only latent scope.

C-21

Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

F3831st of 31

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the positive changes are real, financially well resourced and moderately broad, but an order of magnitude too small to matter against the cycle, and the newest element (fleet growth) points the other way.

  • Size of self-help: the memo's remaining pool is about USD 0.8bn of EBITDA by FY2028 (Ocean network about 0.55bn, overhead about 0.18bn, Solutions whitespace about 0.1bn), possibly 1.1-1.3bn if losses deepen and the 2016/2023 playbook is run. Against this, base-rate erosion is about USD 5bn of EBITDA in FY2027 and about 2.4bn more in FY2028 (memo), or about 5.4bn and 1.5bn on consensus.
  • Breadth: Ocean (Gemini), Terminals (expansion), Logistics (re-segmentation and new leaders) and overhead are all addressed.
  • Resourcing: abundant cash, but management bandwidth is thin (two-person executive board, new CFO, high turnover).
  • Direction: self-help lowers the trough by a few hundred million dollars and moves Maersk's position relative to peers; it does not change the trough's sign or timing.
C-22

What staircase of novel positive developments lies ahead: which new, dated things can the market look forward to within the next 6 months, in months 6 to 18, and in months 18 to 36; how novel is each; and how well-founded?

F2231st of 31

Judgement: the near-term staircase of positives is short, mostly known and outweighed by dated negatives; the well-founded positives (falling deliveries, scrapping, Terminals capacity, a trough-on-trough valuation) arrive in months 18 to 36.

Foundations

3

Value chain dynamics and industry structure

F1831st of 31

The Investment Analyst's group judgement: Industry and Value Chain

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Industry and Value Chain

F1831st of 31

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The industry structure is poor and the company's position in Ocean is weak and weakening; the structure gives Maersk survival, not returns. Container shipping is concentrated in form (top five about 65%) but behaves as a commodity because its price-setters are volume-first, cash-rich and not run for listed-equity returns, and because exit barriers keep capacity in the market through losses; history (2016, 2023) shows prices fall hard as utilisation falls and discipline appears only after widespread losses. Maersk is slipping from second to third, losing share to carriers with spare capacity, in conflict with the forwarders who supply up to half its volume, and locked by suppliers into peak-cycle costs; customers' spot exposure and shortening contracts mean the coming rate fall will reach its realised rate within about a quarter. Only APM Terminals sits in an advantaged part of the chain. For the model: assume no pricing support from structure in 2027-2028, a Maersk Ocean volume growth below the market until 2029, a realised rate beta of about 0.4-0.5 to spot with a one-quarter lag, and treat the precedents of 2016 (deep) and 2019 (moderate) as the two bookends for the trough rate.

3a

Value chain dynamics

6 answers
C-3222C-3325C-3425C-3733C-3818C-3928
C-32How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?F2231st of 31full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-33

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

F2531st of 31

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the intermediaries hinder Maersk; forwarders supply up to half of ocean volume and increasingly treat Maersk as a competitor, and the hindrance grows in a loose market when forwarders can route cargo to any neutral carrier at the lowest price.

  • Bernstein (Madre): forwarders "will avoid booking with Maersk Ocean due to its aggressive integrator strategy"; experts report diversion to MSC and CMA CGM; Maersk ended direct contracts with some forwarders, pushing them to spot-only.
  • Forwarder consolidation (DSV-Schenker, about 7% share; the top three meet in every tender) increases their bargaining weight.
  • Peers avoid the conflict: Hapag-Lloyd stays a pure carrier and partners with forwarders (Kuehne + Nagel "Ship Green"); CMA CGM owns CEVA but serves forwarders.
  • Size: the memo estimates the integrator-forwarder conflict costs about one point of volume a year in a loose market; at 100k FFE = USD 10m of EBIT the direct earnings effect is small, but it worsens network utilisation when fixed costs dominate.
  • Fragility: high; the Risks report rates a forwarder boycott high plausibility, with monthly NVOCC volume shares as the indicator.
C-34

How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

F2531st of 31

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: competitors' behaviour hurts Maersk; it is losing volume to carriers with spare capacity now and will face price competition from them in 2027-2028; its position is defensible on cost and balance sheet, not on share or price.

  • Hurting: MSC's standalone direct-call network takes volume from constrained networks; CMA CGM's fleet growth; COSCO and the Ocean Alliance dominate the transpacific (about 35-37% of capacity, more than 40 services); in Q3 2026 peers grew while Maersk's full network shrank 1%.
  • Helping: Hapag-Lloyd, the Gemini partner (about 340 ships, 3.7m TEU), shares hub and cost economics and has bought into Maersk's terminals; its ZIM deal, if approved, adds volume to Gemini-linked networks only if ZIM's services are brought in, which is not established.
  • Defensible elements: Gemini unit cost about 3-7% below non-Gemini carriers (memo), net cash, Terminals earnings floor; these let Maersk outlast weaker carriers in a price war but not avoid it.

C-38

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

F1831st of 31

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the disruption forces act hardest on carriers, squeezed between strengthening suppliers upstream and strengthening buyers downstream while their own new capacity floods the market; they leave Maersk's Ocean position fragile and its Terminals position robust.

Industry structure

10 answers
Judgement18C-2435C-2545C-2638C-2730C-2812C-2910C-3112C-3555C-3655
Group judgementGroup judgement: Industry and Value Chain (Layer 1)F1831st of 31full answer under 3 Value chain dynamics and industry structure
C-24

Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

F3529th of 31

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Maersk is number two in container shipping today and is likely to become number three in 2027; the gap to number one is about eight share points and widening, by choice until 2026 and now by arithmetic, since its own new ships arrive only in 2029-2030.

  • Ocean: MSC about 21.5% of capacity (7.3m TEU, 2.9m TEU on order); Maersk 13.6-14% (about 4.7m TEU, operated fleet held at 4.1-4.6m TEU; 754 vessels, 338 owned and 416 chartered); CMA CGM 12.7% (fleet +34% gross to 2028; Alphaliner expects it to pass Maersk in 2027); COSCO about 11%; Hapag-Lloyd about 7% (more than 3m TEU with ZIM, fifth). Maersk's share was about 20% before 2019 (19.3% in 2018) and is at a twenty-year low.
  • Terminals: a top-tier global gateway operator (53 gateway terminals), in a fragmented field where scale matters less than location and concession quality.
  • Logistics: sub-scale against DSV (about 7% of global forwarding after Schenker), Kuehne + Nagel and DHL.
  • Insight: Clerc capped the fleet on the argument that share does not drive margin. The 2026 order reverses that argument without the ships to back it for three years, so the share loss continues through the trough, when share matters most for absorbing fixed network cost.
C-25

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

F4524th of 31

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: highly concentrated in form (top five about 65%, top ten about 85%), but the distribution is not steep enough, and ownership is too heterogeneous, for concentration to produce pricing discipline.

  • Top five 65.4%; top ten 84.7-86.3%, up from about 65% in 2015. Numbers six to ten hold about 20%, so the top five hold about 3.2 times the next five, short of the five-times steepness that typically marks a disciplined oligopoly.
  • Concentration is higher still at route level (route Herfindahl indices up to 5,100) and through alliances: three East-West alliances plus MSC control about 62% of total capacity and 69.7% of East-West mainlane capacity (Ocean Alliance 31.2%, Gemini 21.3%, Premier 17.2%).
  • Why it does not discipline: two of the top five are not run for listed-equity returns (MSC private, COSCO state), CMA CGM is family-owned, and all hold large cash; exit barriers are high (C-31). Consolidation since 2015 has reduced the number of players without reducing the urge to add capacity.
C-26

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

F3829th of 31

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: shares are unstable among incumbents and are bought with ships; new entrants have taken almost nothing.

  • MSC moved from below Maersk to clear number one (overtaking in 2022 after expanding its fleet 32%); Maersk fell from about 19-20% to 13.7%; CMA CGM rose and will pass Maersk. Share follows each carrier's ordering decisions, not its service quality.
  • New entrants: small opportunistic transpacific operators in 2021-2022 left when rates fell; regional intra-Asia players (Haian) grow with small newbuilds. Capital needs (billions), networks and alliances block entry. The real "entrants" are financial: Chinese bank-backed leasing houses now own about 9% of the global merchant fleet and supply capacity through charters.
  • So-what: with share a function of capacity, a carrier that stops ordering loses share; one that orders adds to oversupply. This is the trap Maersk has now chosen to stay in.
C-27

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

F3030th of 31

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: share consolidates toward the largest and most aggressive orderers (MSC, CMA CGM), not toward the most profitable or most advantaged; Maersk is not among the consolidators in Ocean, but its Terminals arm is among the advantaged in the chain.

  • Volume moves to MSC's standalone direct-call network and to carriers with spare capacity (Q3 2026: Maersk -1% while the market grew 2-4% and COSCO +4.5%).
  • The advantaged in returns are elsewhere in the chain: terminals (33% EBIT margins), asset-light forwarders (DSV ROIC 15.4%), shipyards (books to 2029). Container carriers' through-cycle returns are below their cost of capital (Maersk ROIC 5.7% in 2025 against a 7.5% target; Hapag-Lloyd 4.6% against WACC 9.4%).
  • Consolidation by M&A continues (Hapag-Lloyd and ZIM) but removes no capacity.
C-28

What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

F1231st of 31

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the price-setters are volume-first and willing to suffer losses; prices fall hard when volume or utilisation falls; cooperation exists on cost (alliances), not on price.

  • Volume-first: MSC ("volume and network expansion", largest orderbook); CMA CGM (+34% fleet); COSCO (state backing, domestic cargo guarantees). More return-oriented: Maersk and Hapag-Lloyd (pure-play-plus, 14% orderbook-to-fleet, cost programme USD 1.1bn).
  • Willing to suffer: industry net cash USD 26-30bn; Bernstein, Jefferies and DNB Carnegie all describe a "game of chicken" in which lines keep modern ships rather than scrap.
  • Prices fall with utilisation: 2015-2016 (two years of depressed rates, rates at historic lows, Hanjin's failure), 2023 (Maersk group EBITDA fell to USD 839m in Q4 2023 and Ocean EBIT was negative). Carriers lose money below about USD 1,000 per TEU in spot (expert); Hapag-Lloyd's all-in break-even is about USD 1,100 per TEU, ZIM's about 300 higher. Discipline (blank sailings, idling above 10%) appears only after losses are widespread.
C-29

How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

F1031st of 31

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the attitude is stable in its instability: situation-dependent, reactive and quick to cut price when capacity is loose. A shark attack in 2027-2028 is likely, because the leading players have both the motive (filling new ships) and the means (cash), and Maersk's share loss and spot exposure make it a visible target.

  • Last three years: 2023 losses did not stop ordering; 2024-2026 windfalls produced record orders (orderbook up about 1.6m TEU in Q3 2026 alone; MSC kept ordering after passing Maersk). The 2M split (January 2025) ended Maersk's cost and capacity alignment with MSC, which now competes head-on with direct calls.
  • Precedent for a shark attack: 2016, when the largest carriers held rates below cost until a weaker carrier failed.
  • Who is exposed: carriers with high spot shares and high cost (ZIM: 65% of transpacific volume on spot; Jefferies estimates cash burn of USD 775m at 2023 rates). Maersk has 56% spot-exposed volume, so it will feel a shark attack faster than Hapag-Lloyd (about 55% on long-term contracts), although its Gemini cost base and net cash mean it will survive one.
C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

F1231st of 31

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the structure is unsupportive (an oligopoly in form, a commodity market in behaviour), and the only support of 2024-2026 was exogenous and extremely fragile: removable within weeks by a Suez return.

  • Entry minus exit decides intensity (Wisdom Library: competitive intensity is entry minus exit). Entry is hard, but exit is harder: 25-year asset lives (Maersk now depreciates over 25 years itself), state and family owners, cash buffers, cross-subsidy from terminals and logistics. Capacity stays in the market through losses.
  • Value capture is elsewhere: shipyards (gross margin about 15-34%), terminals (33-36% EBITDA/EBIT margins), forwarders; ocean carriage EBIT margin -2.3% in Q1 2026.
  • The fragile support: Cape routing (about 7% of fleet), congestion (8.5-12.6%), Panama restrictions. Each disappears on a political or weather change.
  • What would make it supportive: a margin-first turn by MSC or CMA CGM (seen briefly in 2016-2017 once losses were widespread), or scrapping above 1% of fleet a year.
C-35To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?C-5512th of 31full answer under 1a External headwinds/threats
C-36

To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

C5510th of 31

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: low and slow; the structural substitute is shorter trade (nearshoring), not another mode.

  • China-Europe rail and the Trans-Caspian corridor gain during sea disruptions and high oil prices but carry small volumes and need port bottlenecks fixed.
  • Air takes only high-value, urgent cargo.
  • Nearshoring and multi-sourcing (buying shifting from China to Mexico) shorten hauls and move volume to intra-regional lanes, where Maersk grew 7% in Q3; this lowers TEU-miles per unit of trade over time.

Memo topic 3, Value chain, structure, model, financials, management: the Rater's score

4a

Business quality

F3229th of 318 answers
Judgement32C-4135C-4245C-4340C-4450C-4520C-4638C-4735
Group judgement

Company - Competitive advantage

F3229th of 31

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Maersk's genuine competitive advantage is concentrated in APM Terminals, a strong and widening concession-based moat that produces the group's earnings floor; the rest of the group competes as a capable but advantage-poor operator. Gemini gives a real 3-7% unit-cost edge and top reliability in normal conditions, but it is shared with Hapag-Lloyd, fragile under congestion and competed away as rates fall; scale and integration are not moats in an unbounded, price-first market; pricing power is absent in Ocean. For the model: treat Terminals as a compounding asset (EBITDA growing about 5-7% a year from about 2.1bn) and Ocean as a price-taker whose Gemini edge keeps its EBITDA positive at a rate that puts the marginal carrier in cash loss (the memo estimates Maersk Ocean EBITDA at about USD 165 per FFE at the trough rate), rather than as a business that can defend price.

F3530th of 31

Judgement: one strong and widening moat (APM Terminals), one moderate but shared and competable cost edge (Gemini), a strong balance sheet that is a survival advantage rather than a moat, and weak or negative advantages elsewhere. The moat sits in the part of the group that is about 7-10% of revenue and about 15% of FY2026 EBITDA (but 25-30% of consensus FY2028 EBITDA).
