C-42

How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

F4526th of 31

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Maersk is the largest listed container-logistics group by revenue but only the second-largest carrier by capacity and sub-scale in forwarding; its scale confers procurement, network breadth, terminal funding capacity and survival in a price war, but not pricing power or unit-cost leadership over MSC.

  • Revenue USD 54.0bn (FY2025), about 65bn consensus FY2026; against Hapag-Lloyd EUR 18.6bn, DSV DKK 247bn (about USD 37bn), CMB.TECH USD 1.7bn. More than 100,000 employees; about 130 countries; more than 100,000 customers; about 40,000 suppliers; 754 vessels.
  • What scale gives: network coverage that a niche carrier cannot match; the ability to fund a USD 3bn terminal programme and a green fleet while paying dividends; bunker and charter purchasing; access to large accounts (top 200 customers about 46% of revenue at peak).
  • What it does not give: lower unit cost than MSC (larger, direct-call), or any ability to set price. Scale in ocean shipping is matched by rivals simply ordering ships.
C-43How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?F4027th of 31full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-44

How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

F5025th of 31

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: demonstrably not inferior, and leading on reliability when the network is not congested; but the surplus utility is valued only by a minority of shippers and cannot be monetised in a loose market.

  • Leading: Gemini about 90% schedule reliability in its first year against 50-60% for the industry (62% alliance average); Maersk number one for on-time arrival (experts); reliable schedules cut customers' safety stocks; strong digital tracking.
  • Not leading: reliability fell to about 68% in August-September 2026 under congestion; hub-and-spoke adds transshipment exposure (Tanjung Pelepas); MSC offers more direct calls and the Ocean Alliance more transpacific services (more than 40); value-conscious customers switch to MSC for non-priority cargo; mid-market customers reject complex integrated bundles.
  • Terminals: clearly superior utility to carriers (gateway access, productivity), shown by third-party carriers providing 63% of revenue and Hapag-Lloyd buying in.
C-45

To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

F2030th of 31

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility?

Judgement: demonstrable pricing power exists only in Terminals and is narrow; in Ocean there is cost-recovery power in a tight market and no pricing power of its own.

  • Ocean evidence: realised rates follow the indices at a beta of about 0.4-0.5 with a one-quarter lag in both directions (dampening, not power); surcharge recovery is formulaic on about 44% of contracts and weaker on spot; the reliability premium is unproven at scale; green premiums were refused.
  • Terminals evidence: revenue per move +6.4% (Q3) and +7.1% (Q2, to USD 385) against cost per move +5.7%; indexed concessions; margins held through 2016 and 2023.
  • Logistics: airfreight price increases passed through in Forwarding; Solutions margins compressed.

For the model: price contributes to revenue and margin in Terminals at about 1 point a year above cost inflation; in Ocean, price is the exogenous rate path minus pass-through.

C-46

To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

F3827th of 31

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto?

Judgement: not as a strategy or culture; Maersk retains its cost gains in tight markets and gives them up de facto, through competition, in loose ones.

  • Management's message is end-to-end value and reliability, not lower prices; Clerc frames capacity management "with a keen eye on the bottom line".
  • Gemini's USD 950m of benefits were kept in 2026 because rates were high; in 2027-2028 they will be competed away as the marginal carrier sets the price, so the edge shows up as a smaller loss rather than a lower price.
  • There is no Costco-style mechanism by which scale savings buy durable share; the share loss to MSC suggests the opposite.
C-47

To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

F3526th of 31

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: the mechanical forces work in both directions and net slightly against Maersk: a positive one in Terminals (concession indexation and capacity additions), a weaker positive in Gemini hub funnelling, and a negative one in Ocean share; Logistics improvement needs heroic integration effort.

  • Positive and mechanical: Terminals revenue per move compounds with indexation (+6-7% a year) and new capacity; Gemini routes more volume through owned hubs (+26% in Q1 2026); tightening carbon rules will, eventually, favour newer dual-fuel fleets.
  • Negative and mechanical: each period without new ships Maersk's capacity share falls as MSC and CMA CGM deliver; forwarder alienation reduces third-party volume in loose markets.
  • Heroic: Solutions whitespace, IT integration of acquired brands, the 6% Logistics margin target.
4b

Management quality

F4228th of 3112 answers
Judgement42C-5550C-5638C-5745C-57b50C-5865C-5935C-6050C-6150C-6245C-6355C-6430
Group judgement

Company - Management

F4228th of 31

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Management is a strong operator and a weak allocator, overseen by a controlling family that provides safety and distributions rather than capital discipline. Clerc's team acts fast and well in operations (Gemini, disruption handling, cost cuts, conservative guidance) and has built a capable logistics bench from outside, but it has a record of pro-cyclical capital decisions, missed strategic targets, a conflicted integrator strategy and incentives that do not reward per-share value. The family's 66% of votes means no outside pressure will change this. For the model: trust management's operational responses (cost cuts, slow steaming, charter redelivery) in a downturn, as in 2016 and 2023; do not assume counter-cyclical buybacks or asset sales; assume capex commitments are honoured; and expect formal guidance to be wide and low, with the narrative more optimistic than the guidance.

C-55

How big is the company's ambition or vision (a force: growth as a priority, outcompeting, innovating, adapting, pushing), particularly in relation to peers?

D5022nd of 31

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: ambition is large in reach (an end-to-end integrator, net zero by 2040, industry-leading reliability) and larger than its carrier peers', but it has been unevenly matched by delivery, and in Ocean it switched in 2026 from deliberate restraint to share defence.

  • Vision: "end-to-end" logistics with Ocean as the anchor; net zero ten years ahead of the industry, 35% absolute Scope 1 cut by 2030, 25% of Ocean cargo on green fuels by 2030; Gemini to "set a new and very high standard" for reliability.
  • Against peers: Hapag-Lloyd is a pure carrier with terminals ("Pure Play Plus"); MSC pursues pure scale; CMA CGM is integrated but serves forwarders; DSV pursues scale consolidation with a ruthless synergy culture and 2030 targets (conversion about 45%, pre-tax ROIC about 20%). Maersk's ambition is the broadest, but it is the only one whose ambition (integration) creates a channel conflict with its own customers.
  • Gap: the 10% organic growth and 6% margin Logistics targets have been missed for years; management admitted it is "not satisfied".
C-56

How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities (an enabler): widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

F3831st of 31

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities?

Judgement: a mixed strategy: Gemini and Terminals are right and bold and work across a range of environments; the integrator strategy is structurally conflicted and fragile in a downturn; the fleet order is pro-cyclical. Overall it does not widen the moat where most of the capital is.

  • Right: Gemini (hub-and-spoke, cost and reliability, robust in tight and loose markets); Terminals expansion in growing gateways (high-return, gravitational); dual-fuel optionality (LNG and methanol) instead of a methanol-only bet.
  • Conflicted: buying forwarders and selling end-to-end puts Maersk in competition with forwarders who provide up to half of Ocean volume; the benefit is uncertain, the cost is visible.
  • Pro-cyclical: 483,600 TEU ordered at the top of yard prices into the largest supply wave in the industry's history, after years of arguing share does not matter.
  • Dynamic? The balance sheet lets the strategy survive a range of environments, but the strategy itself does not capitalise on the coming one (a trough), except through the option to buy assets cheaply, which management has not signalled.
C-57

How great is the chief executive, judged by actions rather than conjecture (an enabler, not an ossifier)?

F4530th of 31

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: a strong operator and decisive network strategist, and a weak capital allocator; neither an ossifier nor a transformational chief executive.

  • Strong actions: dissolving the 2M Alliance and building Gemini quickly (USD 950m of benefits, about 90% reliability in year one); suspending the USD 3bn buyback in February 2024 to protect cash; handling the Red Sea and Hormuz disruptions (landbridges, surcharges recovered within weeks); 1,000 corporate roles cut; conservative formal guidance.
  • Weak actions: as head of Ocean & Logistics, more than USD 8bn of logistics acquisitions near peak multiples (LF Logistics, Pilot), still unintegrated; market share falling from about 20% to 13.7% under his leadership, then a share-defence order at peak prices; a vessel-life accounting change that props up reported EBIT; no open-market share purchases (equity only from vesting: 1,504 B shares on 1 April 2026, worth DKK 24.07m).
  • Communication: structured, but defensive on overcapacity and dismissive of share loss (C-60).

C-61

How strong is the company's record of acting, not just talking: a recent record of huge delivery and action, and a mid-term track record of delivery, not misses or justifications, whatever the environment (outperforming in tougher ones is even better), beyond its records against guidance and estimates?

F5024th of 31

The Investment Analyst's heading How strong is the company's record of acting, not just talking?

Judgement: a strong record of fast operational action in every environment and a weak record of meeting its own strategic targets; it delivers in operations, explains in strategy.

  • Delivered: Gemini on time and above its benefit target; Svitzer demerger (April 2024); cost programmes in 2016 and 2023 (about 4,000 and 10,000 roles); buyback suspension in 2024; disruption handling 2024-2026; Logistics margin up nine quarters in a row; FY2026 guidance never cut in-year under Clerc.
  • Missed: Logistics & Services 10% organic growth (7.2% in 2024) and 6% margin (4.8% in 2025; the STI scored this element at zero in 2025); ROIC above 7.5% (5.7% in 2025); integration of acquired brands; fleet discipline.

C-63

Explain, with the evidence, the extent to which the company puts shareholders first, ahead of management, and how prudent its governance is (scaffolding put in place even where it was not needed): what does the evidence show, and why?

C5510th of 31

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: shareholder-friendly in distributions and balance-sheet prudence, because the controlling family owns 41.5% of the capital and wants dividends and safety; weak in minority-protecting governance, because the same family controls 66% of the votes and the board's key committees. Shareholders come ahead of management, but the family's priorities (stability, long horizons, the integrator vision) come ahead of minority shareholders' priorities (returns on capital, timing).

  • Shareholders first: dividends of 30-50% of underlying profit; USD 10.9bn paid in early 2023; share count -27% (2019-2025); the Familiefond takes part pro rata in buybacks (no transfer of value from minorities); executive pay moderate and fell from USD 30m (2022) to 15m (2025) for the executive board; executives must hold shares worth 200% of base salary.
  • Prudence (scaffolding): net cash and BBB+ kept through the cycle; buyback suspended in 2024 before cash was needed; strong compliance (70-plus professionals, whistleblowing since 2011); an Audit Committee of three independent directors.
  • Weaknesses: the Chair is CEO of the controlling holding; nomination and remuneration committees have low independence; PwC has audited for fifteen years; individual board shareholdings are not disclosed; no open-market insider buying; the vessel-life change gives management discretion over reported profit; pay has no EPS or TSR element (relative TSR dropped from the 2025-2027 plan).
C-64

To what extent is the company an enlightened capital allocator and a dynamic repurchaser of its shares, not just an operator or generator, with a record of allocating capital at a high return on new invested capital?

F3027th of 31

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent is the company an enlightened capital allocator and a dynamic repurchaser of its shares, with a record of allocating capital at a high return on new invested capital?

Judgement: not an enlightened allocator; it allocates pro-cyclically, buys back and pays out at peaks and stops at troughs, and has earned a high return on new capital only in Terminals.

  • Pro-cyclical: logistics acquisitions near peak multiples (2021-2023); a fleet order at peak yard prices (2026); USD 10.9bn dividend at the 2023 peak; buyback suspended in February 2024 near a share-price trough (about DKK 10,000-12,000) rather than increased.
  • Repurchases: large in total (share count -27%) but not counter-cyclical; now 1bn a year with expansion deferred.
  • High return: Terminals concessions and expansions (ROIC about 14-16%); Gemini (low capital, high return).
  • The opportunity management has not claimed: a trough in 2027-2028 with net cash would allow counter-cyclical buybacks or asset purchases; nothing in the record suggests it will.

Group judgement

Company - Economic strength

F1831st of 31

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Maersk's economic strength is a strong balance sheet wrapped around weak, extremely geared and lower-quality earnings power. EPS growth in FY2026 is a disruption peak; returns on capital are below the cost of capital through the cycle outside Terminals; free cash flow after leases is barely positive in a peak year and negative in the trough; margins are at their peak and heading to the lowest since 2016; and the trough earnings base is flattered by an accounting change, decaying interest income and a hidden tax floor that together are worth about USD 60 a share in FY2028 (memo). The balance sheet (net cash excluding leases about USD 15.5bn, BBB+) ensures survival and continued investment, not returns. For the model: build EPS from the bottom up below EBIT (vessel-life benefit, net interest on a falling cash balance, fixed lease interest, a tax charge of about 0.3-0.45bn, minorities about 0.1bn, share count about 14.3m with buybacks only if announced), deduct lease repayments and a working-capital drain of about 15-17% of any revenue decline from free cash flow, and hold capex at 5-6bn a year.

C-50

How much immediate scope is there to deploy capital or operating spend at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what does that mean for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple?

F2831st of 31

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: there is real high-return scope only in Terminals, perhaps USD 0.5-1bn a year; most of the group's capital (5-6bn a year) is going into Ocean ships whose return on new capital will probably sit below the cost of capital through 2030. This justifies a low through-cycle multiple on the group and a higher one on Terminals.

  • Terminals: the 3bn expansion and new concessions earn about 13-16% against a 9% hurdle.
  • Ocean: 483,600 TEU ordered at yard prices about 46% above pre-COVID, to deliver into the 2029-2030 market after a supply wave; green capex is compliance spending, not growth spending.
  • Logistics: warehouses dilute Solutions margins until filled; acquisitions since 2019 (8bn) earn below WACC.
  • Fair multiple: Ocean's through-cycle ROIC below WACC means Ocean deserves to trade at or below book over the cycle (0.4-0.6x in troughs, 0.8-1.0x at peaks); Terminals deserves an infrastructure multiple (the memo uses 10x EBITDA, about USD 20bn). The sum of the parts with Ocean at nil gives about DKK 14,000-15,700 a share (memo).

How high are the returns on capital: post-tax return on new invested capital, on capital employed and on tangible capital?

  • Segments: Terminals ROIC 13.8% (last twelve months to Q3 2026), 15.7% (Q1 2026), 16.1% (2025); Logistics & Services below WACC since 2021 (Morgan Stanley: must double EBIT to cover its capital cost); Ocean below target through the cycle.

C-52

How high is the conversion of net income into free cash flow, and how do net working capital and similar items behave?

F2031st of 31

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: on the company's own definition (after leases) free cash flow is barely positive even in a peak year and turns negative in FY2027-FY2028; FactSet and consensus free-cash-flow figures overstate it because they ignore lease repayments, and they probably ignore the working-capital drain that a revenue decline triggers.

  • The lease gap: lease repayments of about USD 3.5-3.9bn a year (Q1 2026: 1.18bn plus 176m interest). Consensus FactSet free cash flow of 4.65 / 4.18 / 0.48bn (FY2026-FY2028) becomes roughly +1.0 / +0.4 / -3.3bn after leases on a crude deduction; the memo has company-basis free cash flow of +3.5 / -1.0 / -2.7 / -2.2 / -0.6bn (FY2026-FY2030); FY2026 guidance is above 3.0bn.
  • Nine months of 2026: free cash flow -874m (Q1), +549m (Q2), +2,389m (Q3); cash conversion (operating cash flow to EBITDA) 59% (Q1), 75% (Q2), 69% for H1 against 92% a year earlier, because of bunker inventories and receivables.
  • Working capital insight: net working capital is structurally negative (about -USD 10.0bn at Q3 2026, -16.6% of last-twelve-month revenue), because payables exceed receivables plus inventories. When revenue falls, payables fall faster than receivables are collected, so a revenue decline drains cash: a fall of USD 5-11bn of revenue (consensus to memo for FY2027) implies an outflow of roughly 0.8-1.8bn at the current ratio. The FY2024 swing (receivables outflow of 754m) and Q1 2026 (-913m) show the size of these movements.
  • History: FactSet free cash flow over reported net income 114% (FY2024), 131% (FY2025), but these lines do not reconcile with FactSet's own operating cash flow and capex and exclude leases.

C-54

How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

F2828th of 31

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: the headline gap is small, but the earnings base is flattered in ways that matter most at the trough: an accounting depreciation change, interest income that will decay, and a tax charge that does not fall with profit. Together they decide the sign of trough EPS.

  • Vessel-life change: about USD 700m a year of lower depreciation from FY2026 (USD 176m in Q1, 175m in Q2): about 9% of FY2026 consensus EBIT, about 25% of FY2027 and about 90% of FY2028. It does not change cash.
  • Tax floor: tonnage tax keeps the rate low, but the absolute charge has been about USD 0.3-0.45bn a year whatever profit does (FY2023: about 0.32bn on pre-tax 3.8bn; FY2024: about 0.45bn on 6.3bn; FY2025: about 0.42bn on 2.8bn), because Terminals and Logistics profits are taxed normally while Ocean losses get little relief. At a pre-tax profit near zero the effective rate becomes very high. Consensus FY2028 implies tax and minorities of about 0.4bn on pre-tax 1.23bn, so some brokers model this; the memo has tax at about USD 28 a share in FY2028.
  • Interest: net cash excluding leases of about USD 15.5bn earns interest (about 40m pre-tax per 1bn); it falls to about 6-7bn by 2029 on the memo's numbers as capex, leases and dividends consume it. Lease interest (about 0.7bn a year) does not fall. Consensus FY2028 pre-tax profit (1,228m) is above consensus EBIT (786m), implying positive net financial income of about 0.44bn, which looks inconsistent with a declining cash pile; part may be a broker-composition artefact, but it is the line most likely to be revised down.
  • Other flattering items: congestion storage income in Terminals (transitory); restructuring costs excluded from "underlying" (USD 77m in Q1 2026; about 200m for the overhead programme); FX derivative trading gains of USD 510m in 2025 (in financial items).
  • Bases differ: FY2025 EPS 181.6 (brokers), 179.0 (reported basic), 149.3 (FactSet diluted before unusual items), 155.5 (Viewforth definition).
  • Impairment: an Ocean impairment (headroom about 1.1bn) would sit outside underlying EPS but cut book value, the anchor for valuation.
C-76

How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?

D6219th of 31

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: there is no financial gearing to amplify EPS, and balance-sheet comfort is very high; but the cash pile turns from an earnings support into a slow drag as it is spent, and leases are a fixed claim.

  • Net cash excluding leases about USD 15.5bn (end-2025), 13.7bn (Q2 2026, company); net cash including leases 3.2bn (Q3, company); FactSet net debt to EBITDA -0.24x; BBB+ (S&P) and Baa1 (Moody's); liquidity 18-21bn; USD 865m held in countries with currency controls.
  • Path (memo): net cash excluding leases 16.7bn (FY2026) falling to 12.6, 9.1, 6.7 and 5.9bn (FY2027-FY2030) as capex of 5-6bn, leases of 3.5-3.9bn and the FY2026 dividend (about 2.8bn) exceed operating cash flow.
  • Amplification: each 1bn of cash spent costs about 40m of pre-tax income; the end of buybacks removes about 2% a year of EPS accretion; lease interest about 0.7bn a year is fixed.
  • Risk: insolvency talk (Bernstein's "structural insolvency risk" in a full Suez return) is not credible with this balance sheet; even the memo's deeper case keeps net cash excluding leases near zero by 2029, not negative.

P-04

After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

F1522nd of 23

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: price will take about 12.8bn out of Ocean revenue between FY2026 and FY2028 and about 10 points out of the group EBITDA margin; it is the whole of the revenue decline and about 160% of the 7.9bn EBITDA decline, before the cost relief puts part of it back. Maersk has no pricing power of its own in Ocean, so price is the market's: it transmits within about a quarter, with only a one-to-two-quarter cushion from contracts. Real pricing power exists only in Terminals and adds about 0.1bn a year, and even there the 2016 record says to hold it flat in the first year of an overcapacity episode.

How price is transmitted to Maersk's realised rate.

  • Beta and lag. The realised loaded rate follows the spot indices at a beta of about 0.4-0.5 with a lag of about one quarter (Q1 2026 -14% year on year against Drewry -32%; Q2 2026 +32% quarter on quarter against SCFI +55%); Bernstein measures a 74.5% correlation with the CCFI. North-South lanes (31.6% of volume in 2022) can decouple realised rates from Chinese export indices, as in late 2016.
  • The contract cushion is short. Long-term contracts (over three months) were 47% of loaded volume in Q3 2026 (46% for FY2026); average contract duration has compressed from 25 months (2021-2022) toward the pre-2020 average of 7.5 months; about 20% of large shippers have moved from annual to quarterly bids (expert, March 2026); contracts are "may-haul" and are renegotiated when spot falls below them. Hapag-Lloyd's larger contract share shows what a real cushion looks like (2023: about 1,500 per TEU against Maersk's modelled 1,240; 2024: flat against ZIM's +57%); Maersk sits between Hapag-Lloyd and ZIM, so its rate falls faster than its Gemini partner's.
  • No reliability premium. Gemini's reliability (about 90% in its first year) fell to 68.4% in August-September 2026, below MSC's 68.9% (Sea-Intelligence); Carnegie's 2019-2024 analysis finds rates highest when reliability is lowest; the inventory saving of two to three days is worth about USD 40 a box. Green premiums were refused outside a few contracts (Epson's ECO Delivery).
  • Surcharges are pass-through. Emergency bunker surcharges (USD 200 per TEU in March 2026, matched by CMA CGM and MSC), war-risk fees and the Fossil Fuel Fee recovered about 500m a month of conflict costs in early 2026; recovery is about 80-90% in a tight market and about 70% in a loose one; the US Federal Maritime Commission blocked fast-tracked surcharges in April 2026. In 2027 they leave revenue with their costs.

The price path and its contribution (base).

FY2025 FY2026E FY2027E FY2028E FY2029E FY2030E
Average loaded rate (USD/FFE) about 2,240 about 2,840 about 2,150 1,920 1,960 2,060
Change +27% -24% -11% +2% +5%
Ocean freight revenue (bn) about 29 37.7 29.3 26.7 27.8 30.3
Price effect on group revenue (bn, against prior year) +7.9 -9.3 -3.3 +0.6 +1.5
  • Quarterly shape. Q4 2026 about 3,200 (implied by the guidance range of 14.0-14.5bn; Asia-Europe spot falling from October, SCFI Europe -16.8% month on month to 5 October); Q1 2027 2,600 (Asia-Europe contracts signed December-February against falling spot, Chinese New Year, the Suez Canal Authority expecting all Maersk services back by March 2027); Q2 2,200 (transpacific contracts in May; 2027 deliveries); Q3 1,975; Q4 1,875; Q1 2028 1,880. Q1 2027 is still about +25% year on year on the depressed Q1 2026 base (2,081); Q2 2027 is the first year-on-year fall (-20%).
  • Contribution to margin. Price on its own would take FY2028 EBITDA from 14.15bn to about 1.9bn (a margin near 3%); the cost relief of about 290 per FFE on the full-year average (about 400 from the peak quarter) puts back about 4.0bn, volume about 0.5bn and the non-Ocean growth about 0.35bn, giving 6.3bn and 11.6%. Each 100 per FFE is about 2.6 points of group EBITDA margin at FY2028 revenue.
  • The 2027 contract season. The signals conflict: COSCO expects 2027 contracts to rise, Morgan Stanley says strong spot could support renewals, Xeneta (11 November 2026) expects spot to soften in 2027 and Morgan Stanley (October) sees Suez transits already pushing Asia-Europe spot down. I assume Asia-Europe 2027 contracts settle well below the 2026 average, which is already in my Q1-Q2 2027 rates; a settlement near 2026 levels is the main way H1 2027 beats my base (about +2.3bn of FY2027 EBITDA if Q1 realises 3,000 and Q2 2,500).
  • Terminals. Indexed concessions (CPI-linked base tariffs, the right to pass through labour and levies) gave revenue per move +6.4% against cost per move +5.7% in Q3 2026 (revenue per move USD 385 in Q2). That is real but narrow: about 0.5-1 point a year of spread, about 0.1bn of EBITDA. In the 2016 overcapacity APM Terminals' EBIT per TEU fell about 33% (Jefferies), and storage income from congestion is fading now; ICTSI shows tariff rises are limited by concession terms in many gateways.
  • Logistics. Forwarding passes freight rates through (lower revenue, similar gross profit); Solutions is price-competitive with whitespace drag. Price adds nothing to L&S margin.

Where I differ from the first set. The first set's model guidance has Terminals EBITDA flat in FY2027 and then +5-7% a year on about 1 point a year of real price. I agree with flat FY2027 (2.15bn) and growth thereafter (2.27bn, 2.40bn, 2.53bn), but I would not call Terminals pricing power proven in an overcapacity period: the 2016 precedent (EBIT per TEU -33%) and the fading congestion storage income argue that the risk to Terminals in FY2027-FY2028 is to the downside of my numbers, not the upside. A 10% fall in Terminals EBITDA would cost about 0.2bn, small against the Ocean rate.

For the model: price is the exogenous rate path above; volume +2-3.5% a year; revenue growth of +19.3% (FY2026), -13.1%, -3.6%, +4.1%, +6.6%.

5b

Operating leverage

2 answers
C-7515P-0112
C-75

How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage: how much does a given change in revenue move operating profit, and how wide a range of outcomes does that create?

F1531st of 31

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: extreme, because the Ocean cost base is nearly fixed per sailing; base-rate changes drop through to EBIT at close to 100%, and the resulting range of outcomes is wider than the company's market value can absorb in either direction.

  • Cost structure (Ocean, Q1 2026, operating cost USD 7.0bn a quarter): terminal handling 39% (variable per box), network cost excluding bunker 27% (fixed per sailing), bunker 19% (semi-variable, partly passed through), SG&A 9%; plus group depreciation of about USD 6.3-7.0bn a year.
  • Observed drop-through: from Q1 to Q3 2026 Ocean revenue rose about USD 4.2bn (about 8.35bn to 12.58bn) and Ocean EBIT about 2.8bn (-192m to +2,591m): about 66% drop-through, including fuel-surcharge revenue that carries its own cost; on the base rate alone, drop-through is close to 100%.
  • Constants: 1% on the Ocean rate is about USD 0.35bn of EBIT; USD 100 per FFE about 1.3-1.36bn; 5% on rates about 1.7bn of EBITDA (Morgan Stanley).
  • Consensus leverage: FY2027 revenue -8% with EBIT -66%; FY2028 revenue -1% with EBIT -71%. The memo: FY2027 revenue -17%, EBITDA -40%, EBIT -75%, EPS -77%.
  • Range: FY2027 EBITDA cone about USD 3.7-14.5bn around a base of 8.4bn (memo); brokers' FY2027 EPS from about 60 to 451 and FY2028 EPS from -394 to +282, FY2028 EBITDA from 2.2bn to 10.1bn (FactSet broker sheet).

For the model: build Ocean EBIT as (realised rate × volume) minus cost per FFE that falls only with bunker, congestion relief and self-help; do not model Ocean EBIT as a margin on revenue.

P-01

After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

F1223rd of 23

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: operating leverage will cut EBITDA by about 42% in FY2027 on a revenue fall of about 13%, and keep the EBITDA margin at 11.6-12.0% in FY2028-FY2029, below every year since at least FY2019; the leverage is price leverage on a cost base that does fall, but only by about a third as much as the rate, so the base case sits almost exactly at group EBIT break-even in FY2028 and the sign of earnings per share is then decided below EBIT.

The mechanism, rebuilt from the reported quarters. Ocean EBITDA per loaded FFE equals the average loaded rate less a cash cost per FFE (bunker plus network, handling and overhead, net of Ocean's other revenue). I calibrated that cash cost on actual quarters:

Quarter Loaded rate (USD/FFE) Ocean EBITDA per FFE Implied cash cost per FFE Of which bunker per FFE (approx.)
Q2 2025 2,259 447 1,812 about 490
FY2025 (approx.) about 2,240 about 470 about 1,770 about 520
Q1 2026 2,081 about 280 about 1,800 424
Q2 2026 2,746 607 2,139 about 700 (bunker +44%)
Q3 2026 3,287 1,094 2,193 621 (plus congestion: handling +9.3%, network ex-bunker +12%)
  • The cost line is not fixed; it is inflated today and will deflate. The 2026 cost rise came from three exogenous sources (bunker +25-44%, Cape routing, congestion and Hormuz contingencies), not from the base. As Suez reopens (shorter voyages, about 8-10% less fuel per FFE), congestion clears and bunker eases with oil, I take the cash cost per FFE from about 2,190 (Q3 2026) to about 1,950 (Q1 2027), 1,800 (Q3 2027) and 1,770 (FY2028), then 1,795-1,800 (FY2029-FY2030) as inflation (handling tariffs +5-6% a year, labour, EU ETS about +0.3bn by 2027, Suez tolls back in the cost base) outruns self-help. That is a fall of about 400 per FFE (about 5.5bn a year) from the peak-quarter cost.
  • But the rate falls about three times as far. Base loaded rate: Q4 2026 3,200; Q1 2027 2,600; Q2 2,200; Q3 1,975; Q4 1,875; FY2027 about 2,150; FY2028 1,920; FY2029 1,960; FY2030 2,060. From Q3 2026 to FY2028 the rate falls about 1,370 per FFE against a cost fall of about 420. Ocean EBITDA goes from 10.3bn (FY2026) to 4.3bn (FY2027) and 2.1bn (FY2028), about 150 per FFE, which is where Maersk's Ocean sat in the 2023 trough (Q4 2023 group EBITDA 839m at a rate of 1,925 and unit cost at fixed bunker of about 2,290) and what the memo assumed (about 165).
  • Precedent for the ratio. In 2023 Ocean unit cost at fixed bunker fell from 2,552 (Q1) to 2,389 (Q2) and 2,287 (Q3), about 10% in two quarters, while the rate fell from the mid-2,000s to 1,925 by Q4; in 2015-2016 unit cost fell 13-16% a year but two-thirds of that was bunker. Cost follows the rate down with a lag and at roughly a third of its size. Because bunker is about 20% of the rate and is largely passed through (the Fossil Fuel Fee), a fall in oil lowers revenue and cost together and is close to EBITDA-neutral (net sensitivity about 0.25bn per USD 100 a tonne).

Break-evens that decide the line items. Ocean EBITDA breaks even at a rate of about 1,770-1,800; Ocean EBIT (Ocean D&A about 4.4bn after the vessel-life benefit, about 320 per FFE) at about 2,100-2,150; group company-basis EBIT (adding the non-Ocean floor of about 2.3-2.9bn of EBIT) at about 1,910. My FY2028 rate of 1,920 is ten dollars above group break-even: a 150-200 per FFE band, narrower than the realised rate moved in each of the last six quarters, decides whether FY2028 company-basis EBIT is +2bn or -2bn.

How it shapes the line items against the last six years (my base; FY2023-FY2025 on the brokers' basis):

USDm FY2019 FY2023 FY2024 FY2025 FY2026E FY2027E FY2028E FY2029E FY2030E
Revenue 38,885 51,065 55,482 53,988 64,390 55,930 53,940 56,130 59,830
Revenue growth -1.0% -37.4% +8.7% -2.7% +19.3% -13.1% -3.6% +4.1% +6.6%
EBITDA 5,830 9,591 12,128 9,572 14,150 8,170 6,260 6,710 8,440
EBITDA margin 15.0% 18.8% 21.9% 17.7% 22.0% 14.6% 11.6% 12.0% 14.1%
D&A -4,258 -6,238 -6,178 -6,711 -6,300 -6,450 -6,550 -6,700 -6,900
Company-basis EBIT (incl. JVs) 8,280 2,140 150 470 2,020
  • Drop-through is about 70% each way, not 100%. FY2026 to FY2027 Ocean revenue falls about 8.9bn and Ocean EBITDA about 6.1bn (69%); from Q1 to Q3 2026 the rise was 66%. The base rate drops through at close to 100% at the margin; the fuel and surcharge pass-through and the deflating congestion cost are what bring the average down.
  • Volume is not leverage here. 100k FFE is about 10m of EBIT. I carry loaded volume of 13.3m (FY2026), 13.6m (+2.5%, as the Suez return frees Maersk's own capacity from the 96% utilisation cap of Q3 2026 and Maersk prices to fill it, as it did in Q1 2026 when volume rose 9.3% on a rate down 14%), 13.9m, 14.2m and 14.7m (FY2030, own newbuilds arriving).
  • Positive leverage exists only outside Ocean and is small. Terminals revenue per move rose 6.4% against cost per move 5.7% (Q3 2026): EBITDA 2.13bn (FY2026) to 2.53bn (FY2030). Logistics & Services EBITDA 1.89bn to 2.24bn on revenue of 17.1bn to 19.4bn (EBIT margin 5.5-5.8%, below the 6% target). Together they add about 0.8bn over four years against an Ocean swing of 8.2bn.
  • D&A is fixed and drifts up (new leases from the 9.3bn committed but not started, the 26-ship order from 2029, Terminals capex), from 6.3bn to 6.9bn, so the EBIT margin falls further than the EBITDA margin: company-basis EBIT margin 12.9% (FY2026), 3.8%, 0.3%, 0.8%, 3.4%, against 6.0-10.5% in FY2023-FY2024.

Width of the outcome range created by the leverage. FY2027 EBITDA: about 4.7bn (deeper), 8.2bn (base), 13.7bn (disruption-extended); FY2028: about 3.1bn, 6.3bn, 9.2bn. The scenario-weighted FY2027 EBITDA (8.2bn) is close to the base; the weighted FY2028 EBITDA (5.9bn) is below it, because the deeper case is more probable than the upside case and the upside case fades by 2028.

Where I differ from the first set. (1) The first set says base-rate changes drop through at close to 100% and asks the model to treat the cost per FFE as falling "only with bunker, congestion relief and self-help". I agree with the method but the quantity matters: those three sources together are worth about 400 per FFE (about 5.5bn a year) between Q3 2026 and FY2028, a third of the rate fall, so the model should not carry the Q3 2026 cost level into 2027. (2) I agree with the memo's trough Ocean EBITDA (about 150-165 per FFE in FY2028) and test it against Maersk's own 2023 trough, which sat at or below that level for two quarters. (3) My FY2029 EBITDA (6.7bn) is below the memo's (7.15bn) because I do not have the rate recovering in 2029 (P-05).

For the model: build Ocean EBITDA as volume times (rate minus a cash cost per FFE of about 1,950 in Q1 2027 falling to about 1,770 in FY2028); do not model it as a margin on revenue; hold D&A between 6.3bn and 6.9bn.

Financial leverage

C-76How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?D6219th of 31full answer under 4c Financial profile
P-02

After assessing the financial leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years?

F4022nd of 23

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: there is no financial gearing to amplify earnings, but the balance sheet turns from an earnings support into an earnings drag: net cash excluding leases peaks at about 17.5bn at end-2026 and falls to about 3.6bn by end-2030, which moves net financial items from about -0.14bn to about -0.77bn, a swing of about USD 45 a share that deepens and prolongs the EPS trough; solvency and the rating are not at risk in the base case, and the buyback ends after 2027, so the share count stops falling for the first time since 2019.

The cash path (company basis, USDbn).

End-2025 End-2026E End-2027E End-2028E End-2029E End-2030E
Net cash excluding leases 15.5 17.5 12.7 8.5 5.0 3.6
Lease liabilities (about) 12.2 12.6 12.6 12.5 12.5 12.5
Net debt including leases -3.3 -4.9 -0.1 4.0 7.5 8.9
Net debt incl. leases / EBITDA -0.35x 0.0x 0.6x 1.1x 1.05x
  • What consumes the cash. Capex of 4.7bn (FY2026), 5.8bn, 5.8bn, 5.7bn, 5.2bn (2026-2027 within the company's 10-11bn guidance; the 26-ship order and the 3bn Terminals programme fixed thereafter); lease principal of 3.4-3.6bn a year plus lease interest of about 0.73-0.75bn; the FY2026 dividend of about 3.0bn paid in March 2027; a 1bn buyback in 2027. Against an EBITDA of 6.3-8.4bn from FY2027, capex plus lease cash (9.0-9.4bn a year) absorbs more than the whole of EBITDA in FY2028-FY2029.
  • What it does to net financial items. Interest income on gross cash of 18-21bn earns about 0.75bn in FY2026 (financial income received 556m in nine months; FY2025 1,006m); at 3.3-3.6% on a falling balance it drops to 0.73bn, 0.54bn, 0.40bn and 0.32bn. Lease interest (531m in nine months of 2026, about 0.71bn a year) does not fall, because new leases replace expiring ones; interest on about 5.3bn of borrowings (about 0.26-0.34bn) rises a little if debt funds the newbuild deliveries. Net financial items before joint ventures and minorities: -0.14bn (FY2026), -0.27bn, -0.48bn, -0.65bn, -0.77bn.
  • What it does to EPS. USD -10 a share in FY2026, -19, -34, -47 and -55 in FY2030. Each 1bn of cash spent costs about 35-40m of pre-tax income. In FY2029 the interest drag alone is about the size of the whole loss per share (-51).
  • The share count stops working for EPS. Outstanding shares fell from 20.1m (FY2019) to 14.7m (FY2025), about 5% a year of EPS accretion. Today: 14.70m nominal after the June 2026 cancellation (9.59m A, 5.11m B) less 0.297m treasury = 14.40m at 30 September 2026; about 14.36m at end-2026 as the DKK 6.3bn programme finishes on 29 January 2027; about 14.0m at end-2027 after a new 1bn programme; flat thereafter (vesting adds about 0.01m a year). Accretion falls from about 5% a year to about 2.5% in FY2027 and nil from FY2028.
  • Book value and the valuation anchor. Book value per share about USD 4,260 at end-2026 (consensus 4,270), about 4,185 (end-2027), 4,090 (end-2028), 4,040 and 4,080, before any Ocean impairment; equity falls because dividends and buybacks exceed earnings in FY2027 and losses follow. An impairment of 2-4bn (Ocean headroom was 1.1bn at end-2025, down from 4.1bn, with a 9.8% pre-tax discount rate) would take a further USD 150-280 a share off the price-to-book anchor while lowering D&A by about 0.15-0.25bn a year.
  • Rating and solvency. BBB+ (S&P) and Baa1 (Moody's) sit comfortably with net debt including leases at about 1x EBITDA in the base case. In the deeper case net cash excluding leases falls to about 4bn by end-2028 and turns into net debt of about 5-6bn by end-2030 (net debt including leases about 3x the deeper-case EBITDA); the rating would then come under pressure and management would cut Terminals capex and buybacks first, as in 2016 and 2024. Insolvency talk remains not credible.
  • Peers. Hapag-Lloyd, COSCO and Evergreen hold similar net cash and earn similar deposit yields (COSCO 0.47-6.85% on time deposits; Evergreen interest income about 4% of pre-tax profit in 2025); ZIM is the leveraged exception (lease liabilities 4.19bn, 7.8% average interest on indebtedness). Maersk's lease rate (3.94% effective in its UK subsidiary) is low, so the drag comes from the falling cash, not from expensive debt.

Where I differ from the first set. (1) Buyback. The first set's base is no new buyback (about 45% for some programme). The closest precedent argues the other way: in February 2026, guiding an underlying EBIT loss of 1.0-1.5bn and free cash flow of about -1.5bn, management still launched a 1bn programme; in February 2027 it will hold more net cash (about 17.5bn against about 15.5bn), has "modelled" a 2bn programme and has a new CFO. Only the February 2024 suspension (EBITDA guidance 1-6bn, free cash flow guided to about -6bn) argues against. I put some programme at about 60%, with 1bn the modal size, and carry 1bn in FY2027 and nothing after the January 2028 tail. The EPS effect is small (about +2.5%); the cash effect is 1bn. (2) Speed of the cash decline. My net cash excluding leases falls faster than the memo's after FY2027 (5.0bn against 6.7bn at end-2029) because I hold capex at 5.7-5.8bn in FY2028-FY2029 rather than the memo's 4.8-5.6bn, partly offset by the working-capital release (P-08).

For the model: net financial items before joint ventures of about -0.14bn, -0.27bn, -0.48bn, -0.65bn, -0.77bn; shares of about 14.5m (FY2026 average), 14.2m, then flat at about 14.0m; no buyback after January 2028 unless announced.

How much of each major part of the cost base is cuttable or reducible?

F3529th of 31

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: perhaps USD 0.8bn of EBITDA is realistically reducible by FY2028 (1.1-1.3bn if losses force the 2016/2023 playbook), about a sixth of the base-rate erosion; much of the cost base has been locked in at peak prices.

Cost line Cuttable? Evidence
Terminal handling (39% of Ocean cost) Variable with volume, not per box; falls as congestion clears Q3 unit cost ex-fuel +5% on congestion
Network cost excluding bunker (27%) Partly: blank sailings, idling, charter redelivery as contracts expire, Seaspan retrofits 2016 and 2023 playbooks; 416 chartered vessels; but charters lengthened (to 2031 at 41,000 a day) and about 9.3bn of leases not yet started

P-03

After assessing the scope for cost elimination and efficiency in greater detail, how much is likely to reach the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, and when?

F3522nd of 23

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: management-controlled cost actions will add about 0.6-0.7bn to EBITDA by FY2027 and about 1.0bn by FY2028, roughly an eighth of the base-rate erosion; a further, larger relief of about 4-5bn a year (congestion unwinding, the Suez return, lower bunker) is exogenous and is mostly handed back to customers through rates. The big management actions arrive after the losses do, so they shape FY2028-FY2029, not FY2027.

What is cuttable, how much, and when (EBITDA effect against no action):

Source FY2027 FY2028 Timing logic and evidence
Corporate overhead (about 1,000 roles, 15% of corporate functions) 0.15bn 0.18bn Announced February 2026; 77m restructuring cost in Q1 2026; Bernstein about 180m a year; largely in the run-rate by mid-2027
Ocean network actions (blank sailings, idling, charter redelivery as contracts expire, slow steaming, Seaspan-type retrofits) 0.3bn 0.55bn Triggered by losses, as in 2016 (idling, cancelled sailings late 2015) and 2023 (network cost about -14%); Maersk can redeliver 10-15% of its 416 chartered ships a year (DAOL), but charters have lengthened (Costamare ships to October 2029 and May 2030; Maersk Puelo to September 2031)
Solutions warehouse whitespace 0.05bn 0.12bn Solutions EBIT margin 3.2% in Q3 2026 (-170bp year on year); filling new sites under the new Kuehne + Nagel-lineage head
Gemini hub synergies in Terminals 0.05bn 0.1bn 120-200m a year of terminal benefits at full implementation; mostly banked
A 2023-type group programme 0 0 to 0.4bn Base: not announced until FY2027 losses are visible; the 2023 programme (workforce from 110,000 to below 100,000, 600m G&A savings for 2024, 350m restructuring budget) reached 6,500 roles within months once announced
Total about 0.55-0.65bn about 0.95-1.35bn
  • What is not cuttable. Lease commitments of 17.8bn undiscounted at end-2025 (3.7bn within a year, 7.2bn in one to five years, 6.9bn beyond five years), plus about 9.3bn of leases committed but not started; 5.9bn of euro-indexed green-fuel offtakes; EU ETS (Maersk's cost 196m in 2025 rising to about 491m by 2027, recoverable by surcharge only in tight markets); depreciation (already cut 0.7bn by the vessel-life change); terminal handling (39% of Ocean cost) is variable per box but inflating (APM Terminals cost per move +5.3% to USD 293 in Q2 2026).
  • The exogenous relief is larger than the self-help but is not margin. The congestion cost (Q3 2026 unit cost at constant fuel +5%; handling +9.3%; network ex-bunker +12%), the Cape routing cost (more ships and fuel on Asia-Europe loops) and bunker (Q3 2026 bunker cost 2.1bn, +37%) together are about 400 per FFE, about 5.5bn a year. They unwind with the same events (Suez, falling congestion, peace-lower oil) that cut the rate, and surcharges that recovered them leave revenue at the same time. In my build they sit inside the falling cash cost per FFE (P-01); they cushion Ocean EBITDA by about a third of the rate fall.
  • Net of inflation, the self-help only holds the line. Handling tariffs, labour (about 7%), ETS and Suez tolls add about 3% a year to non-bunker cost; the 1.0bn of self-help by FY2028 roughly offsets that inflation, so Ocean non-bunker cost per FFE in FY2028 ends close to its FY2025 level in real terms and slightly above it in nominal terms.
  • Peers. Hapag-Lloyd's programme targeted more than 1bn over 18 months to 2026; ZIM redelivered 22 chartered ships on expiry; DSV and Kuehne + Nagel can flex costs immediately because they own no ships. Maersk's record of delivering cost programmes is good (Hamburg Süd integration and the Transport & Logistics programme delivered 1.2bn against a 600m target by 2019; Gemini delivered 950m against 820m-1.1bn), so the risk is in timing and size, not execution.
  • Cash cost of the savings. Restructuring of about 0.2-0.35bn (excluded from underlying EBITDA, but a cash outflow in free cash flow) falls in FY2027-FY2028 if the larger programme is launched.

When it reaches the line items. FY2027: about 0.6bn in EBITDA, almost all of it overhead and early network actions, invisible against a 6bn fall. FY2028: about 1.0bn, the year the playbook is fully running. FY2029-FY2030: held, and offset by inflation. In the deeper case the 2023 programme would be launched in H2 2027 and add 0.3-0.4bn more by FY2028 (total 1.3-1.5bn), which is the main reason the deeper case's FY2028 EBITDA does not fall below about 3bn.

Where I differ from the first set. The first set carries about 0.5-0.8bn by FY2028 in the base and 1.1-1.3bn only in a deeper case. I carry about 1.0bn in the base, because in my base Ocean EBIT is negative from Q3 2027, which on Maersk's own record (2016, 2023) is the trigger for the larger programme; the 2023 programme's 600m of G&A savings arrived within about a year of announcement. The difference is about 0.2-0.3bn of FY2028 EBITDA (about USD 15-20 a share).

For the model: the self-help is inside the EBITDA margin inputs (about 0.6bn in FY2027, 1.0bn from FY2028); do not add it again; cash restructuring of about 0.1bn a year sits in my free cash flow cross-check.

5e

Reinforcing feedback loops

C-4735P-0512
C-47To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?F3526th of 31full answer under 4a Business quality
P-05

After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, such that dynamics at play today create additional scope for further positive/negative dynamics in the near/mid-term (reinforcing feedback loops), or the opposite (balancing loops), how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

F1223rd of 23

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

The Investment Analyst's heading After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement: reinforcing loops dominate until at least 2028 and make the trough long rather than sharp: two loss years (FY2028-FY2029) rather than one, EBITDA margins of 11.6-12.0% for two years, and free cash flow of about -3.3bn to -3.5bn in each. The balancing loops (idling, slow steaming, scrapping) are real but are triggered only by widespread losses, which arrive in H2 2027 and engage in 2028-2029; the recovery therefore comes in FY2030 and depends on scrapping, not on falling deliveries.

The loops, ranked by their effect on the line items in 2027-2029.

Loop Type Evidence now (dated) Effect on the line items
Profit to orderbook to deliveries Reinforcing (negative) Orderbook 44% of the fleet, 14.9m TEU (Jefferies, November 2026): 3.5m TEU in 2027 and 5.5m in 2028; MSC 2.91m TEU on order; COSCO 52% of its fleet; Maersk 483,600 TEU for 2029-2030; orderbook +1.6m TEU in Q3 2026 alone Sets the rate path; the roughly 5.9m TEU still due after 2028 keeps supply growth above demand in 2029
Cash buffer delays scrapping Reinforcing Industry net cash 26-30bn; scrapping 5k TEU (2025), about 41k TEU (2026); idle fleet 2.2-2.4%; Bernstein's 2.5m TEU of scrapping over three years "only prevents the oversupply from worsening"; in 2015-2016 scrapping accelerated only after about a year of losses and liquidations Lengthens the trough into 2029
Route-return cascade Reinforcing, fast Suez transits 54% of 2023 levels (5 October) and 73% (November, Morgan Stanley); Gemini six trans-Suez services plus ME1 (2 November); CMA CGM 166 transits in Q3 2026 against 16 a year earlier; Evergreen's first transit in nearly three years (8 November) Each returning carrier gains a cost edge and forces the rest; full return by about March 2027 drives the Q1-Q2 2027 rate steps
Contract shortening Reinforcing (transmission) Durations compressed from 25 to about 7.5 months; 20% of shippers on quarterly bids Spot reaches the realised rate within a quarter; H2 2027 falls fast
Maersk's freed capacity, filled by price Reinforcing Q3 2026 volume -1% at 96% utilisation (capacity-capped); Q1 2026 volume +9.3% on rate -14% Volume +2.5% in FY2027, at the cost of rate
Share defence and capital intensity Reinforcing, and internal to Maersk 26 ships ordered; capital commitments 19.5bn (Q3 2026) from 13.4bn; capex 10-11bn for 2026-2027 Capex held at 5.2-5.8bn while EBITDA falls, so cash falls, interest income falls, EPS falls further (P-02)
Forwarder channel conflict Reinforcing in loose markets Forwarders about 50% of ocean volume; DSV-Schenker more than 70% integrated (October 2026); Kuehne + Nagel sea volume +4.5% in Q3 against Maersk -1% About one point of volume a year; small in EBIT (about 10m per 100k FFE)
Congestion unwinding Balancing for cost, reinforcing for rate Congestion absorbed 8.5-12.6% of the fleet (4.3m TEU in August 2026); 2021-2023 bottlenecks of up to 13.8% unwound within months Cash cost per FFE falls about 400 (P-01), while the rate falls more
Gemini hub funnelling Reinforcing (positive, Terminals) Ocean volume through Maersk-owned terminals +26% (Q1 2026); 120-200m a year of terminal synergies; APM Terminals utilisation fell to 81-85% as capacity was added Supports Terminals EBITDA growth from FY2028; small
Idling, slow steaming, blank sailings, scrapping Balancing Triggered when losses are widespread (2016; 2023); ZIM's break-even about 1,425 per TEU and Hapag-Lloyd's about 1,100 per TEU mark where the pain starts Engages from 2028; floors the rate near the marginal carrier's cash cost; my FY2028 rate (1,920) is that floor
  • The interplay that matters for the model. The route-return cascade and contract shortening make the fall fast (Q1 to Q3 2027); the orderbook and the cash buffer make it long (2028-2029); congestion unwinding cushions EBITDA, not the rate; and Maersk's own capex turns the falling operating cash into a falling cash pile and a falling interest line, a loop that compounds below EBIT exactly when EBIT is near zero.
  • How long the reinforcing loops can run. Until the industry is cash-negative long enough to scrap: on 2015-2016 timing, about four to six quarters after losses become widespread. With losses widespread from H2 2027 in my base, scrapping above 1% of the fleet a year is plausible from H2 2028 and idling above 4% from 2028. Their effect on rates shows in 2029-2030, not before.
  • The dated evidence that would show the turn: idle capacity above 4% (Linerlytica); scrapping above 1% of fleet on an annualised basis; falling second-hand prices (an early warning of discipline); average fleet speeds falling; Suez transits stalling below about 60% (the opposite signal, extending the current phase).
  • How the loops shape the line items: revenue flat in FY2028-FY2029 (53.9-56.1bn); EBITDA 6.3-6.7bn for two years; company-basis EBIT 0.15-0.47bn for two years; EPS -62 and -51; free cash flow -3.5bn and -3.3bn; the recovery to EPS of about 48 in FY2030 needs the balancing loops to have worked.

Where I differ from the first set. The first set (C-22) treats "the delivery profile falls from 2029" as a well-founded positive in months 18-36. The orderbook arithmetic says the fall is from the 2028 peak, not to a benign level: of 14.9m TEU on order, about 9.0m deliver in 2027-2028, leaving about 5.9m TEU for late 2026 and 2029-2031, so 2029 gross deliveries are still likely 6-8% of the fleet, plus Maersk's own 483,600 TEU from 2029. On that arithmetic 2029 is a second trough year and the recovery depends on scrapping, which is why my FY2029 EPS (-51) is well below the memo's (-15) and consensus (115, two brokers).

6c

Case simplicity

7 answers
Judgement33C-7928D-0255D-0325D-0420D-0566D-0657
Group judgement

Risks

F336th of 6

Layer 3 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The forecasts are predictable in direction and unpredictable in level, and the reasons for each are structural, not accidental.

  • Direction. The chain of reasoning in the first two sets is congruent (D-01), its expected change needs no company strength and its floor rests on strengths Maersk has shown (D-02), and the two root causes are a contracted delivery schedule and a Suez return that is now observed, not forecast (D-03). I put the probability that FY2027 EBITDA is more than a quarter below FY2026 at about 80-85%, and that FY2027 earnings per share is below today's consensus at about 72%.
  • Level. One exogenous variable generates more than the whole change (D-03); the outcomes fall into two regimes, with a modestly negative most-likely error and a strongly positive tail (D-04); and inside the base scenario the floor assumptions sit at the favourable end of their records (D-02). FY2027 EBITDA has a plausible range wider than its own base; the sign of FY2028 earnings per share is about a two-in-three call.
  • Tracking. The case can be followed weekly through outside data and twice through company results in six months, with the company itself obliged to announce material departures (D-05). The trace from the rate to EBIT is clear; from EBIT to earnings per share it is opaque (D-06). The share price and the multiple follow the industry factor (D-07), so even a correct forecast does not translate into a predictable share price.

What this means for the memo. The case is simple and high-risk: simple because one number carries it, high-risk because that number has moved by more than 500 per FFE a quarter and each 100 is USD 95-97 of earnings per share. The predictability that exists is predictability of deterioration: of a fall in earnings through 2027, a cut in the dividend from about USD 200 to about USD 35, negative free cash flow from FY2027, and a first FY2027 guidance range below consensus. What is not predictable is how far the trough goes and whether a new closure interrupts it. The Consumer should present the base case as the most likely path with a dated sequence of tests, not as the middle of a range: the Q4 2026 rate (11 February), the Asia-Europe contract outcome and Suez transits above 90% (by March), the first FY2027 range (11 February), Q1 unit cost (May), and the first year-on-year decline with Ocean EBIT turning negative (August to November 2027). Three numbers of the earlier sets should be restated in the memo: the tax floor (0.48bn, not 0.40bn), FY2026 earnings per share (about 495-505, not 517) and the source of the gap to consensus (mainly the rate, not the items below EBIT).

D-02

To what extent does past and present fundamental delivery make the expected change plausible, and to what extent does the thesis rely only on strengths the business demonstrably has, rather than on strengths it would need to acquire?

F556th of 6

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the expected change, a fall in EBITDA of about 42% in FY2027 into a two-year trough, is highly plausible because it requires no strength at all: it is what this business has done each time an exogenous support was withdrawn (2015-2016, 2022-2023). What does rely on the company's strengths is the floor under the forecast, and that floor rests almost entirely on strengths Maersk has demonstrated. The weakness is that the forecast takes four of them (cost deflation, Terminals earnings, the Logistics margin, the working-capital release) at or near the best of their demonstrated ranges at the same time, so the error in the floor is one-sided: I put FY2028 EBITDA about 0.5-1.0bn lower if each lands in the middle of its range. A positive investment thesis would need strengths Maersk has not shown (fleet discipline, counter-cyclical capital allocation, Logistics integration); the forecasts wisely assume none of them.

Element of the forecast What it requires Demonstrated? Lean against the model
Ocean EBITDA stays positive at the trough (about 150 per FFE) Cost follows rate down far enough Yes: Q4 2023 (group EBITDA still positive at 839m with Ocean EBIT negative, at a rate of 1,925) and Q1 2026 (about 280 per FFE at a rate of 2,081) Neutral
Cash cost per FFE falls about 420 (19%) from Q3 2026 to FY2028 About 170-200 from fuel (largely passed through, so neutral to margin); about 220-250, or 14-16%, from non-fuel cost Partly. Unit cost at fixed bunker fell 10.6% in three quarters of 2023 (2,552 to 2,280 per FFE) and 14% year on year in Q4 2023; unit cost fell 11% in 2015. But the 2023 full-year figure (2,371) was 13% above what Jefferies had modelled (2,100), so fixed network cost proved stickier than expected. In 2009 total unit cost fell 15% but only 5% excluding fuel Down. The need is at the top of the demonstrated range, under tighter constraints (below)

Always reactive: delivered after losses, not before Neutral on size; timing follows losses
Terminals EBITDA flat in FY2027 (2.15bn), then +5-6% a year Tariff indexation offsets fading storage income Mixed: EBITDA rose in 2009 (573m to 738m) and 2019 (+11%); ROIC held at 10.5% in 2023. But ROIC fell from 10.9% to 5.7% in 2016, 621m was impaired in 2017, and Q4 2025 EBITDA (440m) missed consensus by 9% as congestion income normalised Down by about 0.1-0.25bn in FY2027
Logistics & Services EBIT margin of 5.5-5.8% throughout Holding for full years a margin reached in one quarter (5.8% in Q3 2026) No. The full-year record is 4.8% (2025); Q3 was helped by airfreight rates and Middle East landside work that Morgan Stanley expects to fade; Solutions earned 3.2% Down by about 0.05-0.1bn
Dividend at 40%, a 1bn buyback in 2027, an investment-grade balance sheet Paying out through a loss-guided year Yes: 1bn buyback launched in February 2026 while guiding an EBIT loss; dividend halved, not cancelled, in February 2017; buyback suspended in February 2024 when EBITDA was guided at 1-6bn and free cash flow deeply negative Neutral
  • Why the cost line is the item to watch. The forecast needs non-fuel cost per FFE in FY2028 to be about where it was in FY2025 and Q1 2026 in nominal terms, after two and a half years of roughly 3% inflation. The Suez return and 1.0bn of self-help can deliver that, but the release valve that worked in 2023 is narrower. In 2023 the fall came from a lower charter cost, lower terminal costs and higher volumes (Maersk, Q4 2023). Now charters run five to seven years (ships fixed to 2029-2031), about 9.3bn of leases were committed but not started at end-2025, charter rates rose quarter on quarter in every size class in Q3 2026 (Jefferies, 10 November) and they follow freight rates down only after four to eight months (an expert). The chief executive says Maersk can return 10-15% of its chartered fleet within a year; Maersk publishes no expiry schedule against which to test that. I put the probability that the full 4.0bn of relief arrives by FY2028 at about 55%. A shortfall of a quarter of the non-fuel relief is about 60 per FFE, about 0.8bn of EBITDA and about USD 60 of earnings per share, roughly half our whole gap to consensus in FY2028.
  • One strength the earlier sets understate for the trough: Gemini is a loose-market design. Hub-and-spoke reliability failed when hubs clogged (68.4% in August-September 2026) and worked when they were free (about 90% through 2025; Ocean unit cost at fixed bunker down 7% in Q1 2026, the last loose quarter). The base case has congestion clearing in 2027, which is the condition in which Gemini's cost edge has been demonstrated. This supports the cost line; it does not support a price premium, which no evidence shows.
  • Strengths a positive thesis would need and Maersk has not demonstrated. Fleet discipline (the 4.1-4.3m TEU cap was abandoned in 2026); counter-cyclical capital allocation (acquisitions, special dividends and buybacks at peaks; suspension at the 2024 trough); integration of acquired logistics brands; Terminals pricing in overcapacity; and, at industry level, scrapping by cash-rich carriers (5k TEU in 2025, about 43k TEU in 2026 against the 2.5m TEU Bernstein says is needed over three years). None is in the base case, which is why the base case is plausible.

Where I differ from the earlier sets. The second set leans H2 2027 and FY2028 down because the deeper scenario outweighs the upside one. I add a second, separate reason: inside the base scenario itself the floor assumptions sit at the favourable end of their records. I have not cut the EBITDA inputs, because the base is a defensible central case and the rate matters more; the Consumer should read FY2028 EBITDA of 6.3bn as nearer the 60th-65th percentile of outcomes within the base scenario than its middle.

D-04

How wide is the cone of plausible outcomes over the next 18 months, how skewed is it, and so how predictable is the case?

F206th of 6

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the cone is very wide, and it is two regimes, not one bell. Over the six quarters to Q1 2028 the base case earns about 14.1bn of EBITDA, within a plausible range (my estimate of about four outcomes in five) of about 9.5-22bn. FY2027 EBITDA runs from about 4.7bn (deeper) to 13.7bn (disruption-extended) around 8.2bn, so the cone is wider than the base itself; FY2027 earnings per share run from about -160 to +480 around 89, a span equal to about 22% of the share price. The skew has two parts that point in opposite directions: the most likely error is modestly negative (the median sits below the base), and the largest possible error is strongly positive (so the probability-weighted mean sits at or near the base). The direction is therefore highly predictable, the level is not, and the sign of FY2028 earnings per share is about a two-in-three call for a loss.

Quarter Reported Base rate (USD per FFE) Base EBITDA (bn) Plausible range (bn, my estimate) What settles it
Q4 2026 11 February 2027 3,200 4.62 4.45-4.95 Guidance implies 4.47-4.97; the rates are largely earned
Q1 2027 early May 2027 2,600 3.07 2.4-3.9 Asia-Europe contracts; completion of the Suez return
Q2 2027 August 2027 2,200 2.18 1.3-3.6 Transpacific contracts (May); 2027 deliveries
Q3 2027 November 2027 1,975 1.62 0.7-3.5 Depth of price competition; the cost line
Q4 2027 February 2028 1,875 1.30 0.4-3.4 The same; idling and blank sailings
Q1 2028 May 2028 1,880 1.27 0.3-3.0 The same; 2028 deliveries
Six quarters 14.06 about 9.5-22

Each USD 100 per FFE on a quarter's rate is about 0.34-0.35bn of that quarter's EBITDA. The range widens from about 5% either side for the quarter in progress to more than 60% four quarters out.

  • The width, measured three ways. (1) Scenarios: FY2027 EBITDA 4.7bn, 8.2bn, 13.7bn; FY2028 3.1bn, 6.3bn, 9.2bn. (2) Brokers: FY2027 EBITDA from 6.9bn to 12.7bn and earnings per share from 60 to 451; FY2028 EBITDA from 2.2bn to 10.1bn and earnings per share from -394 to +282. (3) The company's own record: the outcome against the midpoint of the first annual guidance was +14% (2019), +50% (2020), about +2% (2023), +246% (2024), +27% (2025) and +148% (2026). The first FY2027 range will itself be about 3bn wide.
  • The shape: two regimes. In the first (normalisation continues; about 80-85%), FY2027 EBITDA lies between about 4.7bn and 9.5bn. In the second (a new closure; about 15-20%), it is about 13-14bn. There is little probability between them, because a rate that neither normalises nor is re-disrupted is not a stable state with fleet growth of 7%.
  • Inside the first regime the skew is to the downside, for four reasons: the deeper case (30%) has no counterpart of similar weight above the base; the floor assumptions sit at the favourable end of their records (D-02); the holdout carriers are returning to Suez faster than the first set assumed; and there is no weak carrier whose exit would end a price war early (D-01).
  • The one upside inside the first regime, the 2027 contract season, deserves less weight than the second set gave it. The second set's upside (a contract book "near 2026 levels" adds about 2.3bn to FY2027 EBITDA) rests on signals that I read differently. COSCO's expectation that 2027 contracts rise year on year is attributed to higher bunker surcharges (Morgan Stanley, 30 October), which is pass-through, not margin. The 2026 contracts were struck at the low of early 2026 (DNB Carnegie: renewals down by up to 30% on 2025; Maersk's Q1 2026 rate was 2,081), so "2027 contracts above 2026 contracts" is compatible with a realised rate far below the 2026 average of about 2,840, which the spot-exposed half of the book lifted. And Morgan Stanley reports cargo owners holding back from signing in the expectation of lower spot rates (5 November), while Xeneta projects spot rates 30% lower by 2027 (12 November). A headline of "contracts up" in February should not be read as the upside case.
  • So where the centre is. FY2027 EBITDA: base 8.2bn; my median about 7.8-8.0bn; probability-weighted mean about 8.0-8.25bn. FY2028: base 6.3bn; median about 5.8bn; mean about 5.9bn. Earnings per share, weighted: about 75-95 in FY2027 and about -90 in FY2028.
  • Probabilities (my estimates). FY2027 EBITDA more than 25% below FY2026: about 80-85%. FY2027 EBITDA below today's consensus median (9.04bn): about 70%. FY2027 earnings per share below consensus (188): about 72%. FY2028 earnings per share negative: about two in three. FY2027 EBITDA above FY2026: less than 5%.
  • The cone does not narrow at the next event; it narrows about a quarter later. On 11 February the company will replace a consensus point with a range about 3bn wide. The narrowing comes between March and May 2027, when the Asia-Europe contracts are struck and the Suez return is complete (transits above 90% of 2023 levels). From then the right tail needs a new event, and the width that remains is the depth of price competition, for which the tests are the Q2 and Q3 2027 rate and unit cost.
  • The cash and balance-sheet cone is narrower than the earnings cone, and the dividend is close to certain. Capital spending (5.8bn) and lease payments including interest (about 4.3bn) are fixed, so FY2027 free cash flow on the model's definition is roughly EBITDA less 9.2bn (after tax and the release of working capital): about -1.0bn in the base, about -4.5bn in the deeper case and about +4bn in the disruption case; net cash excluding leases at end-2027 of about 9bn, 12.7bn and 18bn. Solvency is not inside the cone. The FY2026 dividend (about USD 200 a share, about DKK 1,350, proposed 11 February and paid in March 2027) is the one number in the next 18 months that I hold with about 90% confidence to within about 10%.
  • The market's own cone. Morgan Stanley's twelve-month bull and bear values are DKK 36,400 and DKK 10,000 around a price near DKK 19,900: +83% and -50%.

Where I differ from the earlier sets. (1) The second set leans H1 2027 up. I would treat H1 2027 as neutral: the "bears are early" base rate still stands, but the return through Suez is now an observed process (63% of Far East-Europe capacity), not a forecast, and the contract signal is weaker than it looked. (2) I lean the quarter in progress slightly up: the bottom of a November range has always held under the present chief executive and the final FY2024 range (11.0-11.5bn) was beaten (12.1bn), so I centre Q4 2026 on about 4.7bn, not 4.62bn. That is worth about USD 5 a share and does not merit an input change. (3) The second set describes a wide band around each year; I add that the errors come from two regimes, which matters for how the Consumer presents risk: the base is the most likely path, not the middle of a symmetrical range.

D-05

How helpful is the company's disclosure on operating momentum and key drivers of the thesis to monitor the case over about the next six months?

C662nd of 6

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: helpful, and better than the sector's standard for everything inside EBITDA; with the weekly third-party data on the one driver that matters, this is an unusually trackable case over six months. Three limits keep it from being more than that. Only two results fall in the window (Q4 2026 on 11 February; Q1 2027 in early May), and both are flattered by a depressed base, so read on headline growth they mislead. The company is silent on the two things that decide the next six months (the level of the 2027 contract book and the rate assumed in its guidance). And nothing below EBIT is guided. Trackability of the input is not predictability of the outcome: the disclosure tells the Consumer quickly which scenario is unfolding; it does not narrow the cone in advance.
