  • What makes the disclosure helpful. Published sensitivities (rate, volume, bunker, currency); guidance in three lines (EBITDA, EBIT, free cash flow) with the market-growth and Red Sea assumptions stated; segment reporting that now separates the three Logistics units; and Danish disclosure rules, under which a material departure from the guided range must be announced when it is identified (29 June 2026), so the company itself is a tripwire between results.
  • The lag works in the monitor's favour. Realised rates follow spot by about a quarter, so the spot indices of November to January largely determine the Q1 2027 rate before Maersk reports it, and by 11 February the contract season and January-February spot will say most of what Q2 will be.
  • How to read the two prints. Q4 2026 revenue will be up about 32% year on year and Q1 2027 about 16%, on bases depressed by the loose market of late 2025 and early 2026. The information is in the sequential change in the rate and in unit cost at fixed bunker, and in the guidance package, not in growth rates.
  • What is missing, in order of importance for the next six months. (1) The 2027 contract outcome and the rate inside guidance: management gave no early pricing in November. (2) Any guidance on tax, net financial items, minorities or joint ventures. (3) A charter expiry schedule for the 416 chartered ships, which would test the claim that 10-15% can be returned within a year. (4) The rate and volume assumed in the impairment test (only the discount rate of 9.8%, terminal growth of 2.1% and replacement capital spending of 3.2bn are given). (5) The payment schedule for the 26 newbuilds. (6) The share of Terminals earnings that is congestion-related storage income. (7) Gemini's savings as a separate line.
  • Dates inside six months. 1 December 2026: entry to the OMX Nordic 40 (no information). December to February: Asia-Europe contracts. 11 February 2027: Q4 results, FY2027 guidance, dividend, buyback decision, annual report. March 2027: dividend paid; the Suez Canal Authority's date for all Maersk services to be back. Early May 2027: Q1 results, at the edge of the window.

Where I differ from the earlier sets. The first set (C-60, C-72) scores communication as mixed because the strategy is poorly explained. For monitoring the forecasts over six months that weakness matters little: the forecasts turn on the rate, the cost line and the February package, all of which are disclosed or observable. I therefore score this higher than the first set scored communication.

D-06

How clearly does what the company says it will achieve trace into specific operational drivers, onto identifiable revenue, cost and margin lines (the key fundamental milestones and measures), and through to earnings per share; and how clear is the external transmission from value-chain dynamics to earnings per share?

C-573rd of 6

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: clear for three-quarters of the chain and opaque for the last quarter. What the company says it will achieve is small relative to what happens to it, and traces well onto identifiable lines in Terminals and Logistics and poorly in Ocean, where the claimed Gemini benefit is not visible as a line. The transmission from the Ocean rate to EBIT is the clearest in the sector, though the latest quarter showed a term missing from it. From EBIT to earnings per share nothing is guided and the brokers model it inconsistently, and that is where a correct rate view can still give a wrong earnings figure. The external transmission, from supply and route events to the spot rate to Maersk's realised rate to earnings per share, is clear in direction and speed (about one quarter) and unclear in size at its first joint, because the response of the rate to effective capacity is not linear.

Link in the chain Clarity Evidence What is missing
Company targets to operating drivers Partial Terminals ROIC above 9% (13.8%); Logistics EBIT margin 6% (5.8% in Q3) and organic growth 10%; Gemini 820m-1.1bn; overhead about 180m; capital spending 10-11bn for 2026-2027; group ROIC above 7.5% through the cycle Any group earnings ambition; any capacity target since the fleet cap was dropped; any rate or contract assumption
Drivers to lines, Terminals and Logistics Good Revenue per move +6.4% against cost per move +5.7%; margins by Logistics unit Storage income; like-for-like growth separated from acquisitions
Drivers to lines, Ocean self-help Poor Gemini's 950m sits inside unit cost at fixed bunker, which rose 5% in Q3 2026 despite it; overhead savings sit inside administrative cost A bridge of unit cost into network, congestion, fuel and savings
Ocean rate to EBITDA and EBIT Very good, with one flaw USD 100 per FFE is about 1.3-1.4bn; 100k FFE about 10m; USD 100 a tonne of bunker about 0.3bn. Flaw: Q3 2026 missed by 14% on volume and unit cost in a quarter of rising rates; Morgan Stanley and DNB Carnegie use different constants A utilisation or congestion sensitivity
EBIT to earnings per share Poor Two brokers with the same FY2027 EBITDA (about 9.0-9.1bn) have earnings per share of 105 and 211; two with an identical FY2028 EBITDA (8,212m) have 59 and 104; DNB Carnegie has net financial items of +254m in 2027, Bernstein -203m Guidance on tax, financial items, minorities; the split between EBIT with and without joint ventures
Value chain to spot rate Clear in direction, not in size Orderbook 44% of the fleet; Suez return releases about 7% of effective capacity; congestion absorbed 8.5-12.6% The rate response is a tipping-point response, by management's own account at Q2 2026
Spot rate to realised rate Fairly clear Beta of about 0.4-0.5; lag of about a quarter (revenue is recognised as voyages complete); 46-47% of volume on contracts Regional mix: in late 2016 the indices rose and Maersk's rate did not; in Q3 2026 the rate rose 46% against the 55% Deutsche Bank expected
  • The size of what the company says it will do, in earnings per share. Gemini's 950m is about USD 65 a share, already in the 2026 base; the overhead programme about 12; one point of Logistics margin about 12; the Terminals programme about 20 by FY2030. The whole stated agenda is worth about what USD 115 per FFE is worth (D-03). The trace from the company's promises to earnings per share is therefore reasonably clear and of second-order importance; the trace from the market to earnings per share is what the forecasts depend on.
  • Why the last link is opaque: three items do not move in proportion to profit. Tax is levied on fleet capacity for Ocean and on profit for the land businesses, so it is close to a fixed charge (about 0.48bn on my inputs). Net financial items depend on the path of the cash balance, which depends on the timing of capital spending and on distributions that the company has not set out beyond 2027. Joint ventures (about 0.42-0.48bn) are inside the company's EBIT and outside FactSet's, and minorities (about 0.13bn) rise with Terminals. USD 100 per FFE converts to USD 95-97 of earnings per share almost linearly, because the marginal tax on Ocean profit is close to nil; the level around which that linear relation sits is what the brokers disagree on, by up to USD 100 a share at the same EBITDA.
  • What this means for the forecasts. Given a rate, the model's earnings per share should be right to within about USD 20-30 (the uncertainty in net financial items and tax). The cost of the opacity is in reading the sell-side: a broker's earnings estimate cannot be compared with ours without first checking its EBITDA.
  • The informal message does not trace into the formal one. Management says the market has moved to a structurally higher rate level and that congestion will be commonplace; its guidance, its deferral of the buyback decision and its own impairment sensitivities (headroom of 1.1bn that a 0.2-point rise in the discount rate would remove) do not adopt that view. Where the informal and formal messages differ, the lines of the accounts follow the formal one.

Where I differ from the earlier sets. The first set (C-72) calls guidance specific on mechanics and vague on the rate; I agree and add the second gap, below EBIT, which it describes as a sell-side weakness (C-67) but which is first a disclosure gap. I also qualify the "one transmission constant" both sets use: it is reliable for the base rate, but it did not capture the Q3 2026 miss, and two brokers dispute it.

What perception- or sentiment-driven risks are there that are sudden and high-magnitude, including events elsewhere in the sector, theme or market that could hit the share price whatever the company's own fundamentals?

F1831st of 31

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: high; the stock is a crowded momentum winner in a risk-off regime, at the top of its own valuation history, and moves 10-20% in days on headlines.

  • Crowding: twelve-month return +50.9% (dashboard) after +80% at the October high; relative strength 82nd percentile; Morgan Stanley ranked Maersk second in Europe for twelve-month idiosyncratic momentum; systematic positioning neutral and discretionary at the bottom of its band (Deutsche Bank, 6 November); short interest reached 33% of free float in August 2025 and was squeezed, and shorts can rebuild.

  • Technical: the price has already broken its 50-day average (DKK 19,895 against 23,051); the memo's confirmation of a turn would be a subsequent break of the 150-day (DKK 18,916, 5% below).

Track record (guidance raise/sell-side upgrades) ~L2-3Y

C-61How strong is the company's record of acting, not just talking: a recent record of huge delivery and action, and a mid-term track record of delivery, not misses or justifications, whatever the environment (outperforming in tougher ones is even better), beyond its records against guidance and estimates?F5024th of 31full answer under 4b Management quality
C-70

Beyond meeting or beating, what is the record, nature and behaviour of the company and its chief executive in upgrading guidance?

D4518th of 31

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement: a sandbagger in rising markets and an honest tracker in falling ones; upgrades follow the freight rate with a lag and have never come from self-help; under Clerc an in-year range has never been cut.

  • Upgrades: mid-year raises in 2024 and 2025 (7 August 2025); three in 2026 (29 June ad hoc, 13 August, 5 November), taking the EBITDA midpoint from 5.75bn to 14.25bn.
  • Nature: each raise marked guidance to prevailing spot rates; Morgan Stanley and DNB Carnegie both read them as rate-driven; the Researcher's sector note warns that shipping owners' 2026 raises "measure spot rates and asset sales rather than operating improvement".
  • Falling markets: initial guidance below consensus that proved right or conservative (February 2016, 2019, 2024), a halved dividend in 2017 and the 2024 buyback suspension; in 2015 Maersk cut its full-year profit guidance by more than 15% as rates collapsed.
  • Behaviour signal: the November raise was the first the shares did not reward.
C-69

What is the company's record of missing, meeting or beating guidance and sell-side estimates over the last one, three and five years, including in comparable environmental conditions?

F3826th of 31

Layer 1 · Sell-side estimates

Judgement: the record is a function of the sign of the freight-rate surprise, not of management skill: large beats in rising-rate periods (2021-2022, 2024, H1 2026), misses or downward guidance in falling-rate periods (2015-2016, 2023); the comparable environment for 2027 is a falling-rate one, and Q3 2026 was the first miss of this cycle.

  • Last year: FY2026 EBITDA guidance raised from a 5.75bn midpoint to 14.25bn; Q2 2026 beat EBITDA consensus by 44% and EBIT by nearly three times (1,571m against 540m); Q3 2026 missed EBITDA by about 14% (4,710m against 5,456m), EBIT by about 11% and EPS by about 20% (USD 206 against 257), on lower volume and higher unit cost even though rates rose 46%.
  • Last three years: 2024 opened with a wide, low range and ended at about 12.1bn EBITDA (a large beat on rising Red Sea rates); 2025 guidance raised mid-year as demand outside the US held up; 2023 (falling rates) saw earnings fall to an EBITDA of USD 839m in Q4 and a buyback suspension in early 2024.
  • Last five years: 2021-2022 enormous beats on pandemic congestion; the 2023 normalisation followed.
  • Comparable conditions (falling rates): in 2015 Maersk cut full-year profit guidance by more than 15%; in 2016 and 2019 initial guidance below consensus proved right; in each case consensus was too slow to fall.
  • Peers in 2026: Hapag-Lloyd, COSCO, HMM, ZIM and NYK also beat, which confirms the beats were the market's.
P-06

After assessing the track record of delivery in greater detail, how much weight should the forecasts for the next 18 months to four years carry, and in which direction should they be leaned?

F3022nd of 23

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: the forecasts deserve high weight for FY2026 and for the direction of FY2027-FY2028, low weight for the level of any year after FY2026, and very low weight for FY2029-FY2030. Lean the first half of 2027 up (the bears have been early three years running and the contract season is the one live upside), lean the second half of 2027 and FY2028 down (the deeper case is more probable than the upside case, and the sell-side's record in falling markets is to cut late), and lean the Logistics margin down and the Terminals and cost lines neutral, because those are the parts whose delivery record is management's own.

Whose track record is it? Maersk's earnings record belongs mostly to the freight rate, not to management, and the forecasting weight should follow that split.

  • Rate-driven lines (Ocean revenue, Ocean EBITDA, group EPS): poor forecastability. FY2026 underlying EBITDA guidance moved from a 5.75bn midpoint (February) to 9bn (29 June), 11.5bn (13 August) and 14.25bn (5 November): +148% in nine months, all of it the rate. Consensus FY2026 EPS moved from about -55 to about +507 in a year; FY2027 EPS +496% in three months (FactSet, to 11 November). The sell-side's FY2028 EPS spans -394 to +282 and FY2028 EBITDA 2.2bn to 10.1bn. One year out, the error band on Maersk's EBITDA has been plus or minus 60-100% in each of 2021, 2022, 2024 and 2026.
  • Management-controlled lines: good delivery. Gemini delivered 950m against an 820m-1.1bn target, on time; the Hamburg Süd and Transport & Logistics programme delivered 1.2bn against 600m by 2019; the 2023 workforce programme reached 6,500 roles within months; Terminals ROIC 13.8-16.1% against a 9% hurdle; under Clerc an in-year guidance range has never been cut.
  • Strategic targets: poor delivery. Logistics & Services organic growth of 10% (7.2% in 2024) and EBIT margin of 6% (4.8% in 2025; 5.8% in Q3 2026; the short-term incentive scored this element at zero for 2025); ROIC above 7.5% through the cycle (5.7% in 2025); integration of acquired brands; fleet discipline (the 4.1-4.3m TEU cap abandoned).

Weights I would give my own base case (probability that the outcome lands within about 15% of my EBITDA):

Year Weight Why One-sigma band around my EBITDA
FY2026 High (about 85%) One quarter left; guidance implies Q4 EBITDA of 4.47-4.97bn; the bottom of a November range has always held under Clerc 14.0-14.4bn
FY2027 Low on level, high on direction (about 85% that EBITDA falls by more than 25%) The orderbook and the Suez return are contractual or near-certain; the timing of the H1 reset and the contract season are not about 5.5-11bn
FY2028 Low (about 30%) The trough level sits inside a 150-200 per FFE band that decides the sign of EPS about 3.5-9bn
FY2029-FY2030 Very low (about 20%) Depends on scrapping and on 2029 deliveries that are still being ordered about 4-11bn

Which way to lean, and why.

  • H1 2027: lean up. Normalisation forecasts were overtaken in 2024, 2025 and 2026, and a renewed disruption has arrived within twelve months of every forecast normalisation since 2021; carriers are asking for 2027 Asia-Europe increases and COSCO expects its 2027 contracts up; a contract book near 2026 levels would add about 2.3bn to FY2027 EBITDA (Q1 3,000 and Q2 2,500 instead of 2,600 and 2,200). The disruption-extended case (20%) is fatter in H1 2027 than later.
  • H2 2027 and FY2028: lean down. The scenario-weighted FY2028 EBITDA (about 5.9bn) and EPS (about -90) are below my base (6.3bn, -62) because the deeper case (30%; FY2028 EBITDA about 3.1bn, EPS about -295) outweighs the upside case (20%; about 9.2bn, +145). In comparable falling-rate years (2015, 2016, 2019, 2023) the sell-side was too slow to cut and initial guidance below consensus proved right or conservative; in 2015 Maersk cut its full-year profit guidance by more than 15%.
  • By line: lean the Logistics & Services EBIT margin down (hold 5.5-5.8%, not the 6% target; Solutions earns 1.7-3.2%); keep cost self-help neutral (record good, P-03); keep Terminals neutral-to-down for FY2027 (2016 precedent, P-04); lean the below-EBIT items down relative to consensus (P-08), because that is where the sell-side has done least work.
  • Against consensus: consensus FY2027 EBITDA (9.04bn median, 9.32bn mean) sits above my base (8.17bn) and above my scenario-weighted figure (8.24bn); consensus FY2028 EBITDA (7.54bn) sits above both (6.26bn and 5.9bn). On EPS the gap is larger (FY2027 188 against 93; FY2028 57 against -62), and it is mainly below EBIT.

Where I differ from the first set. The first set's steer is to trust management's operational responses and to expect wide, low formal guidance; I agree. I add one refinement: the forecast's error is asymmetric in time, not just in size. The upside error is concentrated in the next two quarters (the contract season and the disruption tail); the downside error is concentrated in H2 2027-FY2028 (deliveries, deeper price war, below-EBIT items). The Consumer should therefore treat a strong Q4 2026 or Q1 2027 print as consistent with my case, and treat a weak Q2 2027 print (my base: revenue -10%, EBITDA margin 15.4%) as the test.

Group judgement

Financial forecasts

F1023rd of 23

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The financial forecast is a rate-driven collapse into a long trough, with no internal lever large enough to change its sign: EBITDA falls from 14.15bn in FY2026 to 8.2bn in FY2027 and about 6.3-6.7bn in FY2028-FY2029, company-basis EBIT sits at break-even for two years, and the items below EBIT that supported earnings in FY2019-FY2025 (low tax, interest income, a shrinking share count) all reverse at once and turn break-even EBIT into losses of about USD 50-60 a share. The operating leverage is price leverage: the cost line deflates by about a third of the rate fall (congestion, Suez, bunker, about 1bn of self-help), which is why Ocean stays EBITDA-positive at about 150 per FFE at the trough, where it sat in 2023, but cannot keep Ocean EBIT positive below a rate of about 2,100. Pricing power is the market's and transmits within a quarter. The feedback loops make the fall fast (route return, contract shortening) and long (orderbook, cash buffer, Maersk's own capex), with balancing loops engaging only from 2028. Financial leverage is nil but the cash pile shrinks from about 17.5bn to about 3.6bn by end-2030, so interest becomes a drag. Free cash flow after leases is negative in every year from FY2027. The base sits below consensus in every year from FY2027, the gap is mostly below EBIT, and the next Analyst should test predictability on four things: the H1 2027 contract and Suez timing (upside error), the FY2028 trough rate (a 150-200 per FFE band decides the sign of EPS), the cost line (whether unit cost at fixed bunker falls in 2027), and the below-EBIT items (where the sell-side has done least work).

After assessing below-operating-profit cost items (e.g. tax, interest, one-offs, minority investments, etc.) and share buybacks or issuance in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share, net income and free cash flow over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

F1823rd of 23

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: the items below EBIT turn from a support (FY2019-FY2025: low tax rates, interest income, a share count falling about 5% a year) into a drag worth about USD 70-85 a share a year in FY2028-FY2029, and they decide the sign of EPS: with company-basis EBIT near break-even, a tax charge of about 0.40bn that does not fall with profit, net financial items of about -0.5 to -0.65bn and minorities of about 0.13bn turn FY2028-FY2029 into losses of about USD 50-60 a share. Free cash flow is negative by 3.3-3.5bn in FY2028-FY2029 because capex plus lease cash (9.0-9.4bn a year) exceed EBITDA; working capital helps a little, not hurts.

Item by item, against history.

  • Tax: a floor of about 0.40bn. Tonnage tax on Ocean is levied on fleet capacity, and Terminals and Logistics profits are taxed at ordinary rates; Ocean losses earn no relief. History: FY2016 tax of 1.05bn on a pre-tax loss of 714m (DNB Carnegie); FY2019 458m on 967m (47%); FY2023 454m on 4.36bn (215m corporate income tax on land activities, 239m tonnage tax); FY2024 about 0.45bn on 6.3bn; FY2025 about 0.42bn on 2.8bn. Hapag-Lloyd paid a cumulative 40% on its 2009-2020 pre-tax profit. OECD Pillar Two can trigger top-up tax even in shipping loss years (FY2025 annual report). My charge: 0.52bn (FY2026, more land profit), 0.42bn, 0.40bn, 0.40bn, 0.42bn. Effective rates: 6.5%, 24%, then negative in FY2028-FY2029 (-86% and -125% of a pre-tax loss), 38% in FY2030. Per share: USD 28-30 in each trough year.
  • Net financial items: from -0.14bn to -0.77bn (P-02). FactSet's history line (net interest expense -0.77 to -1.0bn, FY2019-FY2025) is gross interest expense including lease interest, not the net figure; the net figure was supported by interest income on a 15-24bn cash pile and by FX gains (510m in 2025, not repeatable). DNB Carnegie forecasts +250m (2027) and +104m (2028), which can only hold if the joint-venture share is included and cash does not fall; consensus FY2028 implies about +0.44bn of net financial income (pre-tax 1,228m against EBIT 786m), which I think is the line most likely to be revised down.
  • Joint ventures and associates: steady, about +0.42-0.48bn. Mostly APM Terminals (Q2 2026 108m against 154m a year earlier, flat excluding a prior-year deferred-tax item). It is inside Maersk's underlying EBIT and in my net financial items line in the forecast form.
  • Minorities: about -0.12 to -0.15bn, rising slightly with Terminals profits and Hapag-Lloyd's 25% of Maasvlakte II (completed November 2026).
  • One-offs (outside underlying EPS, inside reported and cash). Restructuring (77m in Q1 2026; about 200m for the overhead programme; 0.2-0.35bn if a larger programme comes); an Ocean impairment (headroom 1.1bn at end-2025 against 4.1bn a year earlier; pre-tax discount rate 9.8%; Maersk does not disclose the rate assumption that would exhaust it): I put an impairment at the FY2026 or FY2027 test at more likely than not on my numbers; it would be several billion dollars non-cash, cut book value by about USD 150-280 a share and lower D&A by about 0.15-0.25bn a year; logistics goodwill of about 4.6bn now tested in three units (Solutions exposed).
  • Shares and buybacks. History: 20.1m (FY2019) to 14.7m (FY2025), buybacks of 2.0bn in FY2025 and 0.55bn in FY2024, and a 1bn programme in 2026 (0.84bn spent in nine months). Forward: about 14.36m at end-2026, about 14.0m at end-2027 after a new 1bn programme (my base, P-02), flat thereafter. EPS accretion falls from about 5% a year to about 2.5% in FY2027 and nil after.
  • Dividends. Policy 30-50% of underlying net profit, 40% in practice: about USD 207 for FY2026 (about DKK 1,400, paid March 2027, about 7% of today's price), 37 for FY2027, nil for FY2028-FY2029 (a loss year pays nothing under the policy), about 19 for FY2030. Cash paid: about 3.0bn in 2027, 0.5bn in 2028, nil in 2029-2030.

  • Free cash flow per share: about +255 (FY2026), -50, -250, -235, -95: below EPS in every year, because capex (0.75-0.9x D&A) plus lease principal (a cash cost that appears in the P&L only as D&A and interest) exceed D&A by 2-3bn a year.

Where I differ from the first set. (1) Working capital. The first set says a revenue decline drains cash at about 15-17% of the decline, from FactSet's quarterly net working capital of about -10.0bn (-16.6% of revenue). I think that is wrong in direction. FactSet's year-end net working capital on the same three lines is near zero (+752m at end-2024, -150m at end-2025), so the quarterly figure is a definitional artefact; and the cash-flow record (outflows while revenue rose in 2026, operating cash flow above EBITDA while revenue collapsed in 2023) shows that Maersk's working capital moves with receivables and bunker stock, so it releases cash when rates fall. The difference is about 1.5-2.5bn of FY2027 free cash flow against the first set's guidance. (2) Tax. I use the top of the first set's range (0.40-0.42bn in the trough rather than 0.3bn), because land profits (Terminals and Logistics EBIT about 3bn) keep growing and Pillar Two adds risk, not relief.

For the model: see the forecast form; tax rate entered as the absolute charge divided by the model's pre-tax profit, which gives negative rates in the loss years.

Ocean unit cost at fixed bunker: falling 3-6% year on year by Q2-Q3 2027 confirms my cost line; flat or rising says the cost relief is not coming and FY2028 is closer to the deeper case. Suez transits above 90% of 2023 levels by March 2027; idle capacity above 4% and scrapping above 1% of the fleet (the turn toward FY2030 recovery). The February 2027 package: FY2027 guidance, the buyback (my base 1bn), the FY2026 dividend (about USD 207) and the Ocean impairment headroom.

Net, my EPS path is below the memo's from FY2028 and my FY2027 free cash flow slightly better.

C-78

Beyond cutting costs, how much plausible scope is there to improve the financial outputs over the mid term; do peer precedents show it is achievable; would it come from a few big areas or many small ones; and is it congruent with the stated strategy?

F3531st of 31

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: modest and concentrated in two areas (Terminals growth and Logistics margins), worth perhaps USD 0.6-0.9bn of EBITDA by 2030; the one big lever (the Ocean rate) is exogenous; the strategy is congruent with the two levers and incongruent in its fleet order.

  • Terminals: EBITDA from about 2.1bn to about 2.55bn by 2030 (memo) on capacity and tariffs; congruent.
  • Logistics: margin from 5.8% toward the 6% target on about 17-18bn of revenue, about 0.05-0.2bn; peers (Kuehne + Nagel, DSV) show forwarders can earn more, but through a franchise and integration discipline Maersk has not shown; congruent but unproven.
  • Gemini: further network gains small; already largely banked.
  • Capital structure: buybacks give EPS, not earnings.
  • Value, not earnings: a Terminals separation (Morgan Stanley: about 14bn of conglomerate discount) would change the valuation, not the P&L.
C-80

To what extent has the core growth engine decelerated over the last twelve months: is the revenue growth rate running below the company's own normal rate, and is the input change force itself slowing?

F1530th of 31

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: reported revenue growth accelerated over the last twelve months (from -9.9% in Q3 2025 to +27.4% in Q3 2026), but the input force behind it (capacity absorption) is slowing and Maersk's own volume engine has already decelerated sharply; this is the classic gap between a lagging output and a turning input.

  • Output still rising: revenue growth -2.6% (Q1 2026), +20.0% (Q2), +27.4% (Q3); EBITDA +75% in Q3.
  • Engine decelerating: loaded volume from +6.5% (H1 2026) to -1% (Q3), a 7.5-point swing, against market growth of 2-4%, so below the company's normal rate (market-like volume growth).
  • Input force slowing: Suez transits doubled from Q1 to 50-59% of 2023 levels; SCFI peaked in early September; transpacific rates falling from late October; front-loading over; congestion (the other absorber) is seasonal.
  • Next twelve months: revenue growth turns negative from Q2 2027; consensus FY2027 revenue -8%.

The memo's FY2028 per-share bridge (EBIT including joint ventures +12, net interest and other -24, tax -28, minorities -7, giving EPS -46) shows the tax floor, interest decay and the end of buybacks are worth about USD 60 a share against the sell-side's implicit assumptions.

FactSet's broker names on its sheet are partly inconsistent between rows, so cite houses by their own published notes where it matters.
  • Credibility: given the change forces (contracted supply, Suez return) and foundations (no pricing power, fixed costs, rising capex), consensus FY2027 is about 5-10% too high on EBITDA and more on EPS; FY2028-FY2029 EPS is materially too high.

  • What is structurally different from FY2019-FY2025. (1) Capex is higher (5.2-5.8bn against 2.0-4.8bn) because the fleet is growing again; (2) the share count no longer falls; (3) interest income decays instead of growing; (4) the vessel-life change props up EBIT by 0.7bn a year, so on the old basis the FY2027-FY2030 average EBIT would be about 0.5bn, close to zero; (5) Terminals and Logistics are larger (combined EBITDA about 4.1-4.8bn against about 3.65bn in FY2025), so the floor is higher than in 2016 or 2023.
  • What is cyclical. The rate. Every line except capex and the share count would revert toward the FY2023-FY2025 averages if the rate returned to about 2,300-2,400 per FFE, the FY2025 level adjusted for cost inflation; on my path that happens after FY2030, not before.
  • Skew. Scenario-weighted, the FY2027-FY2030 averages are a little worse than my base (EBITDA about 7.0bn, EPS about -10), because the deeper case (30%; FY2028 EPS about -295) is both more probable and, by FY2028, larger in its effect than the upside case (20%; FY2028 EPS about +145), whose benefit fades after 2027.

Where I differ from the first set. The first set's overall steer (base about 50%, deeper 30%, disruption 20%; a lower, flatter plateau rather than a V) is right. My net impact is somewhat worse than the memo's in FY2028-FY2030 (EPS -62, -51, 48 against -46, -15, 52; free cash flow -3.5, -3.3, -1.3bn against -2.7, -2.2, -0.6bn) for three reasons: 2029 is a second trough year (P-05), capex stays at about 5.7bn into 2029 (P-02), and net financial items decay faster with the cash (P-02). It is better in FY2027 free cash flow (-0.7bn against -1.0bn) because working capital releases cash (P-08).

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

The fourth set as a whole

F276th of 6

Layer 4 · The fourth set as a whole

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Judgement: our assessment is reflected in the direction of the sell-side's forecasts, in its ratings and in most of the narrative. It is not yet reflected in the level of consensus from FY2027 (our EBITDA is about 10% and 17% below it; consensus earnings per share is about double ours in FY2027 and of the opposite sign in FY2028), in the duration embedded in FY2029, or in guidance, because none exists beyond FY2026. The gap is unfavourable for an investment now. Over the next six months the most likely sequence is a first FY2027 range below consensus (about 75%), cuts by the highest camp of brokers and lower target prices. Over months 6 to 18 it is the first year-on-year decline, Ocean EBIT below zero, a dividend falling from about USD 200 to about USD 35, the end of the buyback and, more likely than not, an impairment. Because the direction is already consensus, the gap is one of depth and duration, not of surprise; its mirror image matters as much: I find nothing positive within 18 months that the market has failed to recognise.

  • Four counter-currents in 2027 that the memo should warn against reading as improvement. Results for Q4 2026 and Q1 2027 that still show growth of about 32% and 16% on depressed bases; a FY2027 range that is raised in August or November while quarterly EBITDA halves; rating upgrades on valuation while estimates fall; and a headline that 2027 contracts are "up", which is compatible with a realised rate about a quarter below 2026's.
  • Where the narrative could move against our view. A new closure of the Red Sea or Hormuz (about 15-20%): fast, and it lifts all carriers together. Evidence through the first half of 2027 that landside constraints are absorbing the delivered ships (rates holding with Suez transits above 90%): this is the high camp's thesis and the Q1-Q2 2027 rate is its test. A larger and faster cost relief than the bears assume: favourable to our numbers against theirs, not against consensus.
  • Where the stock is in its lifecycle, on this evidence. Past the top of its Reinforcing phase and stalling: the price is below its 50-day average and 22% below the high; sell-side momentum has slipped from A to B with breadth about to turn; technical momentum is still rated A, which lags; valuation is F. In the base case the next phase is a collapse of estimate momentum through 2027. Bottoming is a 2028 event at the earliest, and its markers are listed in I-03.
  • A caution on the model's return at the guideline fair multiple. The -111% at 18 months and -53% at three years apply a price-to-earnings multiple of 22 times to earnings two years forward that are negative or close to nil. They measure the unsuitability of a price-to-earnings frame at a cyclical trough, not an expected loss. As a cross-check of my own, not a model figure: 0.45-0.5 times a FY2027 book value of about USD 4,185 a share is about DKK 12,700-14,100 (about DKK 12,100-13,400 after an impairment of about USD 200 a share); with about DKK 1,590 of dividends over the 18 months that is a total return of about -20% to -30% in the base case, about -40% in the deeper case (0.4 times an impaired book) and about +45% in the disruption case (about 0.9 times book), or about -15% weighted by the scenario probabilities. The Consumer should explain the model's figure in those terms.
  • What I would want Richard to take from the fourth set. The market agrees with us on what happens next and disagrees on how bad and how long. That is not the configuration of an opportunity: there is no conservative guidance to be beaten in absolute terms, no estimate momentum to come, and no unrecognised positive. The opportunity the earlier sets describe, a purchase near 0.4-0.5 times book once consensus carries two loss years, guidance has been reset to a loss and scrapping has begun, is 12 to 24 months away, and nothing in guidance, estimates or the narrative yet reflects it.

I-01

How likely is company guidance to be raised, met or reduced at the next few earnings events (all such events over the next 18 months), and what would drive each outcome? Secondarily, how likely is any guidance, mid-term target or ambition for 18 months to three years out to be raised, met or reduced?

F306th of 6

Layer 4 · Guidance

Judgement: three things, in order of importance. (1) The only guidance in force, for FY2026, will be met inside its range on 11 February 2027 (about 85%), and free cash flow will beat its floor; there is no gap between it and our view. (2) The first FY2027 range, given the same day, is the event that matters: I put about 75% on its EBITDA midpoint being more than 5% below today's consensus median of 9.04bn, with a central expectation of about 6-9bn (midpoint about 17% below consensus), an EBIT range of about 0-3bn (midpoint about 45% below consensus) and free cash flow guided negative. (3) Once set low, that range is more likely to be raised or narrowed upward during 2027 than cut, even in our own below-consensus base case: about 50% raised at some point, about 28% maintained, about 22% cut, the last of which would be the first in-year cut under the present chief executive. A raise in 2027 would carry no information about the business: in the same base case quarterly EBITDA falls from about 3.1bn to about 1.3bn through the year. Beyond 18 months, no target is likely to be raised except the Terminals return; the group return target will be missed for three years without being formally reduced.

  • Why below consensus in February: the record sorted by the state of consensus. The first annual range was below consensus in 2019 (by about 20%), 2020 (8%), 2023 (18%) and 2024 (a 3.5bn midpoint against 6.7bn); it was in line in 2025 and close in 2026; it has not been above. The in-line years followed a year in which the bears had already cut estimates. The below-consensus years followed strength. Today consensus FY2027 earnings per share has risen almost six-fold in three months (+496%).
  • What the range will probably look like. EBITDA of about 6-9bn, with about one chance in four each of a midpoint at or above 8.5bn or at or below 6.5bn; EBIT (EBITDA less about 6.45bn of depreciation plus about 0.4bn of joint ventures) of about 0-3bn; free cash flow "negative" or a floor of about -2bn to -3bn (at a 7.5bn midpoint the arithmetic gives about -2bn); the two ends tied explicitly to the pace of rate normalisation after the Red Sea return. Our base (8.2bn) would sit in the upper half of that range.
  • The rest of the 11 February package. Dividend: about USD 200 a share (about DKK 1,350; consensus 203; DNB Carnegie 218), close to certain because the policy is mechanical. Buyback: some new programme about 60%, 1bn the modal size, 2bn about 15%; since Morgan Stanley says consensus models 2bn, the likelier surprise is on size, downward. Capital spending: a 2027-2028 range, if given, at or above 11bn (about 65%), which is a raise that lowers free cash flow. Ocean impairment at the FY2026 test: about 30%; if none is taken the disclosed headroom will be thin (1.1bn at end-2025, removed by a 0.2-point change in the discount rate), and I put the cumulative probability by February 2028 at about 60%.
  • How much of this the market expects. A conservative first range is widely expected (Bernstein says so in terms; the mean rating is Underweight). The surprise is in the level. A midpoint at or below 7.5bn forces the six visible brokers with FY2027 earnings per share of 259 or more to cut; a range of 7-10bn would be read as relief.
  • The in-year pattern, and why it will mislead. Under the present chief executive the range was raised four times in 2024, at least once in 2025 and three times in 2026, and never cut. With a first range of 6-9bn and our base path, the first half delivers about 70% of the midpoint, so management narrows upward in August or November. Headlines of "guidance raised" would then coincide with quarterly EBITDA of 3.07bn, 2.18bn, 1.62bn and 1.30bn. The market has already stopped paying for rate-driven raises (5 November 2026), and it punished the one downward move under this chief executive (the narrowing toward the low end in November 2023: shares -17% on the day). The reaction to guidance is therefore asymmetric: little reward for a raise, a large penalty for a cut.
  • What would produce a cut in 2027 (about 22%). The deeper case: a Q2 or Q3 rate at or below about 1,900-2,000 while unit cost at fixed bunker fails to fall. It would most likely arrive as an ad hoc release under the Danish rules, between results; I put about 35% on at least one ad hoc guidance change, in either direction, during 2027.

Secondarily, 18 months to three years out.

Target or ambition Raised Met or kept Reduced or missed Comment
Group ROIC above 7.5% through the cycle under 5% Kept as a target: about 85% Missed in fact in FY2027-FY2029 (about 2-3%, then about nil); formally lowered about 15% "Through the cycle" lets the target stand while the years miss it
Logistics & Services EBIT margin 6%; organic growth 10% under 5% Margin met for a full year by FY2028: about 25% Growth target re-based or dropped within three years: about 40% Our inputs have 5.5-5.8% and 1-4%

Where I differ from the earlier sets. I agree with both sets on February 2027. I add the dynamic after it (a low first range that is then raised while earnings fall), which changes how the memo should treat "a track record of raising guidance" as evidence; the FY2026 free cash flow beat (about 3.9bn on the model's definition against "above 3.0bn"); and the buyback as a likely disappointment on size against what Morgan Stanley reports consensus to model, even though I keep the second set's base of a 1bn programme.

I-02

How likely are sell-side consensus forecasts, and the net breadth of revisions, to move higher or lower over the next few earnings events (over the next 18 months): to be upgraded, cut or held, and why? Secondarily, how likely are the consensus forecasts for 18 months to three years out to be upgraded, cut or held?

F246th of 6

Layer 4 · Sell-side estimates

Judgement: consensus for FY2027 and FY2028 is more likely to be cut than raised at each of the next events, and the net breadth of revisions to FY2027, positive today, should turn negative within about three months (about 65-70%) and almost certainly within twelve (about 85%). The path will not be one step. FY2026 will be trimmed slightly. FY2027 is still being marked up by late movers and will fall mainly after 11 February and again from August 2027, with a pause around the Q1 print in May. FY2028 is already falling. By the Q2 results in August 2027 I expect the FY2027 EBITDA median at about 8.0-8.5bn (6-11% lower) and earnings per share at about 110-140 (25-40% lower); by February 2028 I put about 55-60% on the FY2028 earnings-per-share median being negative. The sell-side is three camps, not one consensus; our numbers are the lowest camp's; and the fall in "consensus" will be the capitulation of the highest camp, which needs falling reported rates and so will not be complete before August 2027.

  • The state today. FY2027: EBITDA median 9.04bn (mean 9.32bn), earnings per share 188; FY2028: EBITDA 7.54bn (mean 7.23bn), earnings per share 57 (mean about 26-28). Revisions to FY2027 on the dashboard: earnings per share +20% over one month and +496% over three; EBITDA +7% and +58%; target price -2% over one month, its first fall; buy share 7%; pinned sell-side momentum B (A at the time of the memo). FactSet's weekly mean for FY2028 has fallen 22% in a month while FY2027 rose: the brokers are already moving the two years in opposite directions, raising the near year on the exit rate and deepening the trough.
  • Three camps for FY2027 earnings per share (16 brokers visible). Seven sit at 60-116 (among them Jyske Bank 60, Morgan Stanley 74, Deutsche Bank 83); three at 185-211 (DNB Carnegie 211, Nordea 193); six at 259-451 (UBS 268, Fearnley 275, Clarksons Securities 451). Our 89 is inside the lowest camp, and our EBITDA of 8.18bn is the middle of the lower half (seven of fifteen at or below 8.3bn). For FY2028, three of sixteen are already negative (to -394) and four more are within USD 25 of zero. FactSet's broker names are inconsistent between rows for some entries, so I name only those that are consistent.
  • So the "gap to consensus" is the distance between camps, and the median moves when the high camp gives way. Its numbers assume that congestion and landside constraints absorb the new ships (Jefferies, 11 November: rates stay high through 2027). They will be cut on evidence of falling realised rates, which arrives in this order: the first FY2027 range (February); Q1, which will still show growth and an annualised EBITDA above consensus (May); the first year-on-year fall (August); Ocean EBIT below zero (November).
Window FY2026 FY2027 FY2028 Net breadth (FY2027)
To 11 February 2027 Trimmed: EBITDA from 14.45bn toward 14.3bn; earnings per share toward 500 Roughly flat: two estimates dated late September still to be marked up; the bears trimming on spot Drifting down Falls from positive toward zero
February to April 2027 Closed Cut after the range: EBITDA median to about 8.5bn, earnings per share to about 150 Cut Negative (about 65-70%)
May to July 2027 Pause; about 30% chance of net upgrades in the month after Q1 Flat to down Near zero
August 2027 to February 2028 Cut toward the outcome (our base 8.2bn and 89) Cut hard: median earnings per share to about zero or below Strongly negative
  • The arithmetic that will make the revisions look like a collapse. Depreciation of about 6.4bn is fixed, so 1% off consensus FY2027 EBITDA (90m) is about USD 6 a share, 3.3% of 188; 1% off FY2028 EBITDA (75m) is about USD 5, 9% of 57. A 10% cut to EBITDA is a 33% cut to FY2027 earnings per share and takes FY2028 to about zero. On moderate changes to EBITDA the dashboard's revision percentages will be extreme, and I expect the pinned sell-side momentum rating to fall from B to the bottom of its scale within two quarters in the base case.
  • A further USD 30-40 a share of cuts does not need the rate to fall at all. Consensus FY2028 pre-tax profit (1,228m) exceeds EBIT (786m) by 0.44bn, which is about the joint-venture income with no net interest cost; our net financial items are -0.48bn. When brokers roll forward their cash balances for the FY2026 dividend (about 2.9bn), the buyback and capital spending of 5.8bn, that line must fall. The spread between houses shows it has not been done consistently: DNB Carnegie has +254m and +104m for 2027 and 2028, Bernstein -203m and -179m. Tax is the smaller item: the FY2028 median implies tax plus minorities of about 0.39bn against our 0.61bn.
  • Target prices and ratings will move in opposite directions. The mean target (DKK 19,466) is 2% below the price; the targets set after Q3 run from DKK 17,500 to 19,600. Targets follow estimates and book value down (lower in six months: about 70%). Ratings cannot become much more negative (7% Buy, mean Underweight), so as the price falls toward the targets the next changes are as likely to be upgrades on valuation as downgrades; DNB Carnegie's move from Sell to Hold on 6 November, "on lack of catalyst", is the template. I put about 55% on the buy share being higher in twelve months. The dashboard's two sell-side signals will diverge, and the revisions, not the ratings, are the one tied to earnings.
  • What would make consensus rise instead. A new closure (about 15-20%): FY2027 EBITDA to about 12-14bn and earnings per share to about 400-480, with the high camp vindicated. Short of that, a firm contract season and a slower return of the remaining holdouts could hold FY2027 near 9bn until August (about 15%) without changing FY2028.
  • The record supports "late, then abrupt". Before the February 2023 guide consensus FY2023 EBITDA was 11.6bn against an outcome of about 9.6-9.8bn. Q3 2026 was the first miss of this cycle (EBITDA 14% below). Brokers covering the Asian carriers already assume the fall (Yang Ming's earnings per share expected to drop from NT$8.17 to NT$3.44 in FY2027; Evergreen's from NT$40.53 to NT$32.47).

Secondarily, 18 months to three years out. FY2028 EBITDA consensus (7.54bn) is likely to be cut by 10% or more by the end of 2027 (about 70%), toward 6.0-6.5bn. FY2029 rests on two brokers (EBITDA 8.5bn, earnings per share 115) and embeds a recovery that is the least examined number on the sheet: deliveries in 2029 are still 3.59m TEU, fleet growth 9.7% (Jefferies), and Maersk's own ships arrive then. As coverage of FY2029 fills in from a lower FY2028 base, I put about 75% on its earnings-per-share consensus being below 115 in twelve months. Upgrades to 2029-2030 need evidence of idling above 4% of the fleet and scrapping above 1% a year, which on the timing of 2015-2016 comes four to six quarters after losses are widespread: from the second half of 2028.

Where I differ from the earlier sets. Both sets say consensus is too high most of all below EBIT. Measured through our own lines (basis section), the larger part of the gap is EBITDA: about 60% in FY2027 and 75% in FY2028. The below-EBITDA part (about USD 30-40 a share) is real and is the more certain to be revised, because it needs no fall in the rate; but the memo should not describe it as the main reason consensus is wrong. I also replace "consensus" with three camps, which changes the question from "will the sell-side cut?" to "when does the high camp give way?".

11d

Current multiple

F1550th of 53Memo topic 11, The fair multiple: the Rater's score1 answer
D-0730
D-07

To what extent do the share price and the multiple move with sector and industry peers rather than idiosyncratically (on a rolling six-month basis), and so how far is the multiple company-driven rather than industry-driven?

F306th of 6

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement, with a warning about its basis: the co-movement table supplied for this question is empty ("no price history found"), so I cannot measure the rolling six-month relationship; what follows is judgement from other evidence, and the measurement should be made before the memo relies on it. On that evidence the share price is driven mainly by a factor it shares with other container carriers, the expected path of freight rates, and has little in common with the European logistics sector in which the dashboard places it. My estimate is that about two-thirds of the movement over a rolling six months is that common factor and about one-third is specific to the stock, bunched around its own dated announcements and around positioning. The multiple is industry-driven almost entirely: the price-to-book ratio has moved with rate expectations, the forward price-to-earnings ratio is an artefact of a collapsing denominator, and nothing in the files shows a multiple that Maersk has earned for itself.

  • Timing follows the freight index, which is an industry variable. The shares peaked within zero to two months of the rate index in 2015, 2022 and 2026 (SCFI peak in early September 2026; share peak shortly after) and troughed with it in 2023 (first set, C-84). The fall of 22% from the 52-week high and the three-month return of -3.9% came while the index rolled over and Suez transits rose, not on company news.
  • Twelve-month returns place Maersk with the carriers, not with logistics. Maersk +50.9%; NORDEN +49.2%; the shipping cohort about +82%; CMB.TECH +105.6%; Wallenius Wilhelmsen +96.0%; Hoegh Autoliners +113.8%. In the dashboard's logistics sector: DSV +4.8%, DHL +38.3%, Kuehne + Nagel +60.6%, with different drivers (synergies, yields, volumes).
  • The closest peer shows how large the stock-specific part can be. Hapag-Lloyd, the Gemini partner in the same freight market, returned +14.1% over the same twelve months, and its technical rating on the dashboard is D against Maersk's A. The reasons are specific to each stock: Maersk has more spot exposure (53-56% of volume) and so more earnings torque (consensus FY2026 earnings per share +179% against +34% for Hapag-Lloyd); Hapag-Lloyd started at 1.2-1.5 times book with a very small free float, carries net debt of 1.4 times EBITDA and agreed a USD 4.2bn cash purchase of ZIM; Maersk started at about 0.5-0.6 times book with a third of its free float sold short (August 2025). The industry factor set the direction for both; exposure, starting valuation and positioning set the size.
  • Part of the last year was a positioning event. Morgan Stanley's strategists ranked Maersk second in Europe on twelve-month idiosyncratic momentum. A short squeeze from record short interest, buybacks and a guidance sequence of three raises in five months are stock-specific; they are also exhausted (the 5 November raise was the first the shares did not reward).
  • The multiple. Price to book: about 0.4-0.5 times at the 2023 trough, 0.6 times in July-August 2026, about 0.8 times at DKK 22,870 on 8 November, about 0.69-0.70 times now. Those moves track the rate outlook. The 24-month-forward price-to-earnings ratio of 56-57 times, at the 100th percentile of its ten-year history, records the fall in consensus earnings two years out and carries no information about the company's standing against peers.
  • What could make the multiple company-driven, and why it has not. Terminals (Morgan Stanley puts the conglomerate discount at about USD 14bn; DNB Carnegie's sum of the parts gives Terminals about DKK 10,800 a share) would command an infrastructure multiple if separated; that decision belongs to the controlling shareholder, who has not raised it. Capital returns are mechanical (a dividend of 40% of profit) or deferred (the buyback). The entry to the OMX Nordic 40 on 1 December is a small flow. An Ocean impairment would change the denominator of price to book, not the market's view of the company.
  • What follows for predictability. Being right about Maersk's own numbers is not enough to predict the share price over six months: the weekly freight indices and Red Sea headlines move every carrier together, by 10-20% in days. Over the next 18 months I expect the price-to-book ratio to be set by the industry cycle, toward the 0.4-0.5 times at which carriers have troughed, and not by anything Maersk delivers against our forecasts. The same holds in reverse: a new closure would re-rate all carriers at once.
  • What should be measured. Rolling six-month correlation and beta of weekly returns against Hapag-Lloyd, COSCO Shipping Holdings, Evergreen and ZIM, against an equal-weighted carrier basket, against weekly changes in the SCFI and against the dashboard's logistics sector; and Maersk's price to book against the carriers' median through 2015-2016, 2022-2023 and 2026.

Where I differ from the earlier sets. No disagreement; the first set (C-82, C-84) reaches the same view from the historical pattern. I add the comparison with Hapag-Lloyd, which shows that "moves with the industry" and "moves with its closest peer" are different statements for this stock.

Outside the eleven ratings

Answers not placed under any of the eleven ratings

The table the ratings map uses places these nowhere; each is shown with the reason the table records.

Why it is not placed It asks whether the stock fits the sector's historical patterns of share price and financial outperformance, a cross-check across many topics rather than one of the eleven.

C-84

What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance, and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

F1531st of 31

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Maersk today fits the classic container-carrier sell pattern almost exactly (peak earnings, peak multiple percentile, peak momentum, rate peak already passed, record orderbook); the outperformance pattern (buying at 0.4-0.5x book as scrapping starts and deliveries peak) is 12-24 months away.

  • Rule 1: the share price follows the freight index, not earnings. It peaks within zero to two months of the rate peak and troughs six to 24 months before the earnings trough. 2015-2016: peak DKK 16,410 (March 2015), a 55% fall to DKK 7,410, dividend halved, 0.4-0.5x book, two years before the earnings trough. 2022-2023: price peaked with the CCFI in January 2022, troughed (about DKK 10,000) one month after the CCFI bottom in October 2023, ahead of the Q4 2023 loss and the February 2024 buyback suspension. 2026: SCFI peak about 3,590 in early September; share peak near DKK 24,900-25,380 shortly after; price now 22% below.
  • Rule 2: consensus lags rates and peaks with them. FY2027 EPS consensus rose about 500% in three months to November 2026; the cuts come once rates fall.
  • Rule 3: outperformance comes from trough-on-trough buying on book value, not from earnings: 0.4-0.5x book with scrapping beginning and deliveries falling, then a re-rating toward about 0.8-1.0x.
  • Rule 4: record orderbooks precede multi-year rate depression; 2016 was the last precedent of a record orderbook meeting weak demand.
  • Rule 5: bears are early in disruption regimes. Normalisation forecasts were overtaken in 2024, 2025 and 2026; a renewed disruption has arrived within twelve months of each forecast normalisation since 2021. This lowers timing confidence, not direction confidence.
  • Rule 6: in logistics, outperformance comes from management-controlled synergies (DSV) and franchise conversion recovery (Kuehne + Nagel); in terminals, from re-rating as infrastructure; for conglomerates, from separation (Maersk Oil, Drilling, Svitzer).
  • Fit: on rules 1, 2 and 4 Maersk is at the point the pattern sells; on rule 5 the timing could slip by two to four quarters; rules 3 and 6 describe what could make it a buy later.

Why it is not placed The table holds this question in reserve for Riskiness sub-items that are not yet built, so it is not yet placed.

C-83

How far could the shares fall to a fair trough multiple?

F1531st of 31

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: about 30-40% to a fair trough multiple of 0.4-0.5x book, and about 45% in a deep trough; the FY2026 dividend (about DKK 1,300-1,370, paid March 2027) cushions about 7 points.

  • Book value per share: consensus USD 4,270 (FY2026), 4,223 (FY2027), 4,207 (FY2028). An Ocean impairment would lower it.
  • Arithmetic at about DKK 6.75 per USD, on FY2027 book of about USD 4,220: 0.5x gives about DKK 14,250 (-28%); 0.45x about DKK 12,800 (-36%); 0.4x about DKK 11,400 (-43%). With an impairment of about USD 200 a share, 0.4x gives about DKK 10,850 (-45%).
  • Cross-checks: the memo's deeper case DKK 11,200 (-44%); sum of the parts with Ocean at nil DKK 14,000-15,700 (-21% to -30%); 2015-2016 fall of 55% from peak; 2023 trough about DKK 10,000; Morgan Stanley's bear case DKK 6,700 (0.4x book and 1x EV/EBITDA on lower book values, older vintage) is an extreme.
  • Already fallen: 22% from the 52-week high; the trough-multiple downside is measured from today's DKK 19,895.

Why it is not placed It rates the staircase of news the market can look forward to, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or the catalysts as such.

Group judgement

Stock market narrative

F2231st of 31

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The narrative staircase is thin and largely pre-announced for the next 18 months, while the dated negatives (February 2027 guidance, Q2 2027 first decline, impairment headroom, a falling dividend) are concrete; the genuinely novel and well-founded positives belong to 2028-2029. The stock is at the top of its Reinforcing phase on the dashboard's mechanical rule, but the input force has turned and the narrative has already cracked (the November raise was not rewarded; the price is 22% below its high and below its 50-day average). For the second set: the investment narrative a long case would need ("trough-on-trough at 0.4-0.5x book as scrapping starts and deliveries fall") does not exist in print yet and is likely to be written from mid-2028; until then the narrative risk is skewed to the downside except for a geopolitical surprise.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the earlier answers agree with each other, which is a check on the analysis rather than on the company.

D-01

How congruent are the answers to all the questions in the earlier sets: to what extent do they support and align with each other, rather than contradict or jar?

–68

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the two earlier sets are highly congruent in architecture, direction, timing and mechanism, and the eight AlphaSense reports commissioned to test them contradict none of the judgements that carry the forecasts. They jar in seven places. None changes the sign or the shape of the forecasts; three change numbers (tax, working capital, Terminals) and one changes what the memo may claim as our difference from the market. The more important fragility is not a contradiction between the sets but an assumption they share: about 4.0bn of Ocean cost relief by FY2028.

  • What aligns is the whole chain that carries the forecast. External forces (capacity absorption reverses; supply is contracted) lead to industry behaviour (volume-first price-setters, no exit), to the company's position (no Ocean pricing power, 53-56% of volume exposed to spot, cost fixed per sailing), to the line items (rate -24% and -11%; about 70% of lost Ocean revenue falls through to EBITDA), to the items below EBIT, to guidance and consensus (a wide and low first range; consensus too high). Every link uses the same constants (USD 100 per FFE is about 1.36-1.39bn of EBITDA and USD 95-97 of earnings per share; realised rates follow spot at a beta of 0.4-0.5 with a one-quarter lag) and the same dates.
  • An independent check that the case does not extrapolate beyond observed states. The trough the second set assumes for FY2028 (rate 1,920 per FFE, cash cost 1,770, Ocean EBITDA about 150 per FFE) is close to what Maersk reported three quarters ago in a loose market: Q1 2026 had a rate of 2,081, a cash cost of about 1,800, Ocean EBITDA of about 280 per FFE and Ocean EBIT of -192m. The base case for 2028 is Q1 2026 with about 160 less rate and 30 less cost.

Where the sets jar, and how I resolve each.

  1. Working capital (first set: a drain of 15-17% of any revenue decline; second set: a release). The second set is right on direction: Maersk's working capital released cash in every falling-rate year in the record (2009: 565m in container shipping; 2015: 382m; 2019: 477m; 2023: 417m; 2025: 552m) and absorbed it in rising ones (2024: 311m; DNB Carnegie has an outflow of 1.13bn for 2026). The first set's figure was a FactSet artefact (the model summary notes that FactSet holds receivables only at year-ends, so its quarterly figure is inventories less payables). On size the second set overshoots. In 2023, with revenue down 37%, receivables released 1.87bn but other working capital absorbed 1.44bn, a net 0.42bn; no falling year shows a net release near the 1.05bn the second set takes for FY2027, and its FY2026 absorption (about 1.76bn in the model) exceeds DNB Carnegie's 1.13bn and ignores the usual fourth-quarter unwinding. I move about 0.45bn from FY2027 to FY2026. Consequence for the memo: FY2026 free cash flow should beat the "above 3.0bn" guidance comfortably (about 3.9bn on the model's definition), and FY2027 free cash flow is about -1.0bn, not -0.6bn.
  2. Tax (both sets: a floor of about 0.40bn, FY2025 "about 0.42bn"). The reported FY2025 charge was 556m; the model implies about 0.9bn for FY2026; DNB Carnegie's published rates imply about 0.48bn in both 2027 and its loss year 2028; the 8 November memo itself said 0.4-0.5bn. I raise the floor to 0.48bn. This costs USD 4-6 a share a year and strengthens, not weakens, the earlier sets' point that tax does not fall with profit.
  3. The source of the gap to consensus (both sets: mainly below EBIT). As shown in the basis section, it is mainly the rate. The memo should say that our difference from consensus is a lower trough rate (Ocean EBITDA of 2.1bn in FY2028 against 4.2bn), which is the least knowable item in the case, plus about USD 35-40 a share of decaying interest income. It should not present the items below EBIT as an analytical edge over the informed bears, who already have them.
  4. Buyback (first set: none; second set: 1bn in 2027). I side with the second set. Added evidence: the controlling shareholder takes part pro rata in buybacks and has its own cash calls (A.P. Moller Holding bought all of Ocean Yield in August 2026); DNB Carnegie expects buybacks to continue through 2027 because the newbuild instalments fall mainly from 2028; and the February 2026 programme was itself a reduction, which the chief financial officer explained as caution, so 1bn, not the 2bn Morgan Stanley says consensus models, is the modal size.
  5. FY2029 (first set: deliveries fall, recovery begins; second set: a second trough year). The evidence supports the second set. Scheduled deliveries are 3.29m TEU in 2027, 5.23m in 2028 and 3.59m in 2029, with fleet growth of 9.7% in 2029 (Jefferies, 26 October 2026); Bernstein notes that the lower 2029 figure mainly reflects yard slots that liners can still fill with new orders; Maersk's own 483,600 TEU arrives in 2029-2030.
  6. Drop-through (first set: close to 100%; second set: about 70%). Not a contradiction: the base rate drops through at close to 100% at the margin; fuel and surcharge pass-through and the deflating congestion cost bring the average to about 70%.
  7. Terminals (first set: EBITDA compounding at 5-7%; second set: flat in FY2027). I side with the second set and lean further down (D-02): the last quarter without a congestion windfall, Q4 2025, produced EBITDA of 440m, an annual rate of 1.76bn against 2.13bn in FY2026.

Tensions inside the case the two sets share.

  • Volume against share. Maersk is losing share and forwarders divert cargo, yet loaded volume grows 2.5% in FY2027 while demand in TEU-miles falls. The two are reconciled only by price: the Suez return frees Maersk's own ships from the 96% utilisation cap of Q3 2026, and Maersk fills them by pricing, as in Q1 2026 (volume +9.3% on a rate down 14%). The volume assumption and the rate assumption are therefore one assumption. If the company's stated volume sensitivity (100k FFE is about 10m of EBIT) is right, this does not matter; DNB Carnegie argues the true figure is five to seven times larger, in which case share loss is costlier than both sets assume.
  • The rate floor. The second set floors the rate "at the marginal carrier's cash cost". In 2016 the price-setters did not stop there until Hanjin failed. Today the natural candidate for exit, ZIM, has agreed to be bought by Hapag-Lloyd for USD 4.2bn in cash (subject to Israeli consent), and every large carrier holds net cash, so there is no weak hand to fold. The balancing loop available is idling and scrapping, not exit, and it is slower. This is congruent with the second set's long trough, and it is why I regard the 30% weight on the deeper case as a minimum.
  • The valuation frame against the forecasts. Both sets anchor value on book value (0.4-0.5x at the trough) while judging an Ocean impairment more likely than not and noting that the vessel-life change raised carrying values. The model's guideline return applies a price-to-earnings multiple to earnings two years forward that are negative; its -111% at 18 months is an artefact of that. The forecasts are congruent with each other; the price-to-earnings frame is not congruent with the forecasts.

The shared assumption that carries most weight. Between FY2026 and FY2028 the rate takes 12.8bn out of EBITDA and the falling cash cost per FFE (congestion, the Suez return, bunker, self-help) puts 4.0bn back. Both sets rely on it; no broker in my files models it explicitly, and those who comment lean the other way (DNB Carnegie raised its forward unit-cost estimates after Q3; Morgan Stanley expects operating costs to prove sticky). I test it in D-02.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the sell-side and investors already articulate the case, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or how much of the case is known.

I-03

How comprehensively are these dynamics articulated in the current narrative of (i) sell-side commentary and (ii) investor commentary (for example on Twitter): how well known is each of them to investors who own or cover the company or its sector, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to three years out?

F286th of 6

Layer 4 · Stock market narrative

Judgement: the dynamics that fix the direction of the case (the supply wave, the Suez return, the turn in rates, negative free cash flow, the capital commitments) are articulated by every covering house and, on the evidence of ratings, positioning and the price, are known to investors: on direction this is a consensus bear case. What is not articulated falls into three groups. First, the dynamics that fix depth and duration: decaying interest income, the tax floor, the share of trough EBIT that is an accounting change, the thin impairment headroom under the book-value anchor, and 2029 as a second trough year. Second, the optics of 2027: two more results showing growth, guidance that can be raised in a falling year, "contracts up". Third, one dynamic on which we are more optimistic than the bears: the reversal of the 2026 congestion cost. Only the third is favourable, and it is small against the rate. The narrative a buyer will eventually need, a purchase at 0.4-0.5 times book as idling and scrapping begin, is not yet in print. My files hold no direct evidence of investor commentary (none from Twitter or the buy side); I infer what investors know from positioning and price, and the Consumer should treat that half of the answer as inference.

Dynamic Sell-side commentary Reflected in numbers or price? Horizon
Supply wave (orderbook 44% of fleet; fleet +7% and +13%) Universal and prominent (Morgan Stanley "Anchored by Oversupply"; Bernstein; DNB Carnegie; Deutsche Bank) In the narrative fully; in the median numbers partly 0-18 months
Suez return Universal; tracked weekly Direction yes. Pace probably not: most notes describe a gradual return through 2027, while transits were at 73% of 2023 levels on 6 November 0-6 months

Tax that does not fall with profit Modelled explicitly only by DNB Carnegie Not in the median 12-36 months
Decay of interest income Stated by Morgan Stanley; modelled in opposite directions by DNB Carnegie and Bernstein Not in the median (USD 30-40 a share) 12-36 months
Ocean impairment headroom of 1.1bn In the annual report; mentioned by DNB Carnegie and Morgan Stanley; the 0.2-point sensitivity seldom quoted Not in book-value forecasts (consensus book value per share flat at USD 4,210-4,270) 3-15 months

Congestion cost in 2026 and its reversal The cost is known since Q3; the reversal as a cushion of about 4bn is not articulated, and the bears assume sticky cost Our Ocean EBITDA for FY2028 (2.1bn) is above DNB Carnegie's (about nil) and below the consensus mean (4.2bn) 6-24 months
Share loss and forwarder conflict Prominent at Bernstein; expert calls Yes; small in EBIT Continuing

Gemini's reliability below MSC's in August-September Trade press (5 November); brokers still cite 90% No; transitory 0-6 months
The optics of 2027 Not articulated in any report in my files No 3-12 months
The trough-on-trough purchase Hints only (DNB Carnegie's "valuation reset"; Morgan Stanley's bear value of DKK 10,000) The entry narrative does not exist yet 18-36 months
The bull narrative: structural landside bottlenecks Management; Jefferies; the Buy-rated houses; Bernstein engages with it ("Queuing theory", 9 November) while staying at Underperform In the high camp's numbers 0-18 months
  • What investors know, inferred. Short interest reached 33% of the free float in August 2025 and was squeezed; Deutsche Bank (6 November) puts systematic positioning at neutral and discretionary positioning at the bottom of its band; the shares rose about 51% in twelve months against a buy share of 7%. The price rose against the narrative, so the marginal holders of the last year were momentum and short-covering, not conviction. The absence of reward for the 5 November raise and the 22% fall from the high say the turn is being priced. I conclude that investors know the direction well and hold it with little exposure; I have no evidence that they have considered the depth-and-duration items.
  • What the price reflects. At about 0.69-0.70 times book the shares sit about half-way between the 0.8-1.0 times of peaks and the 0.4-0.5 times of troughs, and the mean target price is at the share price. The market has priced the turn and not the trough.
  • How the narrative is likely to move over 18 months. From "peak; when does it turn?" (now) to "how deep, how long?" (from August 2027). The items in the first unarticulated group become the debate at dated points: the annual report of 11 February 2027 (tax note, impairment headroom and sensitivities, lease and capital commitments), the FY2027 dividend, the end of the buyback in January 2028, and the first FY2028 range. Our assessment is therefore likely to become the narrative during the window, which is unfavourable for a holder and gives no reason to buy ahead of it.
  • Eighteen months to three years. The under-articulated negative is duration (2029). The unformed positive is the trough purchase; its markers (idle fleet above 4%, scrapping above 1% a year, an impairment taken, losses in consensus for two years, price near 0.4-0.5 times book) are observable and none is present. A separation of Terminals remains known, valued by brokers and without a sponsor.

Where I differ from the earlier sets. The first set (C-22 and its group judgement) describes a thin staircase of positives; I agree. Two additions. The items below EBIT are not unknown to the sell-side: DNB Carnegie has the tax, Morgan Stanley the interest, Bernstein a net financial expense; they are unevenly modelled, not unseen, which is a smaller claim than the earlier sets make. And there is one place where our view is kinder than the bears': the cost line.
