What each layer was asked, what its scores mean, and the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices

Layer 1: Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

Written 11 November 2026, 19:21 to 19:40 UK, Opus, ricky login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Maersk (MAERSK.B-DK): first set of questions, foundational components of the investment case

Prepared 11 November 2026 for the Investment Analyst answering the second set (the operating and financial model, 18 months to four years).

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Basis and data notes (read before using any figure below)

  • Price and value. DKK 19,895 on 11 November 2026 (dashboard), about USD 2,948 at roughly DKK 6.75 per USD; about 14.6m diluted shares, so a market value of about USD 43bn. FactSet's "market cap 279,148" and "enterprise value 265,170" in the financials block are a multi-class share artefact and must not be used. The price is 13% below the DKK 22,870 on which the 8 November memo was written, 22% below the 52-week high of DKK 25,380, 14% below the 50-day average (DKK 23,051), 5% above the 150-day (DKK 18,916) and 10% above the 200-day (DKK 18,117). Three-month return -3.9%, twelve-month +50.9%.
  • Book value. Consensus book value per share is USD 4,270 (FY2026) and USD 4,223 (FY2027), so the shares trade at about 0.69-0.70x book today (the memo's 0.8x was at DKK 22,870). Historical trough band 0.4-0.5x (2016, 2023); 0.6x as recently as August 2026.
  • Consensus (FactSet median, 11 November 2026; brokers' adjusted basis), USDm unless stated: revenue 65,070 / 59,779 / 58,959 / 62,645 (FY2026-FY2029); EBITDA 14,446 / 9,043 / 7,540 / 8,499; EBIT 8,173 / 2,748 / 786 / 2,274; net income 7,407 / 2,123 / 834 / 2,153; EPS USD 507 / 188 / 57 / 115; DPS USD 203 / 76 / 21 / 49; capex 5,250 / 5,500 / 5,953 / 4,900. FY2029 rests on two brokers and is not a consensus. The like-for-like base is FY2025 on the brokers' basis: revenue 53,988, EBITDA 9,572, EPS 181.63. Never compute growth from the FactSet history lines to the consensus lines.
  • FY2026 guidance (5 November 2026): underlying EBITDA USD 14.0-14.5bn, underlying EBIT 8.0-8.5bn, free cash flow above 3.0bn. Nine-month EBITDA is about 9.45-9.53bn, so the range implies Q4 2026 EBITDA of about 4.5-5.0bn against Q3's 4.71bn.
  • The one transmission constant. USD 100 per FFE on the full-year average Ocean loaded rate moves EBITDA and EBIT by about USD 1.3-1.36bn (about 13.6m FFE a year) and, because Ocean profit sits under tonnage tax, EPS by about USD 90-95. 100k FFE of volume moves EBIT by only about USD 10m; USD 100 a tonne of bunker by about 0.2-0.3bn before recovery; a 10% USD move by about 0.3bn. The non-Ocean earnings floor (Terminals plus Logistics & Services) is about USD 2.3-2.9bn of EBIT.
  • Traps in the data. (1) From 1 January 2026 vessel useful lives rose from 20 to 25 years, lowering depreciation by about USD 700m a year (about USD 175m a quarter, about USD 49 of EPS). Every EBIT and EPS figure from FY2026 on, reported and consensus, contains it. (2) FactSet's debt includes about USD 12.2bn of lease liabilities; the company's net cash excluding leases was about USD 15.5bn at end-2025, and net cash including leases USD 3.2bn at Q3 2026 (FactSet 2.7bn). (3) FactSet's historical free-cash-flow line does not reconcile with its own operating cash flow and capex lines in FY2023 and FY2024, and it never deducts lease repayments (about USD 3.5-3.9bn a year); use the company's definition (after leases) for any free-cash-flow judgement. (4) FactSet's segment revenue for Terminals jumps from 3,960 (FY2025 history) to 5,744 (FY2026 consensus), a definitional break, not growth. (5) Logistics & Services was re-segmented into Forwarding, Solutions and Landside from 1 April 2026; older segment history does not map.
  • Sources conflict on several figures (orderbook 38-44% of fleet; Suez transits 50-59% of 2023 levels; Q3 loaded volume -0.8% or -1.0%). Where they do, I give the range and the source I rely on.

The Investment Analyst's group headings, as written Group: External change forces · Group: Internal change forces · Group: Industry and Value Chain · Group: Company - Competitive advantage · Group: Company - Economic strength · Group: Company - Management · Group: Risks · Group: Guidance · Group: Sell-side estimates · Group: Stock market narrative

The Investment Analyst's closing notes

Overall steer for the second set (operating and financial model)

  1. Build the model on one driver. Ocean EBITDA = (realised average loaded rate × loaded FFE) minus cost per FFE, with USD 100 per FFE worth about USD 1.3-1.36bn of EBITDA and EBIT and about USD 90-95 of EPS. Realised rates follow spot at a beta of about 0.4-0.5 with a one-quarter lag; separate the profit-bearing base rate from fuel and surcharge pass-through (which leaves revenue with its cost).
  2. Rate path anchors (memo, as a starting point to test): FY2026 about 2,790 per FFE (Q3 3,287, Q4 about 3,200); FY2027 about 2,150 (Q1 2,600, Q2 2,200, Q3 1,950, Q4 1,860); FY2028 about 1,905 (trough); FY2029 about 1,960; FY2030 about 2,060. Bookends for the trough: 2019-like (about 2,100) and 2016-like (about 1,650).
  3. Volume: Maersk Ocean about 13.6-14.9m FFE, growing about 1-3% a year, below market until its own ships arrive in 2029-2030.
  4. Non-Ocean floor: Terminals EBITDA about 2.1-2.2bn flat in FY2027 (storage income fades), then +5-7% a year; Logistics & Services EBIT margin 5.1-5.8%, revenue +3-5% a year.
  5. Costs: self-help about 0.5-0.8bn of EBITDA by FY2028 in the base case; EU ETS rising to about 0.5bn by 2027; bunker recovery about 70% in a loose market; congestion relief lowers unit cost when rates fall.
  6. Below EBIT: depreciation about 6.3-7.0bn including the 0.7bn vessel-life benefit; net interest on a falling cash balance (about 40m per 1bn) against fixed lease interest of about 0.7bn; a tax charge of about 0.3-0.45bn even at near-zero profit; minorities about 0.1bn; about 14.3m shares, no new buyback unless announced.
  7. Cash: capex 5-6bn a year to 2028; lease repayments 3.5-3.9bn a year; dividend about 40% of underlying profit; working-capital drain of about 15-17% of any revenue decline.
  8. Scenarios, not a point: base about 50%, deeper (2016-type) about 30%, disruption-extended about 20%; carry an Ocean impairment through book value and depreciation in the base and deeper cases.
  9. Dates: 1 December 2026 (index inclusion); December-February (Asia-Europe contracts); 11 February 2027 (FY2026 results, FY2027 guidance, dividend, buyback, impairment headroom); May 2027 (Q1, transpacific contracts); August 2027 (Q2, first year-on-year decline).
Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 2

Research requests for the next set

Company: A.P. Møller - Mærsk A/S (MAERSK.B-DK), a Danish integrated container logistics group with three segments: Ocean (container shipping, about two-thirds of revenue, run with Hapag-Lloyd in the Gemini Cooperation), Logistics & Services (Forwarding, Solutions and Landside since April 2026) and Terminals (APM Terminals gateway ports).

Request: T-P1

Peers: Hapag-Lloyd (Gemini partner, closest listed pure container carrier, own cost programme); Mediterranean Shipping Company (private, largest carrier, volume-first price-setter); CMA CGM (private, integrated carrier with CEVA Logistics, fast fleet growth); COSCO Shipping Holdings (state-controlled, Ocean Alliance leader); Evergreen Marine and Yang Ming (listed Asian carriers with monthly disclosure); ZIM (high spot exposure, high cost, test of downturn cost flexibility); HMM and Ocean Network Express (Premier Alliance carriers); DSV and Kuehne + Nagel (asset-light forwarders, for the contrast in cost flexibility). Inputs: What share of a container carrier's Ocean cost base is fixed per sailing versus variable per box, using Maersk's Q1 2026 interim report split (terminal handling 39%, network costs excluding bunker 27%, bunker 19%, SG&A 9%) and comparable splits at Hapag-Lloyd and COSCO? How did Maersk's Ocean EBIT per FFE, unit cost per FFE at fixed bunker and loaded freight rate move through the 2015-2016, 2019 and 2023 downturns, quarter by quarter, and how fast did unit cost fall after rates fell? What did Maersk's past cost programmes promise and deliver (the 2016 programme, the 2023 programme of about 10,000 roles, the 2026 cut of about 1,000 corporate roles that Bernstein on 6 February 2026 put at about USD 180m of annual savings), and how long did each take? How much of Maersk's chartered fleet (416 chartered vessels at Q3 2026) comes up for redelivery in 2027 and 2028, at what charter rates, and how much of the roughly USD 12bn lease liability and the leases committed but not yet started is fixed into 2030 and beyond? How much did blank sailings, idling, slow steaming and charter redeliveries reduce costs in 2016 and 2023, and is that playbook smaller now that charter durations have lengthened (for example the three 9,500 TEU charters extended to 2031)? How much of the Gemini Cooperation's reported USD 950m of benefits (Maersk Q2 2026 earnings call) is structural cost reduction that survives a loose market, and why did Ocean unit cost at constant fuel rise 5% year on year in Q3 2026 (Bernstein, 5 November 2026)? How much do bunker surcharges and the Fossil Fuel Fee recover of fuel cost in loose versus tight markets? What is the cost per move trajectory in APM Terminals and the cost of filling warehousing whitespace in Solutions? Focus: How far can Maersk's Ocean unit cost per FFE fall in 2027-2028 as rates fall and congestion clears, and how much of the cost base is now locked by long charters, leases, fuel offtakes and depreciation compared with the 2016 and 2023 troughs.

Request: T-P2

Peers: Hapag-Lloyd (Gemini partner with a higher long-term contract share); Mediterranean Shipping Company (private, direct-call network and price-setter); CMA CGM (private, integrated, serves forwarders); COSCO Shipping Holdings (Ocean Alliance, transpacific strength); ZIM (most spot-exposed listed carrier); Evergreen Marine and Yang Ming (monthly revenue as a real-time pricing read); DSV and Kuehne + Nagel (the largest freight buyers and forwarders, Maersk's customers and competitors); terminal operators DP World, ICTSI and Hutchison Ports (for gateway tariff pricing). Operating conditions: a container supply wave with fleet growth well above demand growth (2027-2028); a return of container shipping to the Suez Canal and the Red Sea; a renewed route closure or port congestion episode; a falling or rising bunker price; rising carbon costs under EU ETS and FuelEU Maritime; a demand air pocket after tariff-driven front-loading. Inputs: How closely has Maersk's realised average loaded freight rate tracked the SCFI, CCFI and Drewry World Container Index in each direction since 2015, with what lag, and how did this differ from Hapag-Lloyd and COSCO? What is the current split of Maersk's Ocean volume between long-term contracts and spot or short-term rates (about 44% long-term according to company and broker material in 2026) and how has contract duration changed (annual to quarterly or half-yearly)? What is the status and likely outcome of the 2027 Asia-Europe and transpacific contract negotiations (COSCO has said it expects 2027 contracts to rise; Xeneta on 11 November 2026 expects spot rates to soften in 2027)? Is there evidence that shippers pay a premium for Gemini's schedule reliability (about 90% in its first year according to Hapag-Lloyd and Jefferies), and what happened to that premium when reliability fell during the 2026 congestion? How have surcharges (emergency bunker surcharges, war risk surcharges, EU ETS surcharges) held up in past loose markets? How much volume have forwarders shifted away from Maersk since its integrator strategy began, and at what price? How are APM Terminals' tariffs indexed, and how did revenue per move behave in 2016 and 2023? Did customers accept any green or low-emission freight premium in 2025-2026? Focus: How much of a fall in spot container rates in 2027-2028 will reach Maersk's realised Ocean rate and how quickly, and whether contracts, Gemini reliability or surcharge mechanisms give any measurable cushion compared with Hapag-Lloyd and MSC.

Request: T-P3

Why requested: The depth and length of the coming container trough depend on reinforcing loops (cash-funded ordering, delayed scrapping, share defence) and balancing loops (scrapping, idling, slow steaming, route changes) whose timing decides Maersk's earnings from 2027 to 2030. Peers: Hapag-Lloyd (Gemini partner, pure-play carrier with its own fleet plans); Mediterranean Shipping Company (largest orderbook, sets the industry's capacity behaviour); CMA CGM (fleet growth of about a third to 2028 according to Bernstein and Morgan Stanley); COSCO Shipping Holdings (state-backed, counter-cyclical ordering history); Evergreen Marine (high orderbook-to-fleet ratio); ZIM (charter-dependent, first to feel a downturn); Seaspan and Global Ship Lease (tonnage providers, for the charter-market loop); DSV (forwarder consolidation and buyer power). Inputs: Profit-to-orderbook: how much did record 2024-2026 profits feed new ordering (Morgan Stanley and DNB Carnegie put the orderbook at about 44% of the fleet in November 2026), and how did the same loop run after 2010 and 2021? Cash buffer and scrapping: with industry net cash of USD 26-30bn (Bernstein), how long did scrapping lag losses in 2015-2016 and 2023, and what scrapping volume would be needed to rebalance the market? Route-return cascade: as each carrier returns to Suez and gains a cost advantage, how fast do the others follow, and what is the evidence from the Gemini returns of September-October 2026 and the CMA CGM transits? Congestion loop: does congestion support rates while raising carrier costs and capping volume, and how quickly does it unwind once rates and volumes soften? Contract-shortening loop: are shorter contracts transmitting spot moves faster in both directions? Share defence: does Maersk's 26-ship order of 2026 signal a wider shift from margin to share among the more disciplined carriers? Gemini hub funnelling: does volume through Maersk-owned hubs reinforce APM Terminals' utilisation? Forwarder channel conflict: does Maersk's integrator strategy cause forwarders to divert volume in loose markets, and is this growing? Focus: Which loops will dominate in 2027-2029, how long the reinforcing ones (ordering, delayed scrapping, share defence) can run before the balancing ones (scrapping, idling, slow steaming) take over, and what dated evidence would show the turn.

Request: T-P4

Why requested: Maersk's earnings per share near the trough will be decided as much by items below EBIT (tax under tonnage taxation, interest on a falling cash balance, lease interest, minorities, buybacks, the vessel-life depreciation change and any impairment) as by operating profit, and these items are rarely modelled in detail. Peers: Hapag-Lloyd (tonnage tax, similar lease structure and cash use); COSCO Shipping Holdings (large net cash, tax regime contrast); Evergreen Marine (net cash carrier with high capex); ZIM (lease-heavy, net debt); DSV (asset-light comparator for tax, interest and buyback effects); Kuehne + Nagel (high payout comparator). Inputs: Tax: how does Maersk's tax charge behave when Ocean profit falls toward zero, given tonnage taxation of Ocean and ordinary taxation of Terminals and Logistics; what was the absolute tax charge in FY2016, FY2019 and FY2023 relative to pre-tax profit; and how is Hapag-Lloyd's tonnage-taxed effective rate (3.8% in FY2023) affected in loss years? Interest: what interest rate does Maersk earn on its cash and deposits (USD 18-21bn in 2026), what is the interest on its lease liabilities (USD 176m in Q1 2026), and what do sell-side models assume for net financial items in 2027-2028? Depreciation: what is the effect of the change in vessel useful lives from 20 to 25 years from 1 January 2026 (about USD 700m a year according to Maersk and Bernstein), what impairment headroom did the FY2025 annual report show for Ocean, and what discount-rate or rate assumptions would trigger an impairment? Capital returns: what does Maersk's dividend policy (30-50% of underlying net result) imply in a low-profit year, what is the status of the share buyback decision deferred to February 2027 (Morgan Stanley, 5 November 2026), and how did buybacks and dividends change in 2016-2017 and 2023-2024? Minorities and associates: what are the minority interests' share of profit and the contribution of joint ventures, mainly in APM Terminals? Shares: what is the current share count after cancellations (share capital reduced by more than DKK 1.1bn in Q2 2026)? What do the main brokers (Bernstein, Morgan Stanley, Deutsche Bank, DNB Carnegie, Jefferies) assume for each of these items, and how far apart are they? Focus: How the below-EBIT items will move Maersk's earnings per share and free cash flow per share in FY2027-FY2029 as operating profit falls, and which of them the sell-side models least consistently.

Layer 2: Translating the foundational components into an operating and financial model for the next 18 months, and 18 months to 3 years out

Written 12 November 2026, 01:46 to 02:10 UK, Opus, ricky login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years, based on the first set's answers, a base of the company's historical financial and operating performance, and how businesses in the sector and industry outperform or underperform, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a trustworthy judgement regarding the predictability of delivery of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Maersk (MAERSK.B-DK): second set of questions, the operating and financial model, 18 months to four years

Prepared 12 November 2026 for the Investment Analyst answering the third and fourth sets. Money in USD unless stated; the share price in DKK (DKK 19,895 on 11 November 2026, about USD 2,948 at DKK 6.75 per USD).

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Basis, and how to read the numbers in this file

  • One base case runs through every answer. It is mine, built bottom-up by quarter for the next six quarters and by year to FY2030, and entered in forecast-inputs.md. Headline: underlying EBITDA 14.15bn (FY2026), 8.17bn, 6.26bn, 6.71bn, 8.44bn (FY2030); underlying EPS about USD 517, 93, -62, -51, 48; company-basis free cash flow (after leases) +3.7bn, -0.7bn, -3.5bn, -3.3bn, -1.3bn. FactSet consensus (median, 11 November 2026): EBITDA 14.45 / 9.04 / 7.54 / 8.50bn (FY2029 two brokers); EPS 507 / 188 / 57 / 115. The 8 November memo (the first set's source): EBITDA 14.05 / 8.4 / 6.3 / 7.15 / 8.5bn; EPS 486 / 112 / -46 / -15 / 52.
  • Bases. EBITDA and EPS are on the company's "underlying" basis, which is the brokers' basis (FY2025 base: EBITDA 9,572m, EPS 181.63). FactSet's history lines (operating EBITDA 9,200m in FY2025; reported EPS 178.99) are a different basis and must not be used to compute growth into my numbers. "Company-basis EBIT" includes the share of profit of joint ventures and associates (about 0.43bn a year, mostly APM Terminals), as Maersk's guidance does; "model EBIT" (EBITDA less D&A) does not, and in the forecast form the joint-venture share and minorities are carried in the net financial items line (explained in Part 4 of the form).
  • The vessel-life change (20 to 25 years from 1 January 2026) lowers D&A by about 0.7bn a year (about USD 50 a share). Every FY2026-FY2030 figure here, mine and consensus, contains it.
  • The one transmission constant carried forward from the first set and re-tested here: USD 100 per FFE on the full-year average loaded rate is about 1.36-1.39bn of EBITDA and EBIT (13.6-13.9m loaded FFE) and about USD 95-97 of EPS, because the marginal tax on Ocean profit is close to nil.
  • Scenarios (first set's weights, which I keep): base 50%; deeper, 2016-type price war into a fast Suez return, 30%; disruption-extended, renewed Red Sea or Hormuz closure, 20%.
  • Where I disagree with the first set I say so inside the answer, under "Where I differ from the first set". The five that matter most: working capital releases cash in a falling-revenue year rather than draining it (P-08); a USD 1bn buyback in 2027 is the base case, not no buyback (P-02); FY2029 is a second trough year rather than the start of recovery (P-05); the Ocean cost line falls by about a third of the rate fall, so drop-through over the cycle is about 70%, not about 100% (P-01); and Terminals EBITDA should be held flat in FY2027, not grown, because the Terminals pricing record in overcapacity is weaker than the first set allowed (P-04).

The operating drivers behind the inputs

  • Ocean is built as volume times (average loaded rate less a cash cost per FFE). The cash cost per FFE (bunker plus network, handling and overhead, net of Ocean's other revenue) is calibrated on reported quarters: about 1,770-1,810 in FY2025 and Q2 2025, about 1,800 in Q1 2026, 2,139 in Q2 2026 and 2,193 in Q3 2026 (bunker +25-44%, congestion, Hormuz contingencies). It falls to about 2,100 (Q4 2026), 1,950 (Q1 2027), 1,850, 1,800, 1,780 (Q4 2027) and 1,770 (FY2028) as congestion clears, the Suez return cuts fuel per FFE by about 8-10%, oil eases (Brent about 85 in 2027, 75 from 2028) and self-help arrives (about 0.6bn by FY2027 and 1.0bn by FY2028), partly offset by about 3% a year of handling, labour, EU ETS and canal-toll inflation; then 1,795 (FY2029) and 1,800 (FY2030).
  • Average loaded rate (USD/FFE): Q4 2026 3,200; Q1 2027 2,600; Q2 2,200; Q3 1,975; Q4 1,875; Q1 2028 1,880; FY2026 about 2,840; FY2027 about 2,155; FY2028 1,920; FY2029 1,960; FY2030 2,060. Basis: fleet growth of 7.4% (2027) and 13.2% (2028) on Bernstein's count against demand of 2-4%; Suez transits at 73% of 2023 levels in November 2026 and all Maersk services expected back by March 2027; realised rates following spot at a beta of 0.4-0.5 with about a one-quarter lag; long-term contracts 46-47% of volume and shortening; about 5.9m TEU of the 14.9m TEU orderbook still to deliver after 2028, so 2029 remains a trough year; the rate floor at the marginal carrier's cash cost, where Maersk's Ocean EBITDA sat in the 2023 trough (about 150 per FFE).
  • Loaded volume (m FFE): 13.3 (FY2026), 13.6, 13.9, 14.2, 14.7; the Suez return frees Maersk's own capacity from the 96% utilisation cap of Q3 2026 and Maersk prices to fill it; own newbuilds (483,600 TEU) from 2029. 100k FFE is worth only about 10m of EBIT.
  • Sensitivity: USD 100 per FFE on the annual average rate is about 1.36-1.47bn of EBITDA (13.6-14.7m FFE), about 2.6 points of FY2028 group EBITDA margin and about USD 95-100 of EPS.
  • Terminals: EBITDA flat in FY2027 (congestion storage income fades; the 2016 precedent of EBIT per TEU -33% in overcapacity), then +5-6% a year on indexed tariffs (revenue per move +6.4% against cost per move +5.7% in Q3 2026) and the 3bn capacity programme.
  • Logistics & Services: revenue +1-4% a year (forwarding revenue falls with freight rates), EBIT margin 5.5-5.8%, below the 6% target; Solutions whitespace filling about 0.1bn by FY2028.
  • D&A: 6.3bn in FY2026 (including the 0.7bn reduction from the vessel-life change to 25 years), drifting to 6.9bn by FY2030 as about 9.3bn of committed leases commence, capex runs at 5.2-5.8bn and the newbuilds arrive. An Ocean impairment (headroom 1.1bn at end-2025) is not in the base; 2-4bn would lower D&A by about 0.15-0.25bn a year.
  • Net financial items (the line in Part 1) are a composite, because the model has no separate lines for joint ventures or minorities: net interest and other financial items (-140m, -265m, -480m, -650m, -775m: interest income on a falling cash pile at 3.3-3.8%, about 0.73-0.75bn of lease interest, about 0.26-0.34bn of interest on borrowings) plus the share of profit of joint ventures and associates (+430m, +420m, +440m, +460m, +480m, mostly APM Terminals) less minority interests (-120m, -130m, -130m, -140m, -150m). This makes the model's pre-tax profit less tax equal to underlying net profit attributable to shareholders.
  • Tax: set as an absolute charge (520m, 420m, 400m, 400m, 420m), because tonnage tax on Ocean and ordinary tax on Terminals and Logistics do not fall with group profit (FY2016: tax 1.05bn on a pre-tax loss of 714m). The rates in Part 1 are that charge divided by the model's pre-tax profit; they are negative in FY2028-FY2029 because pre-tax profit is negative while the charge is not. If the model's pre-tax profit differs from mine, keep the absolute charge, not the rate.
  • Dividend: 40% of underlying EPS for the year (the policy is 30-50%; 40% used in practice), nil in a loss year; USD, declared for the fiscal year and paid the following March (FY2026 about DKK 1,400 at DKK 6.75).
  • Shares: 14.40m outstanding at 30 September 2026 (14.70m nominal after the June 2026 cancellation, less 0.297m treasury); about 14.36m at end-2026; the change line is vesting from treasury only.
  • Capex: 4.7bn in FY2026 (3.1bn in nine months) and 5.8bn in FY2027, inside the company's 10-11bn guidance for 2026-2027; 5.7-5.8bn in FY2028-FY2029 (the 26 dual-fuel newbuilds, Terminals programme) and 5.2bn in FY2030.
  • Working capital is on FactSet's year-end basis (+1.4% at end-2024, -0.3% at end-2025). Rising rates absorbed about 1.3-1.6bn in 2026; falling rates release it (FY2023: operating cash flow 10.8bn against EBITDA 9.3bn as revenue fell 37%). If the model's base year uses FactSet's quarterly definition (about -16.6% of revenue at Q3 2026), apply the same changes in points (+2.8, -1.5, -0.5, 0, +0.2) rather than these levels.
  • Lease payments are the repayment of lease liabilities (principal) only; lease interest is in net financial items. Lease liabilities 12.58bn at Q3 2026; undiscounted commitments 17.78bn at end-2025.
  • Acquisitions less disposals: FY2026 includes the sale of Maersk Training and H2S Safety Services and of 25% of APM Terminals Maasvlakte II to Hapag-Lloyd (completed November 2026), whose proceeds are not in my files: the +300m is a placeholder of plausible size and is immaterial to the case; thereafter small terminal concessions and stakes.
  • Buybacks: the DKK 6.3bn 2026 programme (0.84bn spent in nine months) finishes on 29 January 2027; I assume a new 1bn programme announced on 11 February 2027 (the February 2026 precedent: a 1bn programme launched while guiding an EBIT loss), and none after the January 2028 tail.
  • Scenarios not in these inputs: deeper (30%): FY2027 EBITDA about 4.7bn and EPS about -160, FY2028 about 3.1bn and -295; disruption-extended (20%): FY2027 about 13.7bn and +480, FY2028 about 9.2bn and +145.

The bridge from the company's own reported figures to the basis consensus uses

  • Consensus is on Maersk's "underlying" basis, which excludes restructuring costs, gains and losses on sales of businesses and assets, and impairments. FY2025: underlying (brokers') EBITDA 9,572m against FactSet's operating EBITDA 9,200m (+4.0%); brokers' EPS 181.63 against reported basic EPS 178.99 and FactSet's diluted EPS before unusual items 149.31. My EBITDA margin and EPS inputs are on the underlying basis; the FY2026 base for growth is FactSet's FY2025 revenue (53,986m, the same as the brokers' 53,988m).
  • EBIT: Maersk's underlying EBIT (and its guidance) includes the share of profit of joint ventures and associates (about 0.43bn a year). The model's EBIT (EBITDA less D&A) does not; I carry that share in net financial items, so pre-tax profit and EPS are right while the model's EBIT is about 0.42-0.48bn below the company's.
  • Free cash flow: Maersk defines free cash flow after capex, lease principal and lease and other interest; FactSet's free cash flow (operating cash flow less fixed-asset capex) deducts no leases, so consensus "free cash flow" (4,653m / 4,175m / 479m for FY2026-FY2028) is about 4.2bn a year higher than the company basis. My cross-check below is on the company basis.
  • Debt: FactSet's debt (17,889m at Q3 2026) includes 12,583m of lease liabilities; Maersk's "net cash excluding leases" was about 15.5bn at end-2025 and is about 17.5bn at end-2026 on my numbers.
  • Vessel-life change: from 1 January 2026 vessel useful lives rose from 20 to 25 years, lowering D&A by about 0.7bn a year (527m in the first nine months of 2026); FY2026-FY2030 reported and consensus EBIT and EPS contain it, FY2025 and earlier do not.
  • Dividends: FactSet's dividend history is dated by payment period; my DPS is for the fiscal year declared (paid the following March), which is how consensus DPS (202.99 for FY2026) is built.
  • Share count: brokers' FY2026 EPS implies about 14.6m shares (FactSet diluted); outstanding shares are 14.40m (30 September 2026), so my EPS is about 1% higher than consensus would be for the same net income.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 3 and Layer 4

Research requests for the next set

Company: A.P. Møller - Mærsk A/S (MAERSK.B-DK), a Danish integrated container logistics group with three segments: Ocean (container shipping, about two-thirds of revenue, operated with Hapag-Lloyd in the Gemini Cooperation), Logistics & Services (Forwarding, Solutions and Landside since April 2026) and Terminals (APM Terminals gateway ports)

Request: T-D1

Peers: Hapag-Lloyd (Gemini partner and closest listed pure carrier, with its own cost programme and a higher long-term contract share); Mediterranean Shipping Company (private, largest carrier, volume-first price-setter with a direct-call network); CMA CGM (private, integrated carrier with CEVA Logistics, fast fleet growth); COSCO Shipping Holdings (state-controlled, Ocean Alliance leader, integrated terminals); Evergreen Marine and Yang Ming (listed Asian carriers whose monthly revenue gives a real-time read); ZIM (charter-heavy, spot-exposed, the test of downturn cost flexibility); HMM and Ocean Network Express (Premier Alliance carriers); DSV and Kuehne + Nagel (asset-light forwarders, Maersk's largest customers and competitors in logistics); DP World, ICTSI and Hutchison Ports (gateway terminal operators, for the Terminals segment) Operating conditions: a container supply wave with fleet growth well above demand growth (2027-2028); the full return of container shipping to the Suez Canal and the Red Sea; a renewed Red Sea or Strait of Hormuz closure; port congestion episodes and their unwinding; a falling or rising bunker price; rising carbon costs under EU ETS and FuelEU Maritime; a demand air pocket after tariff-driven front-loading; previous downturns in 2015-2016, 2019 and 2023 Inputs: How fast and how far did Maersk cut Ocean unit cost at fixed bunker in past downturns, quarter by quarter (for example from USD 2,552 per FFE in Q1 2023 to 2,287 in Q3 2023, per Maersk's 2023 investor presentations), and how much of that came from bunker, congestion relief, network actions and overhead? Can that be repeated in 2027-2028 now that charter durations have lengthened (Costamare charters to Maersk running to October 2029 and May 2030; Maersk Puelo to September 2031) and about USD 9.3bn of leases were committed but not started at end-2025 (Maersk Annual Report 2025)? How much of the Cape-of-Good-Hope routing and 2026 congestion cost (Q3 2026 Ocean unit cost at constant fuel +5% year on year, container handling +9.3%, network cost excluding bunker +12%; Maersk Q3 2026 interim report and Bernstein, 5 November 2026) unwinds when routes and ports normalise, and how quickly? What did Maersk's 2016, 2023 and February 2026 cost programmes promise and deliver, and over what period? Is the Gemini Cooperation's reported USD 950m of benefits structural in a loose market? Can APM Terminals keep revenue per move rising faster than cost per move in an overcapacity period (Jefferies, January 2017, reported a 33% fall in APM Terminals' EBIT per TEU in the 2016 downturn)? Has Logistics & Services ever reached its 6% EBIT margin target, and what did forwarders that improved margins (DSV after UTi and Panalpina; Kuehne + Nagel) do that Maersk has not? Has any carrier with a strong balance sheet kept paying dividends and buying back shares through a loss-making year, and what did Maersk do in February 2016, February 2024 and February 2026? Focus: Whether Maersk's demonstrated strengths (cost programmes, Gemini, APM Terminals, net cash) can hold Ocean EBITDA positive and limit losses through a 2027-2028 supply wave, or whether the plan relies on strengths it has not shown in a comparable downturn, such as logistics margin delivery, terminal pricing in overcapacity and fleet discipline.

Request: T-D2

Peers: Hapag-Lloyd (same freight market and Gemini network, different contract mix and tonnage tax structure); COSCO Shipping Holdings (state-backed, largest orderbook relative to fleet); ZIM (most spot-exposed listed carrier, the fastest transmission of rate falls); Evergreen Marine and Yang Ming (monthly revenue data, net cash carriers); Mediterranean Shipping Company and CMA CGM (private price-setters whose capacity decisions set the industry's rates); DSV and Kuehne + Nagel (asset-light comparators whose earnings are less concentrated in one driver) Operating conditions: a supply wave meeting a Suez return in 2027; a 2016-type price war; a 2019-type moderate trough; a renewed route closure extending high rates; falling or rising oil prices; high or falling interest rates on cash deposits; an Ocean asset impairment Inputs: How concentrated is the change in Maersk's earnings in the average loaded freight rate, against volume, unit cost, bunker, Terminals and Logistics, using the company's own sensitivities (about USD 1.3bn of EBIT per USD 100 per FFE, Deutsche Bank, 3 November 2026; 100k FFE of volume about USD 10m)? How wide has the gap been between Maersk's initial annual EBITDA guidance, the consensus a year ahead and the outcome in 2016, 2019, 2023, 2024, 2025 and 2026 (FY2026 guidance moved from USD 4.5-7.0bn in February 2026 to 14.0-14.5bn in November 2026)? How far and how fast did Maersk's realised loaded rate fall after the 2015 and 2022 peaks, and how did the lag to the SCFI, CCFI and Drewry WCI behave? How do the items below operating profit behave when Ocean profit falls toward zero: tax under tonnage taxation (FY2016 tax of USD 1.05bn on a pre-tax loss of USD 714m, DNB Carnegie, 7 February 2024), interest income as cash falls, lease interest, minorities and joint-venture income? How widely do brokers disagree (FactSet broker EPS for FY2028 ranged from about USD -394 to +282 on 10 November 2026), and which assumptions explain the spread? How many container fleet deliveries remain for 2029-2031 after the 2027-2028 peak, and how much scrapping would be needed to balance the market (Bernstein, April 2026, projects about 2.5m TEU over three years)? How often have forecasts resting on one driver been met for container carriers? Focus: How wide and how skewed the range of Maersk's FY2027 and FY2028 EBITDA and EPS outcomes is, and whether the sign of trough-year EPS is decided by the freight rate alone or also by tax, interest and depreciation items that brokers model inconsistently.

Request: T-I1

Peers: Hapag-Lloyd (Gemini partner; its guidance and 2027 outlook are the closest read-across); COSCO Shipping Holdings (has said it expects 2027 contracts to rise); ZIM, Evergreen Marine, Yang Ming and HMM (listed carriers with recent guidance and monthly data); DSV and Kuehne + Nagel (forwarders' commentary on 2027 contract tenders and volumes); Mediterranean Shipping Company and CMA CGM (private; their capacity and pricing behaviour as reported by brokers and the trade press) Operating conditions: the Asia-Europe contract season (December 2026 to February 2027) and the transpacific season (May 2027); the full return of Gemini services to Suez (the Suez Canal Authority expects all Maersk services back by March 2027); the 2027 delivery wave; a renewed route closure; port congestion; a post-front-loading demand air pocket Inputs: How has Maersk set its first annual guidance each February (2016, 2019, 2023, 2024, 2025 and 2026) relative to consensus and to the final outcome, and has an in-year range ever been cut under the current chief executive? What will the 11 February 2027 release contain: FY2027 EBITDA, EBIT and free cash flow guidance, the FY2026 dividend, the share buyback decision deferred to February 2027 (Morgan Stanley, 5 November 2026), the Ocean impairment test (headroom of USD 1.1bn at end-2025 against 4.1bn a year earlier, Maersk Annual Report 2025)? Where does FactSet consensus sit (FY2027 EBITDA median USD 9,043m and EPS USD 188, FY2028 EBITDA 7,540m and EPS 57, on 11 November 2026) against the company's Q3 2026 run-rate and against the guidance implied for Q4 2026? What are the net revisions (FY2027 EPS +20% over one month and +496% over three months, target price -2% over one month, FactSet to 11 November 2026) likely to do as realised rates fall? What do peers' latest results and outlooks (Hapag-Lloyd, COSCO, ZIM, Evergreen, HMM) imply for 2027? What are Xeneta, Drewry and brokers reporting on the 2027 Asia-Europe and transpacific contract negotiations? What would need to happen for FY2027 guidance to be set above consensus? Focus: Whether Maersk's first FY2027 guidance on 11 February 2027 is likely to be set below, in line with or above the current consensus, and how far and how fast FY2027 and FY2028 consensus is likely to move over the next two to three reporting events.

Request: T-I2

Peers: Hapag-Lloyd (same freight exposure, tonnage-taxed, Gemini partner); COSCO Shipping Holdings (Ocean Alliance, large net cash); ZIM (spot-exposed, charter-heavy); Evergreen Marine (net cash with high capex); DSV and Kuehne + Nagel (forwarders whose narratives centre on self-help rather than freight rates); Wallenius Wilhelmsen and Hoegh Autoliners (shipping cohort peers at the dearest points of their valuation ranges) Operating conditions: a peaking freight cycle with a record orderbook; the Suez return; a renewed disruption; a risk-off equity market with defensives leading; a crowded long position in shipping equities after large twelve-month gains Inputs: the effect of Maersk's tax charge not falling with profit under tonnage taxation; the decline of interest income as Maersk's cash is spent on capex, leases and dividends; the USD 700m a year depreciation benefit from extending vessel lives to 25 years and its share of forecast trough-year EBIT; the Ocean impairment headroom of USD 1.1bn at end-2025; how Maersk's working capital behaves when freight rates fall; capital commitments of USD 19.5bn at Q3 2026 and the order for 26 dual-fuel 18,600 TEU ships for 2029-2030; whether Maersk will launch a new buyback in February 2027 after launching a USD 1bn programme in February 2026 while guiding an EBIT loss; the orderbook still due for delivery in 2029-2031 after the 2027-2028 peak; the cost of 2026 port congestion to Maersk (Q3 2026 Ocean unit cost at constant fuel +5%) and whether it reverses; forwarder channel conflict and Maersk's market share at a twenty-year low (13.7% per DAOL Investment & Securities, mid-2026); the value of APM Terminals inside the group (Morgan Stanley, July 2025); Gemini reliability against MSC in August-September 2026 (Sea-Intelligence) Focus: Which of these dynamics, especially those below operating profit and in cash flow, are discussed by sell-side analysts and investors and which by no one, so that it is clear what the current narrative on Maersk has and has not priced.

Request: OWN-1

Question: How has A.P. Møller - Mærsk's working capital behaved in years when container freight rates and revenue fell (2009, 2012, 2015-2016, 2019 and 2023): what were cash flow from operations against EBITDA, and the movements in trade receivables, bunker inventories and trade payables, in each year; what explains the difference between FactSet's quarterly and year-end net working capital figures for Maersk; and how did Hapag-Lloyd's and COSCO Shipping Holdings' working capital behave in the same years?

Request: OWN-2

Question: For A.P. Møller - Mærsk's Ocean segment, how much of the additional cost of Cape of Good Hope routing and of the 2026 port congestion (extra vessels and charters, bunker consumption, container handling, war-risk insurance) unwinds as services return to the Suez Canal, what canal tolls are added back, how quickly did costs fall on the Gemini services that returned from September 2026, and what does Maersk's chartered-fleet redelivery schedule for 2027-2029 (416 chartered vessels at Q3 2026) allow in capacity and cost reduction, compared with Hapag-Lloyd and ZIM?

Request: OWN-3

Question: What is the container ship delivery schedule for 2029, 2030 and 2031 by carrier and by vessel size after the record orderbook of 2026 (about 14.9m TEU, Jefferies, November 2026), what share of it is ordered by Mediterranean Shipping Company, CMA CGM, COSCO and A.P. Møller - Mærsk, what are the precedents for order cancellations, deferrals and accelerated scrapping (2009-2011, 2016-2017, 2023-2024), and what do brokers and Clarksons, Alphaliner and Drewry project for net fleet growth and freight rates in 2029-2030?

Request: OWN-4

Question: What assumptions did A.P. Møller - Mærsk use in the FY2025 impairment test for its Ocean cash-generating unit (freight rates, volumes, terminal growth, pre-tax discount rate of 9.8%, headroom of USD 1.1bn after the change in vessel useful lives from 20 to 25 years), how sensitive is the headroom to each assumption, what impairments did Maersk and other carriers (Hapag-Lloyd, ZIM, Evergreen, HMM) book in 2016 and 2023, and how do brokers expect the FY2026 and FY2027 tests to come out?

Møller - Mærsk decided its dividends and share buybacks each February since 2016 relative to its guidance, net cash and the freight outlook (including the February 2024 suspension and the USD 1bn programme of February 2026), what have the new CFO Robert Erni and the chair said about capital allocation since February 2026, what are A.P. Moller Holding's own cash needs and distribution expectations, and what do brokers expect Maersk to announce on 11 February 2027?

Request: OWN-6

Question: How did APM Terminals' revenue per move, cost per move, EBITDA margin, storage income and return on invested capital behave in the container downturns of 2009, 2016, 2019 and 2023, how are its concession tariffs indexed and capped by jurisdiction, how much of its 2026 earnings came from congestion-related storage income, and what do the results of DP World, ICTSI, Hutchison Port Holdings Trust and COSCO Shipping Ports imply for A.P. Møller - Mærsk's Terminals earnings in an overcapacity period in 2027-2028?

Layer 3 and Layer 4: Assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months / Assessing the extent to which our assessment of the foundational components and financial forecasts is reflected or not in current company guidance, sell-side estimates and stock market narrative, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to 3 years out

Written 12 November 2026, 10:21 to 10:47 UK, Fable, ricky login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a robustly tested assessment of the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months, based on the first and second sets' answers.

The objective is to produce that judgement, based on the first and second sets' answers and your answers to the third set, for the Investment Analyst who writes the investment memo, so that Richard, who reads the memo, can form his judgement on the investment case.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Maersk (MAERSK.B-DK): third set of questions (the predictability of the forecasts) and fourth set (what guidance, sell-side estimates and the narrative already reflect)

Prepared 12 November 2026 for the Investment Analyst who writes the investment memo. Money in USD unless stated; the share price in DKK (DKK 19,895 on 11 November 2026 on the dashboard; the Financial Analyst's model uses DKK 19,785; about USD 2,948 at DKK 6.75 per USD).

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Basis: the forecasts I test, the cross-check gaps, and the three inputs I change

The forecasts I test are the model's (update 1), which reproduce the second set's base case. The second row of each pair is my own arithmetic on the model's figures after the three input changes below; the next model update gives the figures of record.

FY2026 FY2027 FY2028 FY2029 FY2030
EBITDA (bn) 14.15 8.18 6.26 6.72 8.45
Company-basis EBIT, including joint ventures (bn) 8.28 2.15 0.15 0.48 2.03
Earnings per share, model update 1 493 93 -61 -51 48
Earnings per share after my changes 493 89 -67 -56 43
Equity free cash flow, model update 1 (bn) 3.46 -0.57 -3.32 -3.12 -1.16
Equity free cash flow after my changes (bn) 3.91 -1.02 -3.46 -3.20 -1.24
Consensus EBITDA, median (bn) 14.45 9.04 7.54 8.50 (two brokers) none
Consensus earnings per share, median 507 188 57 115 (two brokers) none
  • Use company-basis EBIT when comparing with guidance or consensus. The model's EBITA excludes about 0.42-0.48bn a year of joint-venture profit that Maersk's guidance and the brokers include; the model summary's "-37%" and "-137%" against consensus EBIT overstate the difference (on the company basis: about -22% in FY2027 and about -80% in FY2028).
  • Scenarios. I keep the earlier sets' weights for arithmetic: base 50%, deeper (a 2016-type price war into a fast Suez return) 30%, disruption-extended 20%. The news of the last week argues for shading the third toward 15%: Suez containership transits were at 73% of 2023 levels on 6 November (Morgan Stanley), services carrying 63% of Far East-Europe capacity already route through the canal (Alphaliner, cited by Ernst Russ, 12 November) and Evergreen, one of the holdouts on which the first set relied for a staggered release, has returned (reported 12 November). The disruption-extended case now requires a new closure that reverses a return in progress, not the persistence of the present state.
  • Errors in the AlphaSense reports that the Consumer should not carry forward. "Lease liabilities surged from 13.4bn to 19.5bn" is the capital commitments figure; lease liabilities were 12.6bn at Q3 2026. The February 2026 buyback was about USD 1bn (DKK 6.3bn), not "DKK 1bn". Gemini's reliability is quoted both as 93.4% (an article of 25 August) and as 68.4% for August-September against 68.9% for MSC (Sea-Intelligence, 5 November); the later figure is the one to use. The co-movement table supplied for D-07 is empty ("no price history found").

The cross-check gaps in the model summary, explained.

  1. FY2026 earnings per share: 517 in the second set's cross-check, 493 in the model. The model's pre-tax profit (8,020m) is the cross-check's, so the whole gap is tax: the model's net income (7,120m) implies a charge of about 900m, 11.2% of pre-tax profit; the cross-check used 520m, 6.5%. The 6.5% input gives 517 only if applied to the whole year; the model evidently carries FactSet's actual tax for the three reported quarters and applies the input to the fourth quarter alone, which implies about 0.7bn of tax in the nine months to September. I judge the model nearer right, for three reasons. (a) The FY2025 tax expense was USD 556m, an effective rate of 16.0% (Annual Report 2025, cited in the AlphaSense report on the narrative), not the "about 0.42bn" both earlier sets took from FactSet's derived rate, and taxable land profits are higher in 2026. (b) FactSet's nine-month EBIT (4,833m) plus about 0.35bn of joint-venture income, less about 0.1bn each of net financial items and minorities, exceeds nine-month reported net income (4,302m) by about 0.7bn, which is the room for tax and unusual items. (c) The cross-check's 517 is above consensus (507) although its EBITDA is 0.3bn (about USD 20 a share) below consensus; only a tax charge that is too low explains that, and consensus itself implies tax plus minorities of about 0.97bn (pre-tax 8,376m less net income 7,407m). The model's reported quarters are on the Viewforth definition, not the brokers' underlying basis, so I take FY2026 underlying earnings per share as about 495-505. No FY2026 input is wrong (6.5% is reasonable for the fourth quarter); the cross-check was.
  2. Free cash flow: the model is 0.13-0.17bn a year better than the cross-check in FY2027-FY2030 and 0.23bn worse in FY2026. The model's equity free cash flow is EBITDA less tax, plus the composite net financial items line, less capital spending and lease payments, plus the change in working capital (I reproduce every year to within 1m). The composite line carries the share of joint-venture profit (420-480m) as if it were cash, where the cross-check counts dividends received (about 300m), and the model has no line for the about 100m a year of restructuring and other cash items in the cross-check; minorities are treated differently too. These are differences of definition, they account for the gap to within about 50m a year, and they show no input error. In FY2026 they are outweighed by the model's higher tax (about 380m).

The three inputs I change (in inputs-changed.md; reasons in D-01).

  • Tax rate: an absolute charge of about 0.48bn in FY2027-FY2029 and 0.50bn in FY2030, in place of 0.40-0.42bn (rates of 27.4%, -104.1%, -153.6% and 45.3% on the model's pre-tax profit; FY2026 unchanged at 6.5%).
  • Working capital (% of revenue): 1.8 and 0.9 at the end of FY2026 and FY2027 in place of 2.5 and 1.0; later years unchanged. This moves about 0.45bn of free cash flow from FY2027 into FY2026 and leaves the cumulative figure to FY2028 unchanged.
  • Dividend per share: 200, 35, 0, 0, 17 in place of 207, 37, 0, 0, 19, which keeps the payout at 40% of the revised earnings.

A correction to the earlier sets that runs through my answers: where our difference from consensus comes from. The second set says the items below EBIT explain more than half of its FY2028 gap to consensus. Medians of different lines do not add (consensus EBITDA of 7,540m less consensus depreciation of 6,388m is 1,152m, not the EBIT median of 786m), so I measure the gap another way: I put consensus EBITDA through our own lines below EBITDA. That gives earnings per share of about 149 in FY2027 (consensus 188; ours 89) and about +24 in FY2028 (consensus 57; ours -67). So about 60% of the FY2027 gap and about 75% of the FY2028 gap is EBITDA, which is the Ocean rate; about USD 40 a share in FY2027 and USD 33 in FY2028 is below EBITDA, nearly all of it net financial items. Broker by broker, our lines below EBITDA are already Morgan Stanley's: its FY2027 EBITDA of 7,965m gives its earnings per share of 73.8, and through our lines would give 74.

The third set: Assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months

The Investment Analyst's heading (Layer 4) The fourth set: Assessing the extent to which our assessment is reflected or not in current company guidance, sell-side estimates and stock market narrative
Appendix Forecast inputs the Investment Analyst changed before the memo

Changed forecast inputs: A.P. Moller - Maersk A/S Class B (MAERSK.B-DK), as at 12-Nov-26

Money in millions of USD, except per-share figures; costs and outflows negative. Only the lines I change are shown, each in full; every other input of the second set stands.

Part 1. Profit and loss inputs, one column for each of the five fiscal years

Input FY2026 (year to 31-Dec-26) FY2027 (year to 31-Dec-27) FY2028 (year to 31-Dec-28) FY2029 (year to 31-Dec-29) FY2030 (year to 31-Dec-30)
Tax rate (%) 6.5 27.4 -104.1 -153.6 45.3

The absolute charge is raised to about 480 in each of FY2027, FY2028 and FY2029 and about 500 in FY2030 (each rate is that charge divided by the model's pre-tax profit of 1,752, -461, -312 and 1,103; if the model's pre-tax profit differs, keep the absolute charge, not the rate), because the reported FY2025 tax expense was 556 (Annual Report 2025) and not the 0.42bn the second set took from FactSet's derived rate, the model's own FY2026 figures imply a charge of about 900, DNB Carnegie's published rates imply about 480 in both 2027 and its loss year 2028, and the taxable profits of Terminals and Logistics & Services are rising; FY2026 is unchanged at 6.5 because the model already carries the tax of the three reported quarters and applies the input to the fourth quarter only.

Input FY2026 (year to 31-Dec-26) FY2027 (year to 31-Dec-27) FY2028 (year to 31-Dec-28) FY2029 (year to 31-Dec-29) FY2030 (year to 31-Dec-30)
Dividend per share 200 35 0 0 17

The dividend stays at 40% of underlying earnings per share, which I take as about 500 for FY2026 (the second set's cross-check of 517 understated tax; the model has 493 and consensus 507) and which falls to about 89 in FY2027 and about 43 in FY2030 with the higher tax charge above.

Part 2. Cash flow and balance sheet inputs, one column for each of the five fiscal years

Input FY2026 (year to 31-Dec-26) FY2027 (year to 31-Dec-27) FY2028 (year to 31-Dec-28) FY2029 (year to 31-Dec-29) FY2030 (year to 31-Dec-30)
Working capital (% of revenue) 1.8 0.9 0.5 0.5 0.7

The balance at the end of FY2026 is lowered from 2.5 to 1.8 and at the end of FY2027 from 1.0 to 0.9 (later years unchanged), which cuts the FY2026 absorption of cash from about 1,760 to about 1,310 and the FY2027 release from about 1,050 to about 655 while leaving the cumulative movement to the end of FY2028 unchanged, because DNB Carnegie puts the 2026 outflow at 1,130, the fourth quarter normally unwinds part of the summer build, and in the closest precedent, 2023, a release of 1,870 from receivables was offset by 1,440 absorbed elsewhere in working capital, a net release of 417 in a year when revenue fell 37%.

Working capital is the balance at the end of each fiscal year as a percentage of the last twelve months' revenue.

Effect, by my own arithmetic on the figures of model update 1 (the next model update gives the figures of record): earnings per share of about 493, 89, -67, -56 and 43 (from 493, 93, -61, -51 and 48); equity free cash flow of about 3,910, -1,020, -3,460, -3,200 and -1,235 (from 3,460, -568, -3,321, -3,123 and -1,155).

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for the memo

Research requests for the next set

Company: A.P. Moller - Maersk A/S (MAERSK.B-DK), a Danish integrated container logistics group with three segments: Ocean (container shipping, about two-thirds of revenue, operated with Hapag-Lloyd in the Gemini Cooperation), Logistics & Services (Forwarding, Solutions and Landside since April 2026) and Terminals (APM Terminals gateway ports).

Request: OWN-1

Question: For A.P. Moller - Maersk A/S (MAERSK.B-DK), what do brokers, company records and market data show about the valuation and share-price behaviour of the company and of listed container carriers through past cycles: (a) the price-to-book and EV/EBITDA multiples at which Maersk, Hapag-Lloyd, COSCO Shipping Holdings, Orient Overseas, Evergreen Marine and ZIM traded at the peak and at the trough of each container cycle since 2010 (2011-2012, 2015-2016, 2019-2020, 2022-2023 and 2026), how many months each share-price peak and trough came before or after the peak and trough of the SCFI and CCFI freight indices and of reported earnings, and how long each fall and each recovery lasted; (b) how closely Maersk's share price has moved with those carriers, with the freight indices and with European logistics stocks (DSV, Kuehne + Nagel, DHL), including any published correlations, betas or factor analyses (for example Morgan Stanley's 2026 ranking of Maersk on twelve-month idiosyncratic momentum) and the reasons given for the difference between Maersk's and Hapag-Lloyd's share-price performance over 2025-2026; (c) how Maersk's shares reacted in the days and months after each first annual guidance (February 2016, 2019, 2020, 2023, 2024, 2025 and 2026), after the narrowing of guidance in November 2023, after the suspension of the share buyback in February 2024 and after each in-year guidance raise in 2024, 2025 and 2026; and (d) which valuation methods and multiples each covering broker currently uses for its Maersk target price (price to book, sum of the parts with APM Terminals valued separately, EV/EBITDA, discounted cash flow), what trough valuation each cites, and which developments (scrapping, idle fleet, order cancellations, an Ocean impairment, a separation of APM Terminals, a new capital-allocation framework) each names as a condition for a more positive rating?

Request: OWN-2

Question: For A.P. Moller - Maersk A/S (MAERSK.B-DK), what is the latest evidence, from 1 November 2026 onward, on the conditions that will set container freight rates and carrier earnings in 2027: (a) the 2027 Asia-Europe and transpacific contract negotiations (the levels being offered and signed against 2026 contract levels and against spot rates, contract durations, the share of index-linked contracts and the treatment of bunker and other surcharges), as reported by Xeneta, Drewry, Linerlytica, Sea-Intelligence, freight forwarders such as DSV and Kuehne + Nagel, shippers and the trade press; (b) the third-quarter 2026 results and 2027 outlook statements of Hapag-Lloyd (reported 12 November 2026), COSCO Shipping Holdings, Orient Overseas, HMM, Ocean Network Express, ZIM, Evergreen Marine, Yang Ming and Wan Hai, including the Taiwanese carriers' monthly revenue for October and November 2026; (c) the weekly SCFI, CCFI and Drewry World Container Index by trade lane, Suez Canal containership transits as a share of 2023 levels, which carriers and alliances have and have not returned to the Red Sea route, and security, insurance and war-risk developments in the Red Sea and the Strait of Hormuz; (d) port congestion and the share of the fleet it absorbs, by region; and (e) signs of capacity discipline or its absence across the industry: idle fleet, blank sailings, slow steaming, scrapping, second-hand vessel prices, charter rates and durations, new orders, delivery slippage and any deferrals or cancellations of orders; and what brokers and industry analysts conclude from each of these about whether rates in the first half of 2027 will hold, fall gradually or fall sharply?

Request: OWN-3

Question: For A.P. Moller - Maersk A/S (MAERSK.B-DK), what do the company's reports and the covering brokers' models show for the items between operating profit and earnings per share and for cash commitments: (a) the tax expense, profit before tax and effective tax rate reported for each quarter and for the first nine months of 2026 and for each year from 2019 to 2025, split between tonnage tax, corporate income tax on the Terminals and Logistics & Services businesses, withholding tax, deferred tax and any Pillar Two top-up tax (the Annual Report 2025 gives a tax expense of USD 556m and an effective rate of 16.0%); (b) financial income, interest on borrowings, lease interest, foreign-exchange and other financial items for the same periods, the yield earned on cash and term deposits, and gross cash, term deposits, borrowings and lease liabilities at each period end; (c) the share of profit of joint ventures and associates and the dividends received from them, and the profit attributable to and dividends paid to non-controlling interests, including the effect of Hapag-Lloyd's 25% stake in APM Terminals Maasvlakte II (completed November 2026); (d) the forecasts of each covering broker (including Morgan Stanley, Bernstein, DNB Carnegie, Deutsche Bank, Jefferies, UBS, Goldman Sachs, Barclays, Nordea, Jyske Bank and Clarksons Securities) for tax, net financial items, minorities, share count, capital expenditure, lease repayments, working capital and net cash in 2027, 2028 and 2029, with the assumptions behind them; and (e) what Maersk has disclosed about the payment schedule for its 26 dual-fuel 18,600 TEU newbuilds (ordered in September 2026 for delivery in 2029-2030), the maturity profile of its USD 19.5bn of capital commitments at the third quarter of 2026, its leases committed but not yet commenced, and the expiry schedule of its 416 chartered vessels?
